The ablations study is helpful in demonstrating the importance of individual components. The results show promising improvements in both accuracy and efficiency.
    * **Detailed theoretical grounding:** Provides theoretical justification and proof for the proposed framework.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Generality/Adaptability:** The reliance on training questions to define graph partitions raises concerns about the framework's ability to generalize to new domains or datasets where such training data is not available or representative. The partitioning needs to be more robust and adaptable. This approach is probably effective if it is applied on data which has a high degree of structure and can be categorised into a limited set of semantic contexts. But this may not be applicable across domains.
    * **Experimental Rigor/Scope:** While the experimental results are encouraging, more details are needed about the experimental setup. For example:
        * The hyperparameters used for partitioning and the multi-agent system.
        * More details should be provided about the training questions in each experiment.
        * A closer analysis of the types of questions where SPLIT-RAG outperforms other baselines, and why, would strengthen the claims.
    * **Over-reliance on LLMs:** The framework still relies heavily on LLMs for various tasks (agent behavior, graph traversal, evidence textualization, answer generation).  This reliance could be a bottleneck if LLM performance degrades or if API costs become prohibitive. The prompt templates are shared but details on their impact on performance may be informative.
    * **Conflict Resolution limitations:** Conflict Resolution based on confidence score and resolving with logical tests may not be applicable across different types of RAG applications. Logical conflict check will require manually designing the tests and also needs clear ontology in knowledge graph.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence future research in RAG systems by highlighting the importance of efficient knowledge retrieval and demonstrating the benefits of question-driven graph partitioning and multi-agent approaches. However, the specific architecture of SPLIT-RAG might need to be adapted to be more general and less reliant on pre-defined training questions. The theoretical analysis of performance and model trade-offs may stimulate related work.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a practical and potentially effective RAG framework (SPLIT-RAG).  While the underlying techniques are not entirely novel, the specific combination and application to graph-based RAG, together with the comprehensive system design and its initial experimental validation, represent a valuable contribution.  The paper is somewhat limited by its reliance on training questions for graph partitioning, and the experimental results could benefit from more rigor and analysis. Still, the paper presents clear problem, well-defined solution and has strong potential in influencing future RAG system design. The framework, the performance gains, the use of agents for decomposition and the conflict resolution are all useful contributions. But in the final analysis the heavy dependence on training question structure limits broad applicability and the limitations of the conflict resolution are significant.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Activation-Guided Consensus Merging for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14009v1)
- **Authors**: Yuxuan Yao, Shuqi Liu, Zehua Liu, Qintong Li, Mingyang Liu, Xiongwei Han, Zhijiang Guo, Han Wu, Linqi Song
- **Abstract**: Recent research has increasingly focused on reconciling the reasoning capabilities of System 2 with the efficiency of System 1. While existing training-based and prompt-based approaches face significant challenges in terms of efficiency and stability, model merging emerges as a promising strategy to integrate the diverse capabilities of different Large Language Models (LLMs) into a unified model. However, conventional model merging methods often assume uniform importance across layers, overlooking the functional heterogeneity inherent in neural components. To address this limitation, we propose \textbf{A}ctivation-Guided \textbf{C}onsensus \textbf{M}erging (\textbf{ACM}), a plug-and-play merging framework that determines layer-specific merging coefficients based on mutual information between activations of pre-trained and fine-tuned models. ACM effectively preserves task-specific capabilities without requiring gradient computations or additional training. Extensive experiments on Long-to-Short (L2S) and general merging tasks demonstrate that ACM consistently outperforms all baseline methods. For instance, in the case of Qwen-7B models, TIES-Merging equipped with ACM achieves a \textbf{55.3\%} reduction in response length while simultaneously improving reasoning accuracy by \textbf{1.3} points. We submit the code with the paper for reproducibility, and it will be publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Activation-Guided Consensus Merging (ACM), a novel approach for merging large language models (LLMs). ACM addresses the limitations of conventional model merging methods that assume uniform importance across layers. Instead, ACM determines layer-specific merging coefficients based on mutual information between activations of pre-trained and fine-tuned models. This plug-and-play framework aims to preserve task-specific capabilities without requiring gradient computations or additional training. Experiments on Long-to-Short (L2S) reasoning and general merging tasks demonstrate that ACM consistently outperforms baseline methods, achieving significant reductions in response length while improving reasoning accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in the *activation-guided* aspect of the merging process using mutual information (MI). Existing techniques often use static weights or rely solely on the activations of the pre-trained model or require backpropagation.  ACM proposes a theoretically justified (through activation salience analysis) and computationally efficient method to determine layer-specific merging coefficients based on MI between both pre-trained and fine-tuned models. While the general idea of layer-specific weights isn't brand new, the method proposed to calculate them is.

**Significance:** The significance of the paper is multifaceted:

*   **Efficiency:** It provides a lightweight, plug-and-play merging framework, avoiding the computational overhead of training or backpropagation. This makes it a practical tool for integrating the strengths of different LLMs.
*   **Performance Improvement:**  The experimental results consistently demonstrate ACM's ability to outperform baseline merging methods, particularly in the L2S reasoning task. Achieving a balance between accuracy and reduced response length is a crucial challenge in LLMs, and ACM offers a viable solution.
*   **Insightful Analysis:** The paper's analysis of layer-specific weight coefficients provides valuable insights into the functional heterogeneity of neural components and the impact of knowledge distillation between models.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing model merging methods and the need for layer-specific weighting.
*   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a theoretical analysis of activation salience and motivates the use of mutual information as a measure of relevance and redundancy.
*   **Effective Method:**  ACM is a well-defined and computationally efficient merging framework that leverages mutual information to determine layer-specific merging coefficients.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper presents extensive experiments across multiple tasks, model sizes, and benchmarks, demonstrating the robustness and effectiveness of ACM.
*   **Thorough Ablation Studies:** Ablation studies are performed to investigate the impact of different hyperparameters.
*   **Comparison with RL-based methods:** ACM shows competitiveness with RL based models, with the advantage of being training-free.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Model Merging:**  The paper focuses on merging models within the *same* architecture. It does not address the challenges of merging models with different architectures (heterogeneous model merging), a potentially more complex and impactful problem.
*   **Reliance on a Calibration Dataset:** The method depends on a shared calibration dataset to obtain activations, which may introduce a dependency on the quality and representativeness of the data.
*   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** While it provides theoretical justification, the paper can be expanded by diving deeper into the activation-weight salience.
*  **Larger-scale Models Evaluation:** More evaluation on larger-scale models will be needed to solidify the findings.

**Potential Influence:**  ACM has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and effective method for model merging, particularly in scenarios where efficiency and balancing different model capabilities are crucial. It could also inspire further research into activation-guided techniques for model optimization and knowledge transfer.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to model merging, addressing a crucial challenge in LLMs – balancing accuracy and efficiency. The use of mutual information to guide layer-specific merging coefficients is theoretically justified and experimentally validated. While the paper has some limitations in terms of model merging scope and reliance on a calibration dataset, its strengths outweigh its weaknesses. Its practical value as a plug-and-play framework, combined with its performance improvements, makes it a solid contribution to the field.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### AUTOLAW: Enhancing Legal Compliance in Large Language Models via Case Law Generation and Jury-Inspired Deliberation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14015v1)
- **Authors**: Tai D. Nguyen, Long H. Pham, Jun Sun
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of domain-specific large language models (LLMs) in fields like law necessitates frameworks that account for nuanced regional legal distinctions, which are critical for ensuring compliance and trustworthiness. Existing legal evaluation benchmarks often lack adaptability and fail to address diverse local contexts, limiting their utility in dynamically evolving regulatory landscapes. To address these gaps, we propose AutoLaw, a novel violation detection framework that combines adversarial data generation with a jury-inspired deliberation process to enhance legal compliance of LLMs. Unlike static approaches, AutoLaw dynamically synthesizes case law to reflect local regulations and employs a pool of LLM-based "jurors" to simulate judicial decision-making. Jurors are ranked and selected based on synthesized legal expertise, enabling a deliberation process that minimizes bias and improves detection accuracy. Evaluations across three benchmarks: Law-SG, Case-SG (legality), and Unfair-TOS (policy), demonstrate AutoLaw's effectiveness: adversarial data generation improves LLM discrimination, while the jury-based voting strategy significantly boosts violation detection rates. Our results highlight the framework's ability to adaptively probe legal misalignments and deliver reliable, context-aware judgments, offering a scalable solution for evaluating and enhancing LLMs in legally sensitive applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "AUTOLAW: Enhancing Legal Compliance in Large Language Models via Case Law Generation and Jury-Inspired Deliberation" addresses the critical challenge of ensuring that Large Language Models (LLMs) comply with nuanced, region-specific legal regulations. The authors argue that existing legal benchmarks lack the adaptability and contextual awareness needed to effectively evaluate LLMs in dynamically evolving regulatory landscapes. To address this, they propose AUTOLAW, a novel framework that combines adversarial data generation with a jury-inspired deliberation process. AUTOLAW dynamically synthesizes case law reflecting local regulations and uses a pool of LLM-based "jurors" to simulate judicial decision-making. Jurors are ranked based on their legal expertise, and the framework aims to minimize bias while improving detection accuracy. Experiments on three benchmarks (Law-SG, Case-SG, and Unfair-TOS) demonstrate the effectiveness of AUTOLAW in improving LLM discrimination and violation detection rates.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a genuinely novel approach to evaluating and improving the legal compliance of LLMs. The combination of adversarial data generation with a jury-inspired deliberation mechanism is innovative and moves beyond static benchmarks. The dynamic creation of case law tailored to specific regulations is also a notable contribution. While elements of adversarial testing and ensemble methods exist in the literature, their integration within a legal compliance framework, particularly with the jury deliberation analogy, represents a significant step forward.

*   **Significance:** The problem addressed by the paper is of increasing importance as LLMs are deployed in legal and other regulated domains. The potential for LLMs to violate local regulations or provide inaccurate legal advice is a serious concern. AUTOLAW offers a scalable and context-aware solution for evaluating and enhancing LLMs in these legally sensitive applications. The reported results on the three benchmarks are compelling, demonstrating the effectiveness of AUTOLAW in improving both LLM discrimination and violation detection rates.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing legal evaluation benchmarks and motivates the need for a more adaptive and context-aware approach.
    *   **Methodology:** The AUTOLAW framework is well-designed and integrates several key components, including adversarial data generation, juror ranking, and jury deliberation.
    *   **Experimental Results:** The experimental results provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of AUTOLAW in improving LLM legal compliance across different benchmarks.
    *   **Code and Data Availability:** The authors pledge to make their source code and datasets publicly available, enhancing reproducibility and facilitating future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** While the framework is well-designed, the multiple stages and components of AUTOLAW add complexity that might hinder adoption or scaling. It is not clear that all the elements are required together.
    *   **Scope:** The experiments focus primarily on Singaporean regulations. While this provides a specific case study, it would be beneficial to demonstrate the generalizability of AUTOLAW to other legal jurisdictions.
    *   **Evaluation of Jurors:** While jurors are ranked based on their legal expertise, the evaluation is still based on LLM evaluation of other LLMs. The reliance on GPT-4.1 to verify Jurors provides a single point of failure, as the verification is not based on human assessment, which would enhance trustworthiness.
    *   **Scalability Evaluation:** A more detailed analysis of computational efficiency and how the framework scales with larger LLMs and larger case law databases would be welcome.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and legally compliant LLMs. The AUTOLAW framework could be adopted by researchers and practitioners in the field to evaluate and improve the legal compliance of LLMs in various applications. Furthermore, the paper highlights the importance of context-aware evaluation and dynamic benchmark generation, which could inspire future research directions.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The strength of this research lies in the creation of a multi-faceted system that aims to both detect and alleviate legal reasoning shortcomings in LLMs. However, as a completely LLM-driven system, it inherit the problems of its components (hallucination, misrepresentation, context-failure) and compounds the risks.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of legal compliance in LLMs. The AUTOLAW framework is well-designed, and the experimental results are compelling. While there are some limitations, such as the scope of the experiments and the reliance on Singaporean regulations, the paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and legally compliant LLMs. The score of 7 reflects the significant novelty and potential impact of the paper while acknowledging some limitations in scope and evaluation. A higher score would require a more comprehensive evaluation across multiple jurisdictions and a more detailed analysis of computational efficiency and scalability.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Adaptive Cyclic Diffusion for Inference Scaling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14036v1)
- **Authors**: Gyubin Lee, Truong Nhat Nguyen Bao, Jaesik Yoon, Dongwoo Lee, Minsu Kim, Yoshua Bengio, Sungjin Ahn
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have demonstrated strong generative capabilities across domains ranging from image synthesis to complex reasoning tasks. However, most inference-time scaling methods rely on fixed denoising schedules, limiting their ability to allocate computation based on instance difficulty or task-specific demands adaptively. We introduce the challenge of adaptive inference-time scaling-dynamically adjusting computational effort during inference-and propose Adaptive Bi-directional Cyclic Diffusion (ABCD), a flexible, search-based inference framework. ABCD refines outputs through bi-directional diffusion cycles while adaptively controlling exploration depth and termination. It comprises three components: Cyclic Diffusion Search, Automatic Exploration-Exploitation Balancing, and Adaptive Thinking Time. Experiments show that ABCD improves performance across diverse tasks while maintaining computational efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Adaptive Bi-directional Cyclic Diffusion (ABCD), a new framework for inference with diffusion models. ABCD addresses the problem of adaptive inference-time scaling, allowing for dynamic adjustment of computational effort based on instance difficulty and task-specific demands. The framework has three core components: Cyclic Diffusion Search (CDS), which enables iterative refinement through bi-directional diffusion cycles; Automatic Exploration-Exploitation Balancing (AEEB), which adaptively controls the depth of exploration by distributing particles across different re-noising levels; and Adaptive Thinking Time (ATT), which provides a principled stopping criterion based on monitoring solution quality. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of ABCD on several tasks, including control planning, maze solving, Sudoku, and molecule generation, showcasing improvements in flexibility, accuracy, and computational efficiency compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of adaptive, cyclic inference in diffusion models is a significant step forward. While techniques like beam search and importance sampling exist, ABCD combines bi-directional refinement, dynamic exploration/exploitation balancing, and an adaptive stopping criterion in a novel way. The separation of exploration and exploitation through varied noise levels and the implicit adaptation through particle selection contribute to the method's strength.

*   **Significance:** Adaptive inference-time scaling is crucial for making diffusion models more practical for real-world applications where computational resources are limited or variable. By allowing the model to focus computation on difficult instances, ABCD improves efficiency and accuracy. The experiments across diverse tasks (planning, reasoning, and generation) highlight the broad applicability of the framework.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Adaptive Framework:** The strength lies in the framework's ability to adapt to the specific needs of each input instance, optimizing computational allocation and improving performance on challenging tasks. The three components (CDS, AEEB, ATT) work synergistically.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The empirical results demonstrate significant improvements over baselines across a variety of challenging tasks, including those involving structured reasoning and molecular generation.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper provides a clear and well-organized description of the ABCD framework, making it easy to understand and implement. The components are well-explained, and the implementation details are sufficient.
    *   **Strong Baselines:** The paper compares ABCD against a variety of strong representative baselines, including Beam Search, SMC Diffusion, and Search over Paths. This ensures that the improvements shown by ABCD are significant and not simply due to a poorly chosen baseline.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Scalability:** The authors acknowledge that the scalability of ABCD to very high-dimensional output spaces (e.g., high-resolution images, videos) remains unexplored. This is a valid concern, as the particle-based approach may become computationally expensive as the dimensionality of the output space increases. Structural priors would improve scalability.
    *   **Theoretical Analysis:** The paper lacks a deeper theoretical analysis of ABCD's properties. While the empirical results are strong, a theoretical understanding of the convergence properties and optimality of the approach would be valuable. The relationship to frameworks like Bayesian optimization or approximate inference could be further elaborated.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** While the paper demonstrates strong overall performance, the ablation studies could be more comprehensive. Specifically, analyzing the impact of each component (CDS, AEEB, and ATT) individually would provide more insight into their relative contributions.

*   **Potential Impact:** The ABCD framework has the potential to significantly impact the use of diffusion models in various fields, particularly in applications where computational resources are limited or variable. The adaptive inference-time scaling capabilities of ABCD make it well-suited for real-world scenarios. Furthermore, the novel components introduced in ABCD could inspire new research directions in diffusion model inference.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of diffusion models by introducing a novel and effective framework for adaptive inference-time scaling. While there are some weaknesses regarding scalability and theoretical analysis, the strengths of the paper—including the adaptive framework, strong empirical validation, clear methodology, and comparison against relevant baselines—outweigh these concerns.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** A score of 8 reflects the paper's significant contribution and potential impact. The novelty lies in the integration of bi-directional refinement, dynamic exploration/exploitation, and adaptive stopping. The significance stems from enabling efficient and accurate inference in diffusion models across diverse tasks. While lacking in-depth theoretical analysis and extensive scalability demonstrations, the empirical results and potential for future research firmly establish this as a significant advancement in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### ProMind-LLM: Proactive Mental Health Care via Causal Reasoning with Sensor Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14038v1)
- **Authors**: Xinzhe Zheng, Sijie Ji, Jiawei Sun, Renqi Chen, Wei Gao, Mani Srivastava
- **Abstract**: Mental health risk is a critical global public health challenge, necessitating innovative and reliable assessment methods. With the development of large language models (LLMs), they stand out to be a promising tool for explainable mental health care applications. Nevertheless, existing approaches predominantly rely on subjective textual mental records, which can be distorted by inherent mental uncertainties, leading to inconsistent and unreliable predictions. To address these limitations, this paper introduces ProMind-LLM. We investigate an innovative approach integrating objective behavior data as complementary information alongside subjective mental records for robust mental health risk assessment. Specifically, ProMind-LLM incorporates a comprehensive pipeline that includes domain-specific pretraining to tailor the LLM for mental health contexts, a self-refine mechanism to optimize the processing of numerical behavioral data, and causal chain-of-thought reasoning to enhance the reliability and interpretability of its predictions. Evaluations of two real-world datasets, PMData and Globem, demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed methods, achieving substantial improvements over general LLMs. We anticipate that ProMind-LLM will pave the way for more dependable, interpretable, and scalable mental health case solutions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ProMind-LLM, a novel approach for proactive mental health care using large language models (LLMs). It aims to address the limitations of existing LLM-based mental health solutions that primarily rely on subjective textual data (e.g., self-reported records) by integrating objective behavioral data from sensors. ProMind-LLM incorporates a domain-specific pretraining phase to tailor the LLM for mental health contexts, a self-refinement mechanism to optimize the processing of numerical behavioral data, and causal chain-of-thought reasoning to enhance the reliability and interpretability of its predictions. The authors evaluate ProMind-LLM on two real-world datasets and demonstrate improved performance compared to general LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The primary novelty lies in the integration of objective behavioral data with subjective mental records in an LLM-based framework for mental health risk assessment. While prior work has explored LLMs for mental health using text or multimodal data (speech, video), the combination with objective, longitudinally tracked sensor data and a pipeline optimized for this integration is a notable contribution. The self-refinement mechanism for processing numerical behavioral data and the use of counterfactual reasoning within the CoT framework are also incremental but potentially valuable innovations.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance of this work is in improving the reliability and interpretability of mental health risk assessment using LLMs. By incorporating objective data, the system is less susceptible to biases and inaccuracies in self-reported information. This can lead to more accurate and timely interventions, especially for individuals who may be reluctant to disclose their mental health struggles. Furthermore, the explanation via causal reasoning allows clinicians to understand what evidence supports the prediction.  The claim of "proactive" mental health care is strong, as the current work focuses on *assessment* rather than *intervention*, but the underlying premise is valid.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing LLM-based mental health approaches and highlights the need for objective data.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The ProMind-LLM pipeline is well-structured, with detailed descriptions of each component (domain-specific training, self-refinement, causal CoT).
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct experiments on two real-world datasets and compare ProMind-LLM against several baseline models, including commercial LLMs and open-source alternatives. The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   **Addresses Uncertainty:** The approach explicitly addresses the uncertainty inherent in mental health data using counterfactual learning.
    *   **Emphasis on Interpretability:** The inclusion of Causal CoT reasoning is significant and shows attention to transparency.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability Concerns:**  It's unclear how the domain pretraining will be specific and how this might be adjusted for specific cohorts (e.g., youth vs. aging populations). There is a stated limitation on environments with diverse populations.
    *   **Dataset Dependency:** The performance of ProMind-LLM is likely to be dependent on the quality and characteristics of the behavioral and mental record data. The datasets used may not be representative of all populations or clinical settings.
    *   **Real-World Validation Needed:** While the experimental results are promising, the ultimate test of ProMind-LLM will be its effectiveness in real-world clinical settings. Deployment in diverse settings, where social determinants of health, access to care, and mental health literacy may vary widely, requires significant real-world validation.
    *   **Scalability:** The models are only evaluated on a small scale LLMs and are compared against large scale open source and commerical LLMs. How the ProMind LLM framework will scale in the future is unaddressed.

*   **Impact:** The work has the potential to influence the development of more reliable and explainable AI-powered mental health tools.  However, significant real-world validation is needed before it can be widely adopted in clinical practice.  It also provides a new perspective on designing LLMs to incorporate both subjective and objective multi-modal data.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of **7** is justified.  The work presents a genuinely valuable contribution by integrating sensor data and causal reasoning into a mental health LLM, showing improved performance compared to baselines. The methodology is well-defined and empirically evaluated. However, there are also valid concerns surrounding dataset dependency, limited scope, and generalizability to diverse real-world scenarios that needs more future work.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Adversarially Pretrained Transformers may be Universally Robust In-Context Learners
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14042v1)
- **Authors**: Soichiro Kumano, Hiroshi Kera, Toshihiko Yamasaki
- **Abstract**: Adversarial training is one of the most effective adversarial defenses, but it incurs a high computational cost. In this study, we show that transformers adversarially pretrained on diverse tasks can serve as robust foundation models and eliminate the need for adversarial training in downstream tasks. Specifically, we theoretically demonstrate that through in-context learning, a single adversarially pretrained transformer can robustly generalize to multiple unseen tasks without any additional training, i.e., without any parameter updates. This robustness stems from the model's focus on robust features and its resistance to attacks that exploit non-predictive features. Besides these positive findings, we also identify several limitations. Under certain conditions (though unrealistic), no universally robust single-layer transformers exist. Moreover, robust transformers exhibit an accuracy--robustness trade-off and require a large number of in-context demonstrations. The code is available at https://github.com/s-kumano/universally-robust-in-context-learner.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether Adversarially Pretrained Transformers (APTs) can achieve universal robustness across diverse tasks through in-context learning, eliminating the need for task-specific adversarial training. The authors theoretically demonstrate that a single APT can generalize robustly to multiple unseen tasks without parameter updates, attributing this to the model's focus on robust features and resistance to attacks exploiting non-predictive ones.  The paper also identifies limitations: under specific (unrealistic) conditions, universally robust single-layer transformers don't exist, and APTs exhibit an accuracy-robustness trade-off while requiring larger in-context demonstration sets.  The analysis is performed on single-layer transformers with linear self-attention using a robust and non-robust feature framework.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel theoretical analysis of adversarial robustness in transformers specifically within the in-context learning paradigm.  While adversarial training and robustness are established areas, linking them to in-context learning and analyzing universal robustness across diverse tasks is a fresh perspective. The analysis of how APTs prioritize robust features over non-robust ones, and the quantification of the impact of redundant dimensions, are significant contributions. The insights about the failure cases and limitations, while negative, also add value by highlighting the boundaries of the proposed approach.

*   **Significance:** If validated and extended to more complex models, the findings could have a high impact. The prospect of using a single adversarially pre-trained model for robust adaptation across various tasks is promising because it could significantly reduce the computational cost associated with task-specific adversarial training. The theoretical framework contributes to a deeper understanding of how adversarial training shapes the internal representations of transformers and how these representations translate to robustness across different tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Solid Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides rigorous mathematical analysis with clear assumptions and well-defined theorems.
    *   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The problem of achieving universal robustness is precisely defined, making the analysis focused and interpretable.
    *   **Identified Limitations:** The honesty in pointing out the limitations of the approach, such as the specific conditions under which universally robust transformers don't exist and the accuracy-robustness trade-off, strengthens the paper.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  Experiments validate the theoretical predictions, supporting the main claims regarding parameter behavior, robustness, and accuracy tradeoffs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplified Model:** The analysis is limited to single-layer transformers with linear self-attention. This makes it difficult to directly generalize the results to larger, more complex transformer architectures used in practice. This simplification is necessary for a theoretical treatment but weakens the real-world applicability of the results.
    *   **Unrealistic Assumptions:**  The specific conditions under which universally robust single-layer transformers don't exist are described as "unrealistic."  While this is acknowledged, it indicates the limited scope of the negative results. More broadly, other simplifications regarding data distribution need to be carefully considered when extrapolating these findings.
    *   **Accuracy-Robustness Tradeoff:** Demonstrating this tradeoff, although a known issue, reinforces a current obstacle. How the theory could guide mitigating the tradeoff would have increased the significance.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in adversarial robustness and in-context learning. It opens doors for exploring more sophisticated adversarial training techniques that lead to more robust and generalizable representations. It provides a foundation for analyzing the robustness of other in-context learning algorithms and exploring the interplay between robustness and generalization in large language models.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel theoretical analysis with significant insights into adversarial robustness and in-context learning. The findings are well-supported by the mathematical framework and empirical results. However, the limitations of the simplified model and strong assumptions restrict the immediate real-world applicability of the results. Nevertheless, the work's solid theoretical foundation and identification of key factors influencing robustness and generalization make it a valuable contribution, warranting a score of 7.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Unaligned to Aligned: Scaling Multilingual LLMs with Multi-Way Parallel Corpora
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14045v1)
- **Authors**: Yingli Shen, Wen Lai, Shuo Wang, Kangyang Luo, Alexander Fraser, Maosong Sun
- **Abstract**: Continued pretraining and instruction tuning on large-scale multilingual data have proven to be effective in scaling large language models (LLMs) to low-resource languages. However, the unaligned nature of such data limits its ability to effectively capture cross-lingual semantics. In contrast, multi-way parallel data, where identical content is aligned across multiple languages, provides stronger cross-lingual consistency and offers greater potential for improving multilingual performance. In this paper, we introduce a large-scale, high-quality multi-way parallel corpus, TED2025, based on TED Talks. The corpus spans 113 languages, with up to 50 languages aligned in parallel, ensuring extensive multilingual coverage. Using this dataset, we investigate best practices for leveraging multi-way parallel data to enhance LLMs, including strategies for continued pretraining, instruction tuning, and the analysis of key influencing factors. Experiments on six multilingual benchmarks show that models trained on multiway parallel data consistently outperform those trained on unaligned multilingual data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TED2025, a large-scale, high-quality multi-way parallel corpus derived from recent TED Talk transcripts.  The corpus spans 113 languages with up to 50-way parallelism. The authors then investigate how to effectively leverage multi-way parallel data to enhance multilingual LLMs through continued pretraining and instruction tuning. The experiments explore different aspects such as comparing multi-way and unaligned pretraining, the impact of different parallelism degrees, and the effectiveness of various instruction tuning objectives. The results show that fine-tuning on multi-way parallel data consistently outperforms training on unaligned data and that tailored instruction tuning objectives improve LLM performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the introduction of the TED2025 dataset and its use for scaling multilingual LLMs. While multi-way parallel corpora are not new, TED2025's scale (number of languages and domains), and its recent updates are an improvement over existing TED-based resources. Applying such data to LLM scaling *is* a valuable contribution.  The exploration of fine-tuning strategies (parallelism degree, inclusion of English, instruction tuning objectives) provide useful practical insights. The method of using human-provided timestamps for sentence alignment to generate cleaner and more reliable alignment is a good engineering decision.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance hinges on demonstrating the advantages of multi-way parallel data over unaligned data for multilingual LLM development. The empirical results presented across several benchmarks provide solid evidence for these advantages. The analysis of impact factors offers valuable guidance for leveraging multi-way parallel data in future LLM research. The paper addresses a relevant problem (improving LLM performance in low-resource languages) and proposes a potentially impactful solution. The zero-shot transfer to unseen languages further supports the generalizability of the proposed techniques.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper introduces a valuable new resource (TED2025) that is publicly available, fostering further research.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive and cover multiple aspects of multi-way parallel data usage.
    *   The analysis of impact factors provides insightful recommendations for future work.
    *   The results are clearly presented with comparisons against a strong baseline (unaligned pretraining).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper relies on LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) for fine-tuning to alleviate the computational requirements. While this is practical, results using full fine-tuning would be a more convincing demonstration of the data's power.
    *   The total size of TED2025 remains modest compared to very large, unaligned corpora. This limitation is acknowledged, but it's important to keep in mind.
    *   The paper could benefit from more qualitative examples illustrating the specific improvements achieved by multi-way parallel data (e.g., better translations, reduced hallucinations).

*   **Potential Impact:** The TED2025 dataset and the findings presented in the paper have the potential to influence future research on multilingual LLMs. It can serve as a benchmark for evaluating new models and techniques. The insights into the impact of parallelism degree and instruction tuning objectives can inform the design of more effective multilingual LLM training strategies.

**Justification:**

While the core idea of using parallel data for multilingual learning is not entirely new, this paper builds upon existing work by introducing a substantial, recently updated dataset with extensive language coverage and exploring systematic fine-tuning techniques for large language models. The clear demonstration of benefits over unaligned approaches and in the context of contemporary LLMs gives the work importance. Its focus on practical considerations such as parallel degree and instruction tuning objectives, makes it relevant for practitioners, improving the generalizability of language modeling and domain adaption.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Improved Methods for Model Pruning and Knowledge Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14052v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Jiang, Anying Fu, Youling Zhang
- **Abstract**: Model pruning is a performance optimization technique for large language models like R1 or o3-mini. However, existing pruning methods often lead to significant performance degradation or require extensive retraining and fine-tuning. This technique aims to identify and remove neurons, connections unlikely leading to the contribution during the human-computer interaction phase. Our goal is to obtain a much smaller and faster knowledge distilled model that can quickly generate content almost as good as those of the unpruned ones. We propose MAMA Pruning, short for Movement and Magnitude Analysis, an improved pruning method that effectively reduces model size and computational complexity while maintaining performance comparable to the original unpruned model even at extreme pruned levels. The improved method is based on weights, bias fixed in the pre-training phase and GRPO rewards verified during the post-training phase as our novel pruning indicators. Preliminary experimental results show that our method outperforms and be comparable to state-of-the-art methods across various pruning levels and different downstream computational linguistics tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MAMA Pruning (Movement and Magnitude Analysis), a novel method for pruning large language models.  MAMA Pruning aims to reduce model size and computational complexity while maintaining performance by identifying and removing less significant neurons based on both their magnitude and dynamic behavior during pre- and post-training.  The method redistributes the weights of pruned neurons to more important connections. The paper presents preliminary experimental results comparing MAMA Pruning to other pruning methods, demonstrating promising results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies in the combination of magnitude and movement (dynamic behavior) analysis of weights during pre- and post-training for pruning. While magnitude-based pruning and other techniques exist (SparseGPT, WANDA), the authors argue that MAMA Pruning's three-step process provides a more sophisticated approach to identifying and preserving important neural connections.  The weight redistribution phase is also a common strategy, but its integration within the MAMA Pruning framework is a distinguishing factor.

**Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its potential to improve the efficiency of large language models, a crucial concern due to the computational cost of deploying such models. The preliminary experimental results showing comparable or superior performance compared to existing methods suggest that MAMA Pruning could be a valuable tool for model optimization. However, a more comprehensive evaluation, including a broader range of tasks and model architectures, is needed to solidify this claim.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the problem of computational costs associated with large language models and the need for efficient pruning techniques.
*   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The MAMA Pruning algorithm is well-described, with clear explanations of the three core steps.
*   **Promising Preliminary Results:** The experimental results, while preliminary, demonstrate that MAMA Pruning can achieve competitive performance.
*   **Comparison to Existing Methods:** The paper contrasts MAMA Pruning with other existing pruning methods, highlighting its advantages.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Experimental Evaluation:** The experimental section is relatively limited.  The experiments primarily focus on perplexity as an evaluation metric. Further evaluation is needed on downstream tasks. A more diverse set of LLMs would strengthen the conclusions.
*   **Lack of Ablation Studies:**  It's unclear which component of the MAMA Pruning method (magnitude analysis, movement analysis, weight redistribution) contributes the most to performance. Ablation studies would strengthen the paper.
*   **Unclear Specifics**: The exact method for *redistributing weights* to related neurons lacks details and could be more thoroughly described.
*   **English Quality:** The language in the paper could be improved. Several sentences are not grammatically correct, and word choice is sometimes awkward.

**Potential Influence:**  If MAMA Pruning proves to be robust and effective across a wider range of models and tasks, it could potentially influence the development of more efficient and deployable large language models. Its emphasis on dynamic behavior analysis could also inspire new pruning techniques that go beyond simple magnitude-based approaches.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to model pruning, but the limited experimental validation and lack of ablation studies temper its significance. While the preliminary results are promising, more rigorous evaluation is needed to confirm the effectiveness and robustness of MAMA Pruning. The writing also needs improvement. Therefore, a score of 5 is warranted.

Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### Field Matters: A lightweight LLM-enhanced Method for CTR Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14057v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Cui, Feng Liu, Jiawei Chen, Xingyu Lou, Changwang Zhang, Jun Wang, Yuegang Sun, Xiaohu Yang, Can Wang
- **Abstract**: Click-through rate (CTR) prediction is a fundamental task in modern recommender systems. In recent years, the integration of large language models (LLMs) has been shown to effectively enhance the performance of traditional CTR methods. However, existing LLM-enhanced methods often require extensive processing of detailed textual descriptions for large-scale instances or user/item entities, leading to substantial computational overhead. To address this challenge, this work introduces LLaCTR, a novel and lightweight LLM-enhanced CTR method that employs a field-level enhancement paradigm. Specifically, LLaCTR first utilizes LLMs to distill crucial and lightweight semantic knowledge from small-scale feature fields through self-supervised field-feature fine-tuning. Subsequently, it leverages this field-level semantic knowledge to enhance both feature representation and feature interactions. In our experiments, we integrate LLaCTR with six representative CTR models across four datasets, demonstrating its superior performance in terms of both effectiveness and efficiency compared to existing LLM-enhanced methods. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/LLaCTR-EC46.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Field Matters: A lightweight LLM-enhanced Method for CTR Prediction."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LLaCTR, a novel and lightweight LLM-enhanced method for click-through rate (CTR) prediction. Unlike existing LLM-enhanced CTR methods that often require extensive processing of detailed textual descriptions for large-scale instances or user/item entities, LLaCTR operates at the *field level*. It first utilizes LLMs to distill crucial and lightweight semantic knowledge from small-scale feature fields through self-supervised field-feature fine-tuning. Subsequently, it leverages this field-level semantic knowledge to enhance both feature representation and feature interactions in downstream CTR models.  The paper demonstrates, through experiments on four datasets and integration with six representative CTR models, that LLaCTR achieves superior performance (both in effectiveness and efficiency) compared to existing LLM-enhanced methods. The authors provide code for reproducibility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the *field-level* approach to LLM enhancement. This contrasts with the dominant paradigm of instance-level or user/item-level processing in existing LLM-enhanced CTR models. This is a valuable shift, as it directly addresses the computational bottleneck associated with applying LLMs to large-scale recommender systems. The self-supervised field-feature fine-tuning (SSFT) is also a reasonably novel and well-motivated method to inject domain-specific knowledge into the LLM. The techniques used for downstream enhancements (FRE and FIE) are more standard applications of embeddings, but their integration in the field-level framework is the novel aspect.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the practical implications of a *lightweight* and efficient LLM enhancement strategy. CTR prediction is a core task in modern recommender systems, and the ability to effectively leverage LLMs without incurring prohibitive computational costs is highly desirable. The experimental results, showing performance improvements over several competitive baselines and superior efficiency, support the claim of practical significance. The code release is also a positive factor, facilitating adoption and further research. The ablation studies contribute to understanding the relative importance of the proposed modules.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined problem and motivation.
    *   Clear explanation of the proposed method.
    *   Rigorous experimental evaluation on multiple datasets and with several baselines.
    *   Demonstrated effectiveness and efficiency compared to existing methods.
    *   Code release for reproducibility.
    *   Ablation Study to test the proposed enhancements.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The individual components of FRE and FIE, while effective within the LLaCTR framework, are not particularly groundbreaking on their own. It's more about the integration.
    *   While the paper shows *substantial* speedups, some readers might still find the runtime overhead of even the lightweight LLM component to be a barrier for real-time, extremely high-throughput online serving environments. More aggressive methods for speeding up LLM inference are still an active area of research and could further improve LLaCTR.
    *   Some aspects of the hyperparameter tuning seem ad-hoc. While the grid search approach is reasonable, a deeper discussion of why certain ranges were chosen might be helpful.
    *   The study on "Negative Societal Impacts" seems dismissive. While it is true that the risk is not particularly high in the field, the study should take into consideration malicious/unethical impacts related to recommendation systems in general (filter bubbles, polarization).

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The field-level approach could influence future research in LLM-enhanced recommender systems, shifting the focus towards more efficient and scalable solutions.
    *   LLaCTR provides a practical framework for integrating LLMs into CTR prediction, which could be adopted by industry practitioners.
    *   The SSFT task could inspire new self-supervised learning strategies for injecting domain-specific knowledge into LLMs for other recommendation tasks.

**Score: 7.8**

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper presents a *novel and significant* contribution to the field of LLM-enhanced CTR prediction. The field-level approach is a clever and well-motivated solution to the computational challenges associated with existing methods. The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness and efficiency of LLaCTR, making it a practically valuable technique. While the individual components (FRE, FIE) are not exceptionally novel, their integration into a cohesive and efficient framework is a key strength. The paper is well-written, and the code release enhances its reproducibility and potential impact. The score is justified by a solid contribution with significant practical value that has a good chance to shift the direction of the field.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Process vs. Outcome Reward: Which is Better for Agentic RAG Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14069v1)
- **Authors**: Wenlin Zhang, Xiangyang Li, Kuicai Dong, Yichao Wang, Pengyue Jia, Xiaopeng Li, Yingyi Zhang, Derong Xu, Zhaocheng Du, Huifeng Guo, Ruiming Tang, Xiangyu Zhao
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) enhances the text generation capabilities of large language models (LLMs) by integrating external knowledge and up-to-date information. However, traditional RAG systems are limited by static workflows and lack the adaptability required for multistep reasoning and complex task management. To address these limitations, agentic RAG systems (e.g., DeepResearch) have been proposed, enabling dynamic retrieval strategies, iterative context refinement, and adaptive workflows for handling complex search queries beyond the capabilities of conventional RAG. Recent advances, such as Search-R1, have demonstrated promising gains using outcome-based reinforcement learning, where the correctness of the final answer serves as the reward signal. Nevertheless, such outcome-supervised agentic RAG methods face challenges including low exploration efficiency, gradient conflict, and sparse reward signals. To overcome these challenges, we propose to utilize fine-grained, process-level rewards to improve training stability, reduce computational costs, and enhance efficiency. Specifically, we introduce a novel method ReasonRAG that automatically constructs RAG-ProGuide, a high-quality dataset providing process-level rewards for (i) query generation, (ii) evidence extraction, and (iii) answer generation, thereby enhancing model inherent capabilities via process-supervised reinforcement learning. With the process-level policy optimization, the proposed framework empowers LLMs to autonomously invoke search, generate queries, extract relevant evidence, and produce final answers. Compared to existing approaches such as Search-R1 and traditional RAG systems, ReasonRAG, leveraging RAG-ProGuide, achieves superior performance on five benchmark datasets using only 5k training instances, significantly fewer than the 90k training instances required by Search-R1.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ReasonRAG, a novel approach to agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) that utilizes process-supervised reinforcement learning.  Unlike traditional RAG systems and recent outcome-supervised agentic RAG methods (like Search-R1) that rely solely on the correctness of the final answer as a reward signal, ReasonRAG uses fine-grained, process-level rewards to improve training stability, reduce computational costs, and enhance efficiency. It introduces RAG-ProGuide, a dataset automatically constructed using Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) and a novel Shortest Path Reward Estimation (SPRE) algorithm, providing high-quality process-level rewards for query generation, evidence extraction, and answer generation.  Experiments on five benchmark datasets demonstrate that ReasonRAG achieves superior performance compared to existing RAG systems and Search-R1, even with significantly fewer training instances.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the application of process-supervised RL to agentic RAG. While both agentic RAG and RL have been explored separately, the specific combination with fine-grained process rewards generated automatically using MCTS and SPRE presents a clear contribution. The SPRE algorithm itself, designed to incentivize efficient reasoning paths, is also a novel element. Furthermore, the RAG-ProGuide dataset, automatically generating training data for process-supervised RAG, lowers the barrier to entry for leveraging this paradigm.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses several crucial limitations of outcome-supervised RL in agentic RAG: low exploration efficiency, gradient conflict, and sparse reward signals. By providing intermediate rewards, the model receives more frequent feedback, leading to faster and more stable learning. This is particularly significant given the computational costs associated with training large language models. The empirical results support these claims, showing that ReasonRAG outperforms Search-R1 with a significantly smaller training dataset, highlighting a crucial advance in training efficiency. The performance on benchmarks, including out-of-domain datasets, demonstrates the robustness of the approach.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing RAG systems and motivates the need for process-supervised RL.
    *   **Novel Methodology:** The combination of MCTS, SPRE, and DPO for process-level reward generation and policy optimization is well-designed and innovative.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments are thorough and comprehensive, demonstrating the effectiveness of ReasonRAG across multiple datasets.
    *   **Data Efficiency:** The most significant strength is the demonstrated data efficiency. ReasonRAG achieving superior performance with significantly fewer training examples compared to Search-R1 has important practical implications.
    *   **Well-written and Organised:** The paper is easy to understand, and presents a clear pipeline.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on LLM Judge:** While the paper details the automated reward generation, the process depends on an LLM judge for assessing intermediate steps. The quality of this LLM judgment influences the quality of the training data. While the authors use GPT-4o, further analysis of the potential biases or limitations of the LLM judge would strengthen the study.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Alternative Reward Functions:** The paper focuses on the proposed SPRE algorithm. Exploring other process-level reward functions and comparing their effectiveness would provide a more comprehensive understanding of the reward design space. It may be valuable to explore more informative signals for negative rewards.
    *   **Scalability Concerns:** Although data efficiency is high, the dependence on MCTS might introduce scalability challenges for more complex reasoning tasks requiring deeper search trees.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** While the paper presents ablation studies, the inclusion of further investigations specifically assessing the impact of SPRE and MCTS configurations would strengthen the results.
*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence future research in several ways. It provides a strong case for process-supervised RL in agentic RAG, encouraging researchers to explore alternative reward functions and training strategies. The RAG-ProGuide dataset can serve as a valuable resource for training and evaluating process-level RL methods. This can facilitate further research in agentic RAG.
*   **Justification:** The paper makes a significant contribution by addressing practical challenges in training agentic RAG systems. The novel approach, strong empirical results, and data efficiency warrant a high score. However, the reliance on an LLM judge and limited exploration of reward functions slightly detract from the overall score.

**Score: 8**

The score reflects the paper's significant contribution to the field of agentic RAG. The novel methodology and strong empirical results, particularly the data efficiency, are highly valuable. While the reliance on the LLM judge and other limitations need further consideration, the paper presents a compelling case for process-supervised RL and offers a promising direction for future research.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Enhancing LLMs via High-Knowledge Data Selection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14070v1)
- **Authors**: Feiyu Duan, Xuemiao Zhang, Sirui Wang, Haoran Que, Yuqi Liu, Wenge Rong, Xunliang Cai
- **Abstract**: The performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) is intrinsically linked to the quality of its training data. Although several studies have proposed methods for high-quality data selection, they do not consider the importance of knowledge richness in text corpora. In this paper, we propose a novel and gradient-free High-Knowledge Scorer (HKS) to select high-quality data from the dimension of knowledge, to alleviate the problem of knowledge scarcity in the pre-trained corpus. We propose a comprehensive multi-domain knowledge element pool and introduce knowledge density and coverage as metrics to assess the knowledge content of the text. Based on this, we propose a comprehensive knowledge scorer to select data with intensive knowledge, which can also be utilized for domain-specific high-knowledge data selection by restricting knowledge elements to the specific domain. We train models on a high-knowledge bilingual dataset, and experimental results demonstrate that our scorer improves the model's performance in knowledge-intensive and general comprehension tasks, and is effective in enhancing both the generic and domain-specific capabilities of the model.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Enhancing LLMs via High-Knowledge Data Selection":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of knowledge scarcity in the pre-training data used for Large Language Models (LLMs). It proposes a novel, gradient-free High-Knowledge Scorer (HKS) to select training data based on knowledge content. The approach involves creating a multi-domain knowledge element pool, quantifying knowledge density and coverage within text samples, and using these metrics to score and select high-knowledge data.  Experiments on a 1.1B model trained on a bilingual dataset, as well as continual pretraining on Llama-3-8B, show that HKS improves performance on both knowledge-intensive and general understanding tasks, and that it can be adapted for domain-specific knowledge selection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of explicitly selecting pre-training data based on knowledge *density* and *coverage*, as opposed to solely relying on perplexity, fluency, or self-influence, is a reasonable step forward. The reduction of knowledge to "knowledge elements" for ease of extraction and annotation is a practical simplification. The creation of a multi-domain knowledge element pool is a significant engineering effort that could be of value to other researchers.
*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates improvements in model performance on knowledge-intensive tasks, which are critical for LLMs to be reliable and useful. The domain-specific adaptation of HKS provides a useful mechanism for specializing model capabilities. The ability to improve model performance without increasing model size or pre-training data volume is economically and environmentally significant.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper presents a well-defined methodology with clear steps for knowledge element identification, scoring, and data selection.
    *   The experimental results, including comparisons to several strong baselines, support the claim that HKS improves model performance.
    *   The analysis of HKS performance on different subsets of the Pile dataset provides valuable insights into the scorer's behavior.
    *   Demonstrates that the knowledge is transferrable across modalities, e.g. monolingual to multilingual.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The definition of a "knowledge element" remains somewhat vague. While simplification is good, the lack of structure might limit the scorer's ability to capture deeper relationships or nuanced knowledge.
    *   The manual annotation step in creating the knowledge element pool introduces subjectivity and potential bias. While the authors report annotation accuracy, it's unclear how consistent or comprehensive the annotation process was.
    *   The dependence on GPT-4 for initial extraction adds a layer of opaqueness. The performance of the method is implicitly tied to the specific capabilities (and biases) of GPT-4.
    *   The method relies on having an external "knowledge base" of extracted text. It should've also explored how to directly infer the correct answer from the pre-training text only.
    *   The selection of Identity, sin, and log functions for scoring, while empirically justified, lack theoretical grounding. A stronger argument for why these functions are suitable for combining density and coverage would enhance the paper.
    *   The experiments focus primarily on a single model size (1.1B) and a specific bilingual dataset. Generalizability to other model architectures and datasets needs further investigation.
    *   The social impact statement is weak. While reducing energy costs is good, the claims about addressing fake news are overstated. It needs more direct support.
*   **Potential influence:**  The paper could influence researchers to explore more targeted data selection strategies based on knowledge content. The knowledge element pool and HKS methodology could serve as a starting point for future research in this area.

**Justification of Score:**

This paper presents a valuable, if somewhat incremental, advance in the field of LLM pre-training. While the basic concept of knowledge-based data selection is intuitive, the authors provide a reasonably sound method and experimental evaluation to support their approach. The demonstrated improvements are not groundbreaking, and the method is still tied to manual intervention and the performance of external tools. I would consider it a middle-of-the-road paper with room for improvement.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Textual Steering Vectors Can Improve Visual Understanding in Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14071v1)
- **Authors**: Woody Haosheng Gan, Deqing Fu, Julian Asilis, Ollie Liu, Dani Yogatama, Vatsal Sharan, Robin Jia, Willie Neiswanger
- **Abstract**: Steering methods have emerged as effective and targeted tools for guiding large language models' (LLMs) behavior without modifying their parameters. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs), however, do not currently enjoy the same suite of techniques, due in part to their recency and architectural diversity. Inspired by this gap, we investigate whether MLLMs can be steered using vectors derived from their text-only LLM backbone, via sparse autoencoders (SAEs), mean shift, and linear probing. We find that text-derived steering consistently enhances multimodal accuracy across diverse MLLM architectures and visual tasks. In particular, mean shift boosts spatial relationship accuracy on CV-Bench by up to +7.3% and counting accuracy by up to +3.3%, outperforming prompting and exhibiting strong generalization to out-of-distribution datasets. These results highlight textual steering vectors as a powerful, efficient mechanism for enhancing grounding in MLLMs with minimal additional data collection and computational overhead.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates using steering vectors derived from text-only LLMs to improve the visual understanding capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). The core idea is that since many MLLMs are built upon pre-trained LLMs, steering vectors extracted from the text-only backbone can be repurposed to guide the MLLM's behavior. The authors explore using Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs), Mean Shift, and Linear Probing on the LLM backbone to derive these steering vectors. They then apply these vectors to the MLLM's hidden states during visual reasoning tasks such as spatial relations and object counting. The experiments demonstrate consistent improvements across diverse MLLM architectures and visual tasks, often outperforming direct prompting. The study also shows generalization to out-of-distribution datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the transfer of steering techniques from text-only LLMs to MLLMs, specifically by utilizing representations learned solely from the text-only backbone. While steering methods themselves are not entirely new, their application to MLLMs in this manner is a relatively unexplored area. The observation that text-derived steering vectors can effectively influence multimodal understanding is an important contribution. Prior research in steering has primarily focused on text-only LLMs or on constructing intervention vectors from multimodal data.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is multi-faceted:

    *   **Practicality:** The approach is practical as it leverages existing LLM steering methods and requires minimal additional data collection or computational overhead. This makes it an accessible technique for improving MLLM performance.
    *   **Interpretability:** Steering methods are generally more interpretable than fine-tuning, offering insights into how the MLLM processes information.
    *   **Performance Improvements:** The consistent gains across multiple models and tasks validate the effectiveness of the proposed approach. The outperformance of prompting highlights the importance of internal representation manipulation for MLLMs. The observation that mean shift often outperforms other techniques is a valuable empirical finding.
    *   **Generalization:** The demonstration of generalization to out-of-distribution datasets is a crucial aspect of the study, indicating the robustness of the learned steering vectors.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement and Motivation:** The paper clearly identifies the gap in applying steering methods to MLLMs.
    *   **Comprehensive Methodology:** The study explores multiple steering vector extraction techniques and applies them to several MLLMs across diverse tasks.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The results consistently demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Analysis of Results:** The paper provides insightful analysis of the results, explaining the observed trends and comparing different steering methods.
    *   **Open-Weight Models:** Use of open-weight models ensures replicability.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Exploration of Layer Selection:** While a grid search is performed for layer selection, a more in-depth analysis of why certain layers are more effective would be valuable. More specifically, some discussion around **why** middle layers are chosen would have been welcome.
    *   **Reliance on Text-Only Backbones:** The method's dependence on a text-only backbone limits its applicability to MLLMs trained from scratch or those with tightly integrated multimodal components. It is unclear if, or how, the results can be extended to end-to-end MLLMs. A discussion of this limitation, and potential future work would be valuable.
    *   **Limited Dataset Diversity:** The study primarily focuses on CV-Bench. While out-of-distribution generalization is shown, expanding the evaluation to a more diverse set of multimodal datasets would further strengthen the findings.
    *   **Modest Gains:** While consistent, the gains are relatively modest. Larger gains would suggest that textual steering vectors could close the gap of MLLM performance when compared to text-only LLMs.
    *   **Prompting Baseline:** While the paper highlights the limitations of prompting, this is partly because the prompt used is generic (e.g. "Emphasize objects' positions relative to each other"). In this instance, the models could have just learned a spurious correlation between prompts and the CV-Bench dataset.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

    *   **Encouraging further research into steering MLLMs:** It highlights a promising direction for improving MLLM performance.
    *   **Promoting the transfer of knowledge between modalities:** It demonstrates the potential for leveraging representations learned in one modality to enhance performance in another.
    *   **Providing a practical and accessible technique for improving MLLM performance:** The proposed approach is relatively easy to implement and requires minimal resources.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and practical approach to steering MLLMs using text-derived vectors. The consistent empirical results and demonstration of out-of-distribution generalization are strong contributions. However, the limitations regarding layer selection, reliance on text-only backbones, modest gains and prompting, dataset diversity and the relatively modest performance gains prevent it from achieving a higher score. Nevertheless, the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field and has the potential to inspire further research into steering and representation learning for MLLMs.
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### CE-LSLM: Efficient Large-Small Language Model Inference and Communication via Cloud-Edge Collaboration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14085v1)
- **Authors**: Pengyan Zhu, Tingting Yang
- **Abstract**: Emerging intelligent service scenarios in 6G communication impose stringent requirements for low latency, high reliability, and privacy preservation. Generative large language models (LLMs) are gradually becoming key enablers for the integration of semantic communication and computation. However, due to the limited computational resources of edge devices and the increasing complexity of heterogeneous terminal access, existing centralized inference approaches fail to meet the dual demands of response efficiency and data privacy in edge-side inference tasks. To address these challenges, this paper proposes a novel collaborative inference architecture that integrates cloud-based LLMs with edge-deployed small language models (SLMs), enabling dynamic scheduling and sharing of semantic-level intermediate states, and establishing a unified computation-communication paradigm tailored for 6G networks. Specifically, a key-value (KV) cache reuse mechanism is introduced to enhance the semantic understanding of edge models through contextual guidance from the cloud, while significantly reducing edge-side computational and storage overhead. Furthermore, a cross-node parallel scheduling mechanism is proposed to achieve asynchronous coordination between model state loading and decoding computation, thereby improving edge responsiveness. In addition, we investigate layer alignment and representation compression strategies between heterogeneous models to alleviate the communication burden on the edge. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed architecture exhibits superior adaptability and scalability in terms of inference latency, system stability, and concurrent processing capacity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the CE-LSLM paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "CE-LSLM: Efficient Large-Small Language Model Inference and Communication via Cloud-Edge Collaboration" proposes a novel architecture for collaborative inference between cloud-based large language models (LLMs) and edge-deployed small language models (SLMs) in 6G networks.  The core idea is to leverage the strengths of both: LLMs for complex semantic understanding and SLMs for low-latency, privacy-preserving local inference.  The architecture includes:

1.  **KV Cache Reuse:**  Sharing key-value (KV) cache generated by the cloud LLM with edge SLMs to enhance their accuracy and reduce computational load.
2.  **Cross-Node Parallel Scheduling:** Achieves asynchronous coordination between model state loading and decoding, improving edge responsiveness.
3.  **Layer Alignment and Representation Compression:**  Strategies to reduce communication overhead between heterogeneous models.

The paper presents experimental results demonstrating the proposed architecture's superior adaptability, scalability, system stability, and concurrent processing capacity compared to cloud-only and edge-only baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the idea of cloud-edge collaboration for LLM inference is not entirely new, CE-LSLM makes several incremental contributions that, combined, create a practical and effective system:

    *   The explicit focus on 6G network constraints (low latency, privacy) provides a relevant and timely context.
    *   The detailed exploration of KV cache reuse strategies, including both cloud-edge and edge-edge sharing, goes beyond simple model partitioning. The hierarchical and pipelined approach is a good design.
    *   The consideration of layer alignment and representation compression specifically to reduce communication bottlenecks is valuable, though these techniques are built upon existing work.
