*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from several factors:

    *   **Improved Performance:**  The empirical results convincingly demonstrate that RLoT improves reasoning accuracy across a range of tasks and LLMs. The performance gains, particularly on challenging benchmarks like GPQA, are noteworthy.
    *   **Efficiency:** The extremely small size of the navigator model (less than 3K parameters) is a major advantage. This makes RLoT highly efficient and applicable even to resource-constrained environments. The ability to boost the performance of smaller LLMs to match that of much larger models is particularly impactful.
    *   **Transferability:** The demonstration of strong transferability across different LLMs and tasks significantly enhances the practicality of RLoT.  This means that a single trained navigator model can be used to improve reasoning in various scenarios without task-specific fine-tuning.
    *   **Adaptability:** RLoT addresses the limitations of fixed logical structures, making it adaptable to task-specific logical structures

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear and Well-Defined Method:** The RLoT framework is clearly explained, with detailed descriptions of the MDP formulation, logic block design, and training process.
    *   **Extensive Empirical Evaluation:** The paper presents a comprehensive set of experiments across diverse reasoning tasks and LLMs.
    *   **Strong Results:** The performance improvements achieved by RLoT are substantial and well-supported by the experimental data.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The efficiency and transferability of RLoT make it a practical and valuable technique for enhancing LLM reasoning in real-world applications.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Process Reward Model (PRM):** The paper relies on the Math-Shepherd as the PRM. While the PRM is pre-trained, the overall system's performance is dependent on its quality and its ability to provide accurate reward signals across different domains. A weak PRM could limit the effectiveness of RLoT.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While ablation studies are included, further investigation into the impact of individual logic blocks and specific training parameters could provide deeper insights.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   **Research Direction:**  RLoT is likely to influence future research in inference-time LLM reasoning, encouraging exploration of dynamic and adaptive techniques.
    *   **Practical Applications:** RLoT has the potential to be widely adopted in various applications where improved LLM reasoning is critical, such as question answering, problem solving, and decision support.

**Overall Assessment:**

RLoT represents a significant and well-executed contribution to the field of LLM reasoning. Its novelty, practical benefits, and potential impact justify a high score. The paper effectively addresses a key limitation of existing inference-time techniques and demonstrates the effectiveness of RL in dynamically constructing reasoning strategies.

Score: 8.5

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### s3: You Don't Need That Much Data to Train a Search Agent via RL
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14146v1)
- **Authors**: Pengcheng Jiang, Xueqiang Xu, Jiacheng Lin, Jinfeng Xiao, Zifeng Wang, Jimeng Sun, Jiawei Han
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems empower large language models (LLMs) to access external knowledge during inference. Recent advances have enabled LLMs to act as search agents via reinforcement learning (RL), improving information acquisition through multi-turn interactions with retrieval engines. However, existing approaches either optimize retrieval using search-only metrics (e.g., NDCG) that ignore downstream utility or fine-tune the entire LLM to jointly reason and retrieve-entangling retrieval with generation and limiting the real search utility and compatibility with frozen or proprietary models. In this work, we propose s3, a lightweight, model-agnostic framework that decouples the searcher from the generator and trains the searcher using a Gain Beyond RAG reward: the improvement in generation accuracy over naive RAG. s3 requires only 2.4k training samples to outperform baselines trained on over 70x more data, consistently delivering stronger downstream performance across six general QA and five medical QA benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "s3: You Don't Need That Much Data to Train a Search Agent via RL" introduces a novel framework, 's3', for training search agents in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems.  Unlike previous approaches that either optimize retrieval using metrics detached from generation quality or fine-tune the entire Language Model (LLM) end-to-end, 's3' decouples the search component from the generator LLM. It trains only the searcher using a novel reward signal called "Gain Beyond RAG" (GBR), which measures the improvement in generation accuracy when using the searcher's retrieved context compared to a naive RAG baseline. A key finding is that 's3' achieves state-of-the-art performance on several QA benchmarks with only 2.4k training samples, significantly outperforming baselines trained on 70x more data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents several genuinely novel aspects:

*   **Decoupled Searcher Training:**  The core idea of training the search component independently of the generator, using a generation-aware reward function, is a significant departure from prior end-to-end or search-metric focused approaches. This allows for flexibility and compatibility with "frozen" or proprietary LLMs.
*   **Gain Beyond RAG (GBR) Reward:** The proposed GBR reward function is a well-motivated contribution.  It directly quantifies the value added by the searcher in improving the generator's accuracy, sidestepping the limitations of relying solely on retrieval metrics or brittle exact match rewards. This design targets a direct measure of the value of search to downstream generation, making it a very useful innovation.
*   **Data Efficiency:**  The remarkable data efficiency achieved by 's3' is a very novel characteristic, enabling effective training with orders of magnitude less data than competing methods. This unlocks potential for low-resource settings.

**Significance:**

The work has potentially high significance due to:

*   **Improved RAG Performance:** Demonstrated state-of-the-art results across a range of QA datasets, improving the effectiveness of RAG systems.
*   **Reduced Training Costs:** The data efficiency makes training practical and accessible, reducing computational burdens. It enables adaptation of RAG search strategies for diverse applications and datasets.
*   **Modular Design:** The modular design increases flexibility, allowing researchers to easily experiment with different searchers and generators.
*   **Compatibility with Frozen LLMs:**  The model-agnostic approach makes this easily deployable in real-world scenarios.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing RAG approaches.
*   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The 's3' framework and GBR reward are logically derived and well-justified.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of 's3'. The comparison against numerous baselines strengthens the claims.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper includes ablation studies and analyses of the training process.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Exploration of Generator Diversity:** The paper focuses on improving search quality for a given generator LLM. While it proves GBR is a general signal, future work could investigate how the optimized searchers trained using GBR adapt to *different* generators (not just versions), particularly smaller LLMs, and explore potential trade-offs.
*   **Limited Insight into Search Strategies Learned:** While showing strong results, the paper doesn’t delve deeply into *what* specific search strategies the searcher is learning through the GBR reward (e.g., does it prioritize certain keywords? What kinds of query reformulations are most effective?). Deeper insight into the learned search behaviors could be very valuable.
*   **Dependency on LLM-based Reward:** LLM-based rewards for computing training signals are effective in this setting but computationally expensive, hindering scalability as pointed out by the authors themselves.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research by:

*   Encouraging more modular approaches to RAG.
*   Highlighting the importance of generation-aware reward functions for retrieval.
*   Opening the door to more data-efficient RL training methods.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper addresses a relevant problem with a novel, well-executed, and empirically validated approach. Its data efficiency and modularity make it an important contribution to the field of RAG. The identified weaknesses point to directions for future research but do not diminish the paper's overall value.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### SHARP: Synthesizing High-quality Aligned Reasoning Problems for Large Reasoning Models Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14147v1)
- **Authors**: Xiong Jun Wu, Zhenduo Zhang, ZuJie Wen, Zhiqiang Zhang, Wang Ren, Lei Shi, Cai Chen, Deng Zhao, Dingnan Jin, Qing Cui, Jun Zhou
- **Abstract**: Training large reasoning models (LRMs) with reinforcement learning in STEM domains is hindered by the scarcity of high-quality, diverse, and verifiable problem sets. Existing synthesis methods, such as Chain-of-Thought prompting, often generate oversimplified or uncheckable data, limiting model advancement on complex tasks. To address these challenges, we introduce SHARP, a unified approach to Synthesizing High-quality Aligned Reasoning Problems for LRMs reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). SHARP encompasses a strategic set of self-alignment principles -- targeting graduate and Olympiad-level difficulty, rigorous logical consistency, and unambiguous, verifiable answers -- and a structured three-phase framework (Alignment, Instantiation, Inference) that ensures thematic diversity and fine-grained control over problem generation. We implement SHARP by leveraging a state-of-the-art LRM to infer and verify challenging STEM questions, then employ a reinforcement learning loop to refine the model's reasoning through verifiable reward signals. Experiments on benchmarks such as GPQA demonstrate that SHARP-augmented training substantially outperforms existing methods, markedly improving complex reasoning accuracy and pushing LRM performance closer to expert-level proficiency. Our contributions include the SHARP strategy, framework design, end-to-end implementation, and experimental evaluation of its effectiveness in elevating LRM reasoning capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SHARP, a novel approach to synthesize high-quality, aligned reasoning problems specifically designed for training large reasoning models (LRMs) in STEM domains.  Recognizing the scarcity of suitable training data, SHARP aims to address limitations of existing synthesis methods (like Chain-of-Thought prompting) which often generate oversimplified or unverifiable examples.  SHARP employs a strategic set of self-alignment principles focusing on graduate-level difficulty, logical consistency, unambiguous answers, and a structured three-phase framework (Alignment, Instantiation, Inference).  The Instantiation phase leverages a three-tier subject categorization to ensure thematic diversity. The SHARP framework is implemented using a state-of-the-art LRM to infer and verify STEM questions, followed by a reinforcement learning loop to refine reasoning. Experiments on benchmarks like GPQA demonstrate improved performance compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a critical problem:** The paper tackles the real bottleneck of data scarcity and quality in training LRMs for complex STEM reasoning. Existing data generation methods are often insufficient, making this problem highly relevant.
*   **Novel Approach:** The SHARP framework offers a unified and comprehensive approach. The integration of self-alignment principles, the three-phase framework, and the three-tier subject categorization are well-designed and demonstrate careful consideration of the challenges. The emphasis on verifiable rewards is crucial for reinforcement learning.
*   **Well-structured and explained:** The paper is clearly written, with a logical flow. The SHARP strategy, framework, and implementation are detailed.  The algorithms are presented clearly.
*   **Experimental Validation:**  The performance improvements on GPQA benchmark, especially the Diamond subset, are significant. Demonstrating improvements over existing methods and approaching expert-level performance adds credibility. The ablation studies and additional analyses in the appendix are valuable.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on LLMs:** The entire framework depends heavily on the capabilities of the underlying LRM (e.g., DeepSeek R1).  While using a powerful LRM is pragmatic, it also raises questions about whether the improvements are primarily due to SHARP or the LRM's existing knowledge. It's not clear how well the method would work with a significantly weaker foundation model. There is limited ablation or analysis around this dependency.
*   **Limited Discussion of Failure Cases:**  While the paper presents successes, a deeper dive into the *types* of problems SHARP fails to generate effectively or where the synthesized data leads to suboptimal learning would be valuable. What are the edge cases or scenarios where the method struggles?
*   **Practicality Concerns:** While experiments are included in the appendix, a lot of information and assumptions are made that are not necessarily verifiable.
*   **Incremental Novelty:** Chain-of-Thought has previously been applied as an additional step. There is no discussion on why the current approach is different from previous approaches.
*   **Metrics & Evaluation:** As identified in the paper checklist, the lack of statistical significance for the data is concerning as there are a lot of metrics and measurements used.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty lies in the **unified and strategic combination** of existing techniques (self-alignment, structured problem generation, reinforcement learning) specifically tailored to the STEM reasoning domain. The three-phase framework and the emphasis on verifiable rewards and thematic diversity are valuable contributions. The significance stems from the potential to overcome data limitations, enable better training of LRMs, and advance AI capabilities in scientific problem-solving. However, the reliance on a high-performing underlying LRM somewhat limits the generalizability and potential impact.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper could influence future research in data synthesis, LRM training, and AI for STEM domains. The SHARP framework provides a concrete blueprint for generating high-quality training data, which could be adopted and adapted by other researchers. The emphasis on verifiable rewards could also spur further investigation into RL-based training methods.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a well-designed and experimentally validated approach for synthesizing high-quality STEM reasoning problems. While there are limitations, particularly concerning the reliance on LLMs and a deeper analysis of failure cases, the contributions are significant and the potential influence on the field is positive.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### MM-Agent: LLM as Agents for Real-world Mathematical Modeling Problem
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14148v1)
- **Authors**: Fan Liu, Zherui Yang, Cancheng Liu, Tianrui Song, Xiaofeng Gao, Hao Liu
- **Abstract**: Mathematical modeling is a cornerstone of scientific discovery and engineering practice, enabling the translation of real-world problems into formal systems across domains such as physics, biology, and economics. Unlike mathematical reasoning, which assumes a predefined formulation, modeling requires open-ended problem analysis, abstraction, and principled formalization. While Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong reasoning capabilities, they fall short in rigorous model construction, limiting their utility in real-world problem-solving. To this end, we formalize the task of LLM-powered real-world mathematical modeling, where agents must analyze problems, construct domain-appropriate formulations, and generate complete end-to-end solutions. We introduce MM-Bench, a curated benchmark of 111 problems from the Mathematical Contest in Modeling (MCM/ICM), spanning the years 2000 to 2025 and across ten diverse domains such as physics, biology, and economics. To tackle this task, we propose MM-Agent, an expert-inspired framework that decomposes mathematical modeling into four stages: open-ended problem analysis, structured model formulation, computational problem solving, and report generation. Experiments on MM-Bench show that MM-Agent significantly outperforms baseline agents, achieving an 11.88\% improvement over human expert solutions while requiring only 15 minutes and \$0.88 per task using GPT-4o. Furthermore, under official MCM/ICM protocols, MM-Agent assisted two undergraduate teams in winning the Finalist Award (\textbf{top 2.0\% among 27,456 teams}) in MCM/ICM 2025, demonstrating its practical effectiveness as a modeling copilot. Our code is available at https://github.com/usail-hkust/LLM-MM-Agent
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary of the paper and then offer a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance, assigning a score between 1 and 10 with a rigorous justification.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "MM-Agent," a novel framework that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) as agents for tackling real-world mathematical modeling problems.  It argues that existing LLMs, while demonstrating reasoning capabilities, often fall short in rigorous model construction due to the open-ended nature of problem analysis, abstraction, and formalization inherent in modeling. To address this, the authors formalize the task of LLM-powered real-world mathematical modeling, requiring agents to analyze problems, construct domain-appropriate formulations, and generate complete end-to-end solutions.  They also present "MM-Bench," a curated benchmark of 111 problems from the Mathematical Contest in Modeling (MCM/ICM), spanning diverse domains (physics, biology, economics) and years (2000 to 2025). The MM-Agent framework decomposes mathematical modeling into four stages: open-ended problem analysis, structured model formulation, computational problem solving, and report generation.  The experimental results on MM-Bench demonstrate that MM-Agent significantly outperforms baseline agents and even surpasses human expert solutions in some cases. Furthermore, MM-Agent assisted two undergraduate teams in winning the Finalist Award (top 2.0%) in MCM/ICM 2025, showcasing its practical effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by addressing a crucial gap in the application of LLMs to real-world problem-solving. Here's a breakdown of my assessment:

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The formalization of LLM-powered real-world mathematical modeling as a distinct task is novel.  Existing work primarily focused on mathematical reasoning within predefined formulations, not the upfront modeling process.
    *   **Benchmark Dataset (MM-Bench):** The creation and curation of MM-Bench is a valuable contribution.  It provides a standardized platform for evaluating the performance of LLM-based modeling agents, facilitating comparative analysis and future research. The diversity of problems within the benchmark is a major strength.
    *   **MM-Agent Framework:** The proposed four-stage framework (problem analysis, model formulation, computation, and reporting) provides a structured approach to guide LLM-based agents, which addresses the open-ended nature of mathematical modeling. The hierarchical mathematical modeling library (HMML) is a particularly interesting component, attempting to encode expert knowledge for better model selection.
    *   **Experimental Results:** The empirical evaluation demonstrating that MM-Agent outperforms baseline agents and sometimes even human solutions is compelling. The success of undergraduate teams using MM-Agent in the MCM/ICM competition provides real-world validation.
    *   **Addresses a Key Limitation of LLMs:** It directly tackles the issue of LLMs struggling with open-ended problem abstraction and principled formalization, a known weakness limiting their utility in many real-world scenarios.

*   **Weaknesses and Limitations:**

    *   **LLM Reliance:** The approach is heavily reliant on the capabilities of the underlying LLM (primarily GPT-40 in the experiments).  Future models with improved reasoning or more structured knowledge may significantly alter the landscape, potentially making specific aspects of the MM-Agent framework less critical. The paper partially addresses this by showing performance with DeepSeek-R1.
    *   **HMML Scalability and Generalizability:** The hierarchical mathematical modeling library (HMML) is a core component, but its scalability and generalizability are not fully addressed.  Maintaining and expanding the library with new domains and methods could become a significant challenge. There is also limited discussion on how HMML was initially constructed.
    *   **Contamination Risk:** The dataset uses problems from competitions, and while the authors mitigate the risk by using recent years' data, the possibility of LLMs being trained on publicly available solutions cannot be entirely ruled out. Deeper analysis of this is needed.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:**  While referencing official modeling evaluation criteria is helpful, subjective assessments are still present. More objective metrics, if possible, should be considered in future work.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Alternatives:** The paper focuses primarily on the proposed MM-Agent framework.  Exploring alternative architectures or methodologies for LLM-powered mathematical modeling could provide valuable insights and comparisons.
    *   **Cost and Computational Requirements:** The cost per task, while relatively low, could still be a barrier for wider adoption, especially for complex problems or iterative modeling processes. Reducing these costs would be highly beneficial.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field by providing a practical and effective methodology for leveraging LLMs in real-world mathematical modeling. This could lead to increased accessibility and automation of modeling tasks across various scientific and engineering disciplines.
    *   MM-Bench provides a valuable resource for future research in this area, enabling standardized evaluation and comparison of different approaches.
    *   The success of undergraduate teams using MM-Agent demonstrates the potential for this framework to democratize access to modeling expertise and enhance STEM education.

**Score:**

Based on the above analysis, I assign a score of **8.5** out of 10.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel, well-executed framework for LLM-powered real-world mathematical modeling, supported by a curated benchmark and real-world validation.  The MM-Agent framework demonstrably improves performance over existing baselines and has the potential to significantly impact the field. The paper also addresses a very difficult, real world problem. However, there are some limitations related to LLM reliance, HMML scalability, and potential contamination risks. Further research is needed to address these limitations and explore the broader implications of this approach.  It would also be beneficial to understand how HMML was initially constructed and what the challenges may be to add to or change the content. The positive impact on education and practical modeling is clear. The limitations are well discussed, and would require more detailed analysis.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 0/10

### Unify Graph Learning with Text: Unleashing LLM Potentials for Session Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14156v1)
- **Authors**: Songhao Wu, Quan Tu, Hong Liu, Jia Xu, Zhongyi Liu, Guannan Zhang, Ran Wang, Xiuying Chen, Rui Yan
- **Abstract**: Session search involves a series of interactive queries and actions to fulfill user's complex information need. Current strategies typically prioritize sequential modeling for deep semantic understanding, overlooking the graph structure in interactions. While some approaches focus on capturing structural information, they use a generalized representation for documents, neglecting the word-level semantic modeling. In this paper, we propose Symbolic Graph Ranker (SGR), which aims to take advantage of both text-based and graph-based approaches by leveraging the power of recent Large Language Models (LLMs). Concretely, we first introduce a set of symbolic grammar rules to convert session graph into text. This allows integrating session history, interaction process, and task instruction seamlessly as inputs for the LLM. Moreover, given the natural discrepancy between LLMs pre-trained on textual corpora, and the symbolic language we produce using our graph-to-text grammar, our objective is to enhance LLMs' ability to capture graph structures within a textual format. To achieve this, we introduce a set of self-supervised symbolic learning tasks including link prediction, node content generation, and generative contrastive learning, to enable LLMs to capture the topological information from coarse-grained to fine-grained. Experiment results and comprehensive analysis on two benchmark datasets, AOL and Tiangong-ST, confirm the superiority of our approach. Our paradigm also offers a novel and effective methodology that bridges the gap between traditional search strategies and modern LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Unify Graph Learning with Text: Unleashing LLM Potentials for Session Search" introduces Symbolic Graph Ranker (SGR), a novel approach to session search that combines sequential and graph modeling using Large Language Models (LLMs). The key idea is to convert session graphs into symbolic textual representations using a defined grammar. This allows LLMs to leverage both the semantic information in the text and the structural information captured in the graph. The paper also introduces self-supervised pre-training tasks (link prediction, node content generation, and generative contrastive learning) to help LLMs understand the symbolic graph representations. Experiments on AOL and Tiangong-ST datasets demonstrate SGR's superior performance compared to existing methods, especially when training data is limited.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its innovative combination of graph and textual representations for session search using LLMs. While individual components like graph-based session modeling and LLM application in IR are not entirely new, the specific approach of converting graphs to symbolic text and using self-supervised pre-training tasks to enhance LLM's ability to understand those representations is relatively novel. Previous methods have either focused on sequential modeling or used graph neural networks with less emphasis on fine-grained semantic meaning at a word level. The specific set of symbolic grammar rules and self-supervised learning tasks are also a significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in session search: effectively leveraging both semantic meaning and structural information. SGR's superior performance compared to strong baselines suggests that the proposed approach can significantly improve the accuracy and relevance of search results in interactive search sessions. The ability to achieve good performance with limited training data is particularly significant in real-world scenarios where labeled data is often scarce. Furthermore, the approach of using graph-to-text with pre-training provides a new and effective methodology that bridges the gap between traditional search strategies and modern LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper presents a clear and well-defined approach.
    *   The symbolic graph representation and the pre-training tasks are well-motivated and designed.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements over strong baselines on two benchmark datasets.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component of SGR.
    *   The analysis of performance with varying session lengths and data amounts provides a thorough evaluation of the model's robustness and scalability.
    *   The paper’s writing is clear and easy to understand.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper’s description can be confusing at times and hard to parse.
    *   While the improvements are significant, they are incremental relative to the state-of-the-art, particularly on the Tiangong-ST dataset.
    *   The reliance on specific LLMs (LLaMa-7B and BaiChuan-7B) limits the generalizability of the results. It would be beneficial to investigate how SGR performs with other LLMs.
    *   The paper could provide more insight into the choice of hyperparameters and the sensitivity of the model to different hyperparameter settings.
    *   The computational cost of pre-training and fine-tuning LLMs can be a barrier to adoption. The paper could discuss strategies for reducing the computational overhead.
    *   The symbolic grammar rules and pre-training tasks are somewhat specific to the session search task. It would be interesting to explore how they can be adapted to other graph-based NLP tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in session search and graph-based NLP. The approach of converting graphs to symbolic text and using LLMs to leverage both semantic and structural information can be applied to a wider range of tasks. The self-supervised pre-training tasks can also be adapted to other graph-based learning scenarios.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of session search by effectively integrating graph structures with textual semantics using LLMs. The novel symbolic graph representation and self-supervised learning tasks are well-designed and demonstrably improve performance. While the improvements are incremental, the overall approach is innovative and has the potential to inspire further research in this area. The weaknesses are also present, it provides a strong stepping stone for LLM applications.

**Score: 7.5**

**Emphasis on rigorous rationale:**
I have rated the paper 7.5 because the approach is innovative, the results are significant, and the analysis is thorough. The improvement over the state of the art is meaningful and the approach is a novel application of existing approaches.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Temporal Alignment of Time Sensitive Facts with Activation Engineering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14158v1)
- **Authors**: Sanjay Govindan, Maurice Pagnucco, Yang Song
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are trained on diverse and often conflicting knowledge spanning multiple domains and time periods. Some of this knowledge is only valid within specific temporal contexts, such as answering the question, "Who is the President of the United States in 2022?" Ensuring LLMs generate time appropriate responses is crucial for maintaining relevance and accuracy. In this work we explore activation engineering as a method for temporally aligning LLMs to improve factual recall without any training or dataset creation. In this research we explore an activation engineering technique to ground three versions of LLaMA 2 to specific points in time and examine the effects of varying injection layers and prompting strategies. Our experiments demonstrate up to a 44% and 16% improvement in relative and explicit prompting respectively, achieving comparable performance to the fine-tuning method proposed by Zhao et al. (2024) . Notably, our approach achieves similar results to the fine-tuning baseline while being significantly more computationally efficient and requiring no pre-aligned datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores using activation engineering (AE) to improve the temporal alignment of Large Language Models (LLMs) for fact recall. LLMs often struggle with time-sensitive queries because their training data spans different time periods. Instead of fine-tuning or overwriting knowledge, the authors propose injecting vectors into the residual stream of LLaMA2 models to align them to specific timeframes. They experiment with different injection layers and prompting strategies, demonstrating improvements in factual recall compared to relative and explicit prompting baselines.  The paper shows AE can achieve comparable performance to fine-tuning, but with less computational cost and without needing pre-aligned datasets.  The experiments use the Head of Governments (HOG) and Temporal Alignment Question Answer (Taqa) datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of activation engineering to the specific problem of temporal alignment in LLMs is a worthwhile contribution.  While AE itself isn't entirely new, using it to correct for *time-sensitive factual recall* provides a distinct perspective. The comparison against fine-tuning, a more established method, provides a strong context for understanding the method's value proposition. The exploration of *multi-layer* injection is also a slight advance over prior work that primarily looks at single-layer AE. The paper clearly explains how its focus on temporal factual recall is different from other uses of AE (toxicity reduction, topic fixation).

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate. The reduced computational overhead compared to fine-tuning is a crucial selling point, especially for resource-constrained environments. AE's potential for increased flexibility and responsiveness is also attractive for real-world applications where LLMs must adapt to changing information. However, the paper is primarily experimental, and the effect sizes are relatively small (up to 10% improvement in F1 score on the HOG dataset). While the method shows potential, it's not a dramatic breakthrough in performance. The dependence on prompt engineering remains, indicating it isn't a "plug and play" solution. The results across models and datasets are somewhat inconsistent.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined experimental setup and evaluation metrics (F1 score).
    *   Comparison against strong baselines (relative/explicit prompting, fine-tuning).
    *   Ablation studies (layer selection) provide insights into the method's behavior.
    *   Quantifiable gains in computational efficiency compared to fine-tuning.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The improvements in F1 score, while notable, are not transformative.
    *   Dependence on dataset quality - the filtering process for the Taqa dataset influences the results. The inconsistency across datasets is a concern.
    *   Some conclusions are speculative (e.g., surpassing fine-tuning with full parameter tuning). The limitation regarding limited GPU resources is also a concern.
    *   The impact of the prompts used and the exact approach to creating the temporal vectors requires more exploration, and how this can scale to more vague requests (vs specific time periods).

*   **Potential Influence:** The work could inspire further research into AE for temporal reasoning and knowledge management. It might also encourage the development of more targeted AE techniques that can address specific biases and inaccuracies in LLMs. It adds to the growing literature on efficient and adaptable methods for improving LLM performance.
* **Rigorous rationale**: The novelty is decent in its targeted use of AE. However, the F1 score gains are modest. The significant benefit seems to be around cost-effectiveness, however, it's not entirely clear that this scales. While the ablation studies provide insights, the core idea is incremental as the idea builds on well-established findings and methods (AE, the concept of time-sensitive recall).

**Score: 6**

The paper presents a solid, incremental advance in applying activation engineering for temporal alignment in LLMs. While the performance gains are not massive, the method's computational efficiency and potential for broader application in temporal reasoning contribute to its value. Further research is needed to improve the robustness and scalability of the technique.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### LMP: Leveraging Motion Prior in Zero-Shot Video Generation with Diffusion Transformer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14167v1)
- **Authors**: Changgu Chen, Xiaoyan Yang, Junwei Shu, Changbo Wang, Yang Li
- **Abstract**: In recent years, large-scale pre-trained diffusion transformer models have made significant progress in video generation. While current DiT models can produce high-definition, high-frame-rate, and highly diverse videos, there is a lack of fine-grained control over the video content. Controlling the motion of subjects in videos using only prompts is challenging, especially when it comes to describing complex movements. Further, existing methods fail to control the motion in image-to-video generation, as the subject in the reference image often differs from the subject in the reference video in terms of initial position, size, and shape. To address this, we propose the Leveraging Motion Prior (LMP) framework for zero-shot video generation. Our framework harnesses the powerful generative capabilities of pre-trained diffusion transformers to enable motion in the generated videos to reference user-provided motion videos in both text-to-video and image-to-video generation. To this end, we first introduce a foreground-background disentangle module to distinguish between moving subjects and backgrounds in the reference video, preventing interference in the target video generation. A reweighted motion transfer module is designed to allow the target video to reference the motion from the reference video. To avoid interference from the subject in the reference video, we propose an appearance separation module to suppress the appearance of the reference subject in the target video. We annotate the DAVIS dataset with detailed prompts for our experiments and design evaluation metrics to validate the effectiveness of our method. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance in generation quality, prompt-video consistency, and control capability. Our homepage is available at https://vpx-ecnu.github.io/LMP-Website/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LMP: Leveraging Motion Prior in Zero-Shot Video Generation with Diffusion Transformer":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LMP, a novel zero-shot framework for controlling motion in video generation using Diffusion Transformers (DiTs).  LMP allows users to transfer motion from a reference video to a generated video, given either a text prompt or a reference image. The framework consists of three main modules: 1) a foreground-background disentanglement module (FBDM) to separate the moving subject from the background in the reference video, 2) a reweighted motion transfer module (RMTM) to inject the motion information into the target video generation, and 3) an appearance separation module (ASM) to suppress the appearance of the subject in the reference video if it differs from the desired subject in the generated video.  The authors annotate the DAVIS dataset with richer prompts and propose new evaluation metrics to assess motion transfer and subject appearance preservation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LMP achieves state-of-the-art performance in generation quality, prompt-video consistency, and control capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in adapting motion transfer to DiT architectures in a zero-shot manner.  While previous works have explored motion control in video generation, they often rely on UNet-based architectures or require training. Moving to the DiT architecture is a significant step, given the increased performance and scaling capabilities of DiTs. The specific modules (FBDM, RMTM, ASM) are also reasonably novel in their design for the DiT paradigm. The use of attention maps for foreground/background disentanglement is conceptually interesting, although not entirely groundbreaking in isolation. The annotation of the DAVIS dataset with more detailed prompts is a useful contribution. The most novel idea is the way in which the modules work together to leverage DiT's global attention mechanism for motion control without specific training.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial limitation of current video generation models: the lack of fine-grained control over motion. By enabling motion transfer from reference videos, LMP empowers users to create more complex and personalized videos. The zero-shot nature of the framework makes it particularly valuable, as it can be applied to any pre-trained DiT model without requiring further fine-tuning. The ability to handle both text-to-video and image-to-video settings further expands the applicability of LMP. The design considerations of appearance separation, a subtle but important problem to address when the target object is different from the one exhibiting motion, underscores the significance of the framework to enable robust downstream applications.
    The paper contributes valuable evaluation metrics, specifically for assessing motion transfer and object identity preservation. The method offers new mechanisms for users to control motion of visual elements in diffusion models, a capability that has broad implications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Results:** The paper presents compelling qualitative and quantitative results demonstrating the effectiveness of LMP. The comparisons with baseline and SOTA methods highlight the superior performance of LMP.
    *   **Well-Designed Modules:** The FBDM, RMTM, and ASM modules are well-motivated and effectively address the challenges of motion transfer in DiT architectures.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, with clear explanations of the methods and experimental setups.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct a thorough evaluation of LMP, using multiple datasets, evaluation metrics, and ablation studies.
    *   **Practical Relevance:**  The zero-shot nature and applicability to both text-to-video and image-to-video settings make LMP a practically relevant contribution.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on LLMs:** The reliance on an LLM for prompt generation might introduce biases or limitations, depending on the performance and capabilities of the LLM.
    *   **Limited Qualitative Diversity:**  While the results are compelling, the qualitative examples could be more diverse to showcase the full range of motion transfer capabilities.
    *   **Computational Cost:** There is a computational burden required to obtain reference motion as the authors denoise the reference video in parallel with target video.

*   **Potential Influence:**  LMP has the potential to significantly influence the field of video generation. The zero-shot approach and the ability to control motion in DiT architectures make it a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners. The paper's insights into the attention mechanisms of DiTs could also inspire future work on controllable video generation.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of video generation. The zero-shot nature, adaptation to DiT architectures, and strong experimental results justify a relatively high score. However, some minor weaknesses, such as the dependence on LLMs and the computational burden, prevent it from achieving a perfect score.
    Also, there is potential to add additional comparisons.

**Score: 8.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### The Strawberry Problem: Emergence of Character-level Understanding in Tokenized Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14172v1)
- **Authors**: Adrian Cosma, Stefan Ruseti, Emilian Radoi, Mihai Dascalu
- **Abstract**: Despite their remarkable progress across diverse domains, Large Language Models (LLMs) consistently fail at simple character-level tasks, such as counting letters in words, due to a fundamental limitation: tokenization. In this work, we frame this limitation as a problem of low mutual information and analyze it in terms of concept emergence. Using a suite of 19 synthetic tasks that isolate character-level reasoning in a controlled setting, we show that such capabilities emerge slowly, suddenly, and only late in training. We further show that percolation-based models of concept emergence explain these patterns, suggesting that learning character composition is not fundamentally different from learning commonsense knowledge. To address this bottleneck, we propose a lightweight architectural modification that significantly improves character-level reasoning while preserving the inductive advantages of subword models. Together, our results bridge low-level perceptual gaps in tokenized LMs and provide a principled framework for understanding and mitigating their structural blind spots. We make our code publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the surprising inability of large language models (LLMs) to perform simple character-level tasks, such as counting letters in words. It frames this issue as a limitation caused by text tokenization, which severs the direct connection between words and their constituent characters. The authors propose that LLMs struggle because they receive very little direct signal (low mutual information) regarding character composition from the training data. The paper then presents a synthetic benchmark of 19 character-level tasks to analyze this phenomenon. The experiments show that character-level reasoning emerges slowly and late in training, is dependent on vocabulary size, and can be explained by percolation theory. Finally, the authors introduce a lightweight architectural modification, a block cross-attention mechanism, to improve character-level reasoning by explicitly exposing character-level information to the model. The results demonstrate that this modification significantly improves performance on the character-level tasks without sacrificing the benefits of subword tokenization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The problem highlighted by the paper is well-known, and character-level tasks are not new. The real novelty lies in the careful framing of the problem as an information bottleneck and its explanation using percolation theory. The proposed architectural modification, while not groundbreaking, is a simple and effective solution. The rigorous synthetic benchmark is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its principled analysis of a persistent limitation of LLMs. By framing the character-level reasoning problem as an issue of low mutual information, the authors provide a theoretical foundation for understanding this limitation. The use of percolation theory further strengthens the analysis by drawing a connection to broader theories of concept emergence.  The architectural intervention, demonstrating a way to bridge the gap between tokenization and character-level understanding, is practically valuable. The findings have implications for how we design and train LLMs to better handle low-level linguistic details.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and defines a specific limitation of LLMs.
    *   **Principled Analysis:** The framing of the problem in terms of mutual information and percolation theory provides a solid theoretical basis.
    *   **Controlled Experiments:** The synthetic benchmark allows for a rigorous and controlled evaluation of character-level reasoning abilities.
    *   **Effective Solution:** The proposed architectural modification is simple, lightweight, and demonstrably effective.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Real-World Validation:** While the results on Wikipedia data are promising, more extensive evaluation on diverse real-world tasks would be beneficial.
    *   **Incremental Architecture:** The proposed architectural change, while effective, is not drastically different from existing attention mechanisms.
    *   **Synthetic Data Reliance:** The tasks are intentionally simple. Its unclear that performance improvements on these would translate to more difficult tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's analysis and proposed solution are likely to influence future research on improving the character-level reasoning abilities of LLMs. It could inspire the development of more sophisticated architectures that better integrate character-level information without sacrificing the efficiency of subword tokenization. The synthetic benchmark could also serve as a valuable tool for evaluating such architectures. The connection between concept learning and character learning using percolation theory is potentially interesting and could lead to other insights.

*   **Score:** 7

*   **Rationale:** The paper makes a solid, significant, albeit not revolutionary contribution. The problem it addresses is relevant, the analysis is well-founded, and the solution is practical. However, the architectural change is relatively incremental, and the extent of real-world applicability needs further exploration. The reliance on somewhat simplistic, synthetic tasks detracts from its value. It is a thoughtful, insightful paper that pushes forward understanding, but not a breakthrough that completely changes the landscape.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Cheaper, Better, Faster, Stronger: Robust Text-to-SQL without Chain-of-Thought or Fine-Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14174v1)
- **Authors**: Yusuf Denizay Dönder, Derek Hommel, Andrea W Wen-Yi, David Mimno, Unso Eun Seo Jo
- **Abstract**: LLMs are effective at code generation tasks like text-to-SQL, but is it worth the cost? Many state-of-the-art approaches use non-task-specific LLM techniques including Chain-of-Thought (CoT), self-consistency, and fine-tuning. These methods can be costly at inference time, sometimes requiring over a hundred LLM calls with reasoning, incurring average costs of up to \$0.46 per query, while fine-tuning models can cost thousands of dollars. We introduce "N-rep" consistency, a more cost-efficient text-to-SQL approach that achieves similar BIRD benchmark scores as other more expensive methods, at only \$0.039 per query. N-rep leverages multiple representations of the same schema input to mitigate weaknesses in any single representation, making the solution more robust and allowing the use of smaller and cheaper models without any reasoning or fine-tuning. To our knowledge, N-rep is the best-performing text-to-SQL approach in its cost range.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "N-rep" consistency, a novel approach to text-to-SQL generation that aims to improve robustness and reduce computational cost compared to existing methods.  It works by leveraging multiple text representations of the database schema and performing schema linking using these representations. The approach then generates SQL candidates based on these linked schemas and uses a two-stage confidence-aware candidate selection process. This allows the use of smaller, cheaper models without chain-of-thought prompting or fine-tuning, while still achieving competitive performance on the BIRD benchmark. The paper demonstrates that N-rep achieves comparable accuracy to more expensive methods at significantly lower inference costs and without incurring any fine-tuning cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Cost-effectiveness:** The most compelling strength is the significant reduction in inference cost. Demonstrating comparable performance to SOTA methods while using cheaper models and fewer LLM calls is a valuable contribution, especially for practical applications. The paper clearly quantifies the cost savings, making the economic benefits readily apparent.
    * **Novelty:**  The approach of using multiple schema representations to improve robustness is a relatively novel idea in the text-to-SQL domain. It tackles the known sensitivity of LLMs to input formatting in a clever way.
    * **SQL-Specific Approach:** The paper differentiates itself from generic LLM techniques like CoT and self-consistency by leveraging domain-specific knowledge of database schemas and SQL. This targeted approach leads to efficiency gains.
    * **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the N-rep framework and its components. The experimental setup, results, and analysis are presented in a structured and understandable manner.
    * **Reproducibility (Partial):** The authors indicate plans to release open-source code, which will enhance reproducibility and adoption.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Evaluation of Schema Representations:** While the core idea of N-rep is to use multiple schema representations, the paper doesn't deeply explore the design space of these representations. A more extensive evaluation of different schema representations and their impact on performance would strengthen the paper.  The selection of the four best schema representations relied on optimization on a 10% dev set; a more rigorous approach, such as testing various representation combinations without this optimization, would reinforce the argument.
    * **Reliance on Manual Selection:** The paper mentions that the schema representations are manually selected.  A method for automating this selection process, or at least providing clear guidelines on how to select effective representations, would make the approach more practical and scalable. Automating the selection or generation of such varied database representations would substantially improve the paper's contribution.
    * **Generalizability Beyond BIRD:** The evaluation is primarily focused on the BIRD benchmark. While BIRD is a challenging dataset, demonstrating similar performance improvements on other datasets would further validate the generalizability of the N-rep approach. Testing on a wider variety of databases, including those with more complex schema characteristics, would enhance the findings.
    * **Black-Box Candidate Selection:**  The candidate selection stage involves an LLM-based pairwise comparison. While the paper states that only low-confidence samples are subject to it, there is a lack of detail concerning the precise prompts and strategies employed. Greater transparency regarding the LLM prompt to the pairwise comparison is required.
    * **Cost Claim Subtleties:** While the paper mentions Gemini 1.5 Flash for all candidate generation, it says Gemini 2.5 Pro is used for the baseline. Gemini 1.5 Flash and 2.5 Pro have different capabilities, limiting a direct comparison.
    * **Lack of Ablation Study:** No ablation study isolates the effects of key components, like the LLM used or number of candidate generations.

* **Novelty and Significance:** The paper demonstrates a tangible improvement in the efficiency of text-to-SQL generation.  While the individual components (multiple schema representations, candidate selection) are not entirely novel in isolation, the combination and the emphasis on cost reduction represent a valuable contribution. The ability to achieve competitive performance with cheaper models has practical implications for real-world deployment.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**.  The paper addresses a significant problem (high cost of LLM inference) and offers a practical solution (N-rep consistency). The performance improvements are clearly demonstrated with quantitative results. The use of multiple schema representations is a clever idea that contributes to robustness. However, the limitations related to manual selection of representations, dependence on a black-box component, and limited generalizability prevent a higher score. The paper is not revolutionary, but it offers a solid and potentially impactful incremental improvement in the field.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Tokenization Constraints in LLMs: A Study of Symbolic and Arithmetic Reasoning Limits
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14178v1)
- **Authors**: Xiang Zhang, Juntai Cao, Jiaqi Wei, Yiwei Xu, Chenyu You
- **Abstract**: Tokenization is the first - and often underappreciated - layer of computation in language models. While Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting enables transformer models to approximate recurrent computation by externalizing intermediate steps, we show that the success of such reasoning is fundamentally bounded by the structure of tokenized inputs. This work presents a theoretical and empirical investigation into how tokenization schemes, particularly subword-based methods like byte-pair encoding (BPE), impede symbolic computation by merging or obscuring atomic reasoning units. We introduce the notion of Token Awareness to formalize how poor token granularity disrupts logical alignment and prevents models from generalizing symbolic procedures. Through systematic evaluation on arithmetic and symbolic tasks, we demonstrate that token structure dramatically affect reasoning performance, causing failure even with CoT, while atomically-aligned formats unlock strong generalization, allowing small models (e.g., GPT-4o-mini) to outperform larger systems (e.g., o1) in structured reasoning. Our findings reveal that symbolic reasoning ability in LLMs is not purely architectural, but deeply conditioned on token-level representations.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Tokenization Constraints in LLMs: A Study of Symbolic and Arithmetic Reasoning Limits":

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the impact of tokenization schemes, particularly subword-based methods like Byte Pair Encoding (BPE), on the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to perform symbolic and arithmetic reasoning. The authors introduce the concept of "Token Awareness" to formalize how token granularity affects logical alignment and generalization.  Through empirical evaluations on arithmetic and symbolic tasks, they demonstrate that token structure significantly influences reasoning performance, sometimes causing failure even with Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting.  The study reveals that aligning tokenization with atomic reasoning units unlocks stronger generalization, enabling smaller models to outperform larger ones in structured reasoning.  The authors conclude that symbolic reasoning ability in LLMs is not solely determined by architecture but is deeply conditioned by token-level representations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper's central thesis, that tokenization significantly impacts the symbolic reasoning capabilities of LLMs, is compelling and well-supported by the empirical evidence. While the architectural limitations of transformers have been extensively studied, the role of tokenization as a bottleneck in exploiting the *theoretical* reasoning capabilities unlocked by CoT has received less attention. This is where the novelty lies.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Statement:**  The paper clearly identifies and articulates the problem: Tokenization, often overlooked, can be a significant constraint on the reasoning abilities of LLMs.
*   **Theoretical Foundation:**  The paper introduces the concept of "Token Awareness" and formally relates it to the expressiveness of the language and the fidelity of the token-to-thought mapping. This provides a theoretical framework for understanding the observed empirical results.
*   **Strong Empirical Evidence:**  The authors conduct systematic experiments on various arithmetic and symbolic tasks, using a variety of LLMs (including closed-source models). The use of "atomic-aligned" and "merged-token" inputs allows for controlled manipulation of tokenization, providing strong support for their claims.  The demonstration that carefully tokenized inputs can allow smaller models to outperform larger ones is particularly striking.
*   **Model-Agnostic Approach:** The chosen evaluation approach is mostly model-agnostic, emphasizing the study of the relation between the input and the black-box model, contributing to its generalizability.
*   **Well-Defined Metrics:** The `Atok` metric provides a clear and quantifiable measure of the degradation in performance caused by tokenization.
*   **Error Analysis:** The analysis of error shifts in counting tasks provides valuable insights into the types of errors induced by BPE-based tokenization.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Tasks:** While the chosen tasks (counting, sorting, reversing) are foundational, the paper could benefit from exploring a wider range of symbolic reasoning tasks.  For example, tasks involving logical inference or constraint satisfaction could further strengthen the claims.
*   **Lack of a Solution:** While the paper convincingly demonstrates the problem, it does not offer a concrete solution or a practical algorithm for optimizing tokenization for symbolic reasoning.  This leaves room for future work to build upon their findings. It would be useful to test specific solutions such as learned tokenizers, character-level models, or specialized pre-processing steps for LLMs.
*   **"Ideal Assumptions" Are Untenable in Practice:** The CoT theoretical guarantees rely on ideal conditions not fully met in realistic scenarios. Addressing this aspect and evaluating how tokenization interacts with CoT’s limitations would have enhanced the analysis.

**Significance and Impact:**

The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by shifting attention towards the importance of tokenization in LLM design and deployment. The findings highlight the need for tokenizers that are better aligned with the symbolic reasoning capabilities of these models. It raises important questions about the trade-offs between compression efficiency and reasoning fidelity in tokenization schemes.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's rigorous methodology, compelling empirical results, and clear articulation of a previously underappreciated problem warrant a high score. While it lacks a specific solution, its identification of a key bottleneck and its theoretical framework provide a strong foundation for future research. The fact that carefully tokenized *smaller* models can outperform larger models is a significant practical implication. However, the limited scope of tasks and lack of a practical solution hold it back from being a truly exceptional contribution.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### SlangDIT: Benchmarking LLMs in Interpretative Slang Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14181v1)
- **Authors**: Yunlong Liang, Fandong Meng, Jiaan Wang, Jie Zhou
- **Abstract**: The challenge of slang translation lies in capturing context-dependent semantic extensions, as slang terms often convey meanings beyond their literal interpretation. While slang detection, explanation, and translation have been studied as isolated tasks in the era of large language models (LLMs), their intrinsic interdependence remains underexplored. The main reason is lacking of a benchmark where the two tasks can be a prerequisite for the third one, which can facilitate idiomatic translation. In this paper, we introduce the interpretative slang translation task (named SlangDIT) consisting of three sub-tasks: slang detection, cross-lingual slang explanation, and slang translation within the current context, aiming to generate more accurate translation with the help of slang detection and slang explanation. To this end, we construct a SlangDIT dataset, containing over 25k English-Chinese sentence pairs. Each source sentence mentions at least one slang term and is labeled with corresponding cross-lingual slang explanation. Based on the benchmark, we propose a deep thinking model, named SlangOWL. It firstly identifies whether the sentence contains a slang, and then judges whether the slang is polysemous and analyze its possible meaning. Further, the SlangOWL provides the best explanation of the slang term targeting on the current context. Finally, according to the whole thought, the SlangOWL offers a suitable translation. Our experiments on LLMs (\emph{e.g.}, Qwen2.5 and LLama-3.1), show that our deep thinking approach indeed enhances the performance of LLMs where the proposed SLangOWL significantly surpasses the vanilla models and supervised fine-tuned models without thinking.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SlangDIT: Benchmarking LLMs in Interpretative Slang Translation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new task, "Interpretative Slang Translation" (SlangDIT), designed to address the challenges of slang translation, particularly in capturing context-dependent semantic extensions and polysemy. The task involves three sub-tasks: slang detection, cross-lingual slang explanation, and slang translation within context.  The authors construct a large-scale dataset, also named SlangDIT, consisting of over 25,000 English-Chinese sentence pairs, each labeled with at least one slang term and a corresponding cross-lingual explanation.  They then propose a deep-thinking model, SlangOWL, which simulates human cognitive processes by identifying slang, judging polysemy, analyzing meaning, and providing the best cross-lingual explanation before generating a translation.  Experiments using LLMs show that SlangOWL outperforms vanilla and supervised fine-tuned models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of explicitly addressing the interdependent sub-tasks of slang detection, explanation, and translation within a single framework and dataset is novel. While each of these sub-tasks has been studied individually, the joint consideration and the focus on interpretative translation add a new dimension. The creation of the SlangDIT dataset itself is a significant contribution, addressing the lack of resources for this specific task.
*   **Significance:** The significance lies in highlighting and addressing the limitations of current LLMs in understanding and translating slang accurately, particularly concerning polysemy and contextual nuances.  By introducing the SlangDIT benchmark and SlangOWL model, the authors provide a valuable tool for evaluating and improving LLMs' abilities in this area. The findings show that simply fine-tuning models or using chain-of-thought reasoning is insufficient, emphasizing the need for a deeper, more context-aware approach.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined task:**  The SlangDIT task is clearly defined and addresses a practical challenge in NLP.
    *   **Large-scale dataset:**  The SlangDIT dataset is a valuable resource for researchers working on slang understanding and translation. The inclusion of polysemous slang terms and cross-lingual explanations enhances its utility.
    *   **Deep-thinking model:** The SlangOWL model is a promising approach to improving slang translation accuracy by simulating human cognitive processes.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:**  The experiments are well-designed and demonstrate the effectiveness of SlangOWL compared to other models.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Language Pair Limitation:** The SlangDIT dataset and experiments are limited to English-Chinese.  Expanding to other language pairs would increase the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Model Complexity:** The SlangOWL model appears to be computationally intensive due to its deep-thinking approach. Further optimization or simplification may be needed for practical applications.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs for Annotation:**  While the authors take steps to ensure data quality, the use of LLMs for dataset creation may introduce biases or errors.  Manual validation of a larger portion of the dataset would strengthen its reliability. The dependence on GPT-4o for evaluation also presents a potential for bias.
    *   **Scope of Slang:** It's not entirely clear what criteria were used to define "slang." A more explicit definition would strengthen the rigor of the study.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in slang understanding, cross-lingual translation, and the development of more robust and context-aware NLP models.  The SlangDIT benchmark could become a standard resource for evaluating LLMs in these areas.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel task, a valuable dataset, and a promising model for interpretative slang translation. While the study has some limitations (language pair, model complexity, reliance on LLMs), the contributions are significant and have the potential to advance the field.  The paper addresses a real-world problem and provides a well-defined benchmark for future research. The thorough experimental results and analyses further contribute to the overall value.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ThinkSwitcher: When to Think Hard, When to Think Fast
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14183v1)
- **Authors**: Guosheng Liang, Longguang Zhong, Ziyi Yang, Xiaojun Quan
- **Abstract**: Large reasoning models (LRMs) excel at solving complex tasks by leveraging long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning. However, this often leads to overthinking on simple tasks, resulting in unnecessary computational overhead. We observe that LRMs inherently possess the capability for efficient short CoT reasoning, which can be reliably elicited through prompt design. To leverage this capability, we propose ThinkSwitcher, a framework that enables a single LRM to dynamically switch between short and long CoT modes based on task complexity. ThinkSwitcher introduces a lightweight switching module trained with supervision signals derived from the relative performance of each reasoning mode across tasks. Experiments on multiple reasoning benchmarks show that ThinkSwitcher reduces computational cost by 20-30% while maintaining high accuracy on complex tasks. This demonstrates the effectiveness of ThinkSwitcher as a scalable and efficient solution for unified LRM deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ThinkSwitcher: When to Think Hard, When to Think Fast" addresses the inefficiency of large reasoning models (LRMs) that tend to "overthink" even simple tasks, leading to unnecessary computational overhead. The authors propose ThinkSwitcher, a framework that enables a single LRM to dynamically switch between a long chain-of-thought (CoT) mode for complex tasks and a short CoT mode for simpler tasks. This switching is managed by a lightweight switching module trained with self-supervised signals derived from the LRM's performance under each reasoning mode.  Experiments across multiple reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that ThinkSwitcher reduces computational costs (token usage) by 20-30% while maintaining high accuracy on complex tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of dynamically adapting reasoning depth is not entirely novel, as prior work has explored dual-system models and prompt engineering techniques to influence CoT length. The paper's primary novelty lies in the specific implementation:
    *   The use of a lightweight, self-supervised switching module trained on performance differences between long and short CoT *without* requiring additional labels or extensive post-training.  This is a practical advantage.
    *   The empirical observation that simply adding empty `<think></think>` blocks can reliably induce short CoT reasoning in existing LRMs *without* any model changes is interesting and potentially useful.  While Qwen3 noted this, the paper provides a deeper evaluation and application.
    *   The margin-aware training objective for the switcher, explicitly encouraging it to choose modes that lead to higher pass rates.
*   **Significance:** The paper has practical significance.  Reducing inference costs for LRMs is a crucial problem, particularly for high-throughput applications. ThinkSwitcher offers a relatively simple and scalable solution, potentially making LRMs more accessible and efficient. The savings on token usage are clearly demonstrated, although more in-depth analyses of the wall-clock time savings would be beneficial (accounting for the overhead of the switcher).
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation across multiple datasets and model sizes, demonstrating consistent improvements in efficiency without significant accuracy loss.
    *   **Simplicity and Scalability:** The ThinkSwitcher framework is lightweight and doesn't require extensive model retraining or architecture modifications, making it easier to deploy with existing LRMs.
    *   **Self-Supervised Approach:**  The self-supervised training of the switching module eliminates the need for costly human annotation or curated datasets.
    *   **Clear Explanation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology, results, and analyses.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:**  The evaluation focuses primarily on mathematical reasoning tasks. It's unclear how well ThinkSwitcher would generalize to other reasoning domains, such as code generation or commonsense reasoning. Further research is required.
    *   **Dependency on Special Tokens:** The method relies on the presence of special tokens like `<think>` and `</think>` in the LRM's architecture. This limits its applicability to models that do not use these tokens.  While this could be overcome by fine-tuning, it somewhat undermines the "no model changes" claim.
    *   **Limited Analytical Depth:** While the paper provides strong empirical evidence, a deeper theoretical understanding of *why* the simple prompt engineering (empty thinking blocks) works would strengthen the paper.  Appendix C offers a helpful starting point, but further investigation could enhance its contribution.
    *   **Switching Overhead:** While the paper mentions the computational cost of the switcher module is negligible, a more detailed breakdown of the inference time with and without the switcher would be useful.
    *   **Adaptive threshold selection:** While the paper selected values that provided a good balance between accuracy and efficiency, it would be interesting to see the implications of an automatically adaptive threshold that adjusts based on the question/task/environment.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical approach to improving the efficiency of LRMs.  It could inspire further research into adaptive reasoning strategies and more efficient LRM deployments.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the paper's novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign it a score of **7**. The core idea is not entirely new, but the specific implementation, the self-supervised training approach, and the demonstrated empirical results make it a valuable contribution to the field. The limitations related to task scope, special token dependency, and lack of in-depth theoretical analysis prevent it from achieving a higher score. Nevertheless, the practical benefits of ThinkSwitcher and its potential to inspire future research justify a solid score. The score represents an incremental but significant step towards more efficient and scalable LRM deployments.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Safety Subspaces are Not Distinct: A Fine-Tuning Case Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14185v1)
- **Authors**: Kaustubh Ponkshe, Shaan Shah, Raghav Singhal, Praneeth Vepakomma
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) rely on safety alignment to produce socially acceptable responses. This is typically achieved through instruction tuning and reinforcement learning from human feedback. However, this alignment is known to be brittle: further fine-tuning, even on benign or lightly contaminated data, can degrade safety and reintroduce harmful behaviors. A growing body of work suggests that alignment may correspond to identifiable geometric directions in weight space, forming subspaces that could, in principle, be isolated or preserved to defend against misalignment. In this work, we conduct a comprehensive empirical study of this geometric perspective. We examine whether safety-relevant behavior is concentrated in specific subspaces, whether it can be separated from general-purpose learning, and whether harmfulness arises from distinguishable patterns in internal representations. Across both parameter and activation space, our findings are consistent: subspaces that amplify safe behaviors also amplify unsafe ones, and prompts with different safety implications activate overlapping representations. We find no evidence of a subspace that selectively governs safety. These results challenge the assumption that alignment is geometrically localized. Rather than residing in distinct directions, safety appears to emerge from entangled, high-impact components of the model's broader learning dynamics. This suggests that subspace-based defenses may face fundamental limitations and underscores the need for alternative strategies to preserve alignment under continued training. We corroborate these findings through multiple experiments on five open-source LLMs. Our code is publicly available at: https://github.com/CERT-Lab/safety-subspaces.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the geometric properties of safety alignment in Large Language Models (LLMs). It challenges the hypothesis that safety is encoded in specific, isolatable subspaces within the model's weight or activation space.
