Through comprehensive experiments on several open-source LLMs, the authors find that subspaces associated with safe behaviors also amplify unsafe ones, and that prompts with different safety implications activate overlapping internal representations. This suggests that safety is not geometrically localized but emerges from entangled, high-impact components of broader learning dynamics.  The paper concludes that subspace-based defenses against misalignment may face fundamental limitations, advocating for alternative alignment preservation strategies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's central claim, that safety alignment is not geometrically localized, is novel and directly challenges a growing body of work that seeks to identify and manipulate subspaces related to specific behaviors. The extensive empirical validation across multiple models enhances the credibility of this claim.

*   **Significance:** If the paper's conclusions hold, they have significant implications for the development of robust safety measures in LLMs. The finding that safety is entangled with general learning dynamics suggests that simple geometric interventions, such as projection or filtering, may be insufficient to preserve alignment under continued training. This calls for a re-evaluation of current subspace-based defense strategies and highlights the need for more sophisticated techniques.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Empirical Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough empirical study, including diverse experiments across different layers (weight, activation space), projection schemes, and a range of open-source LLMs. This rigorous approach strengthens the validity of the findings.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition and Methodology:** The paper clearly defines the problem of preserving safety alignment and presents a well-defined methodology for investigating the geometric properties of safety. The use of techniques like SVD and MSO is appropriate for analyzing high-dimensional data.
    *   **Challenging Existing Assumptions:** The paper directly challenges the prevailing assumption that alignment can be localized geometrically, which could stimulate new research directions.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited to Linear Subspaces:** The study focuses primarily on linear subspaces. While this is a reasonable starting point, it leaves open the possibility that safety could be encoded in more complex, non-linear geometric structures. This is acknowledged in the limitations section.
    *   **Model Size and Type:** While the study considers a diverse set of open-source models, the models are still relatively small compared to the largest proprietary LLMs. Generalizability to larger, more complex models is an open question.
    *   **Definitional Challenges:** The assessment of "utility" and "harmfulness" can be subjective.  While the paper uses established datasets, the specific metrics may not fully capture the nuances of safe and useful behavior.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to shift the focus of safety research towards more holistic, system-level approaches, rather than relying solely on geometric interventions. It may prompt researchers to explore alternative techniques, such as regularization, curriculum learning, or adversarial training, to preserve alignment during fine-tuning.

**Justification:** The paper offers a valuable, well-supported critique of the geometrically-localized safety alignment hypothesis. It provides a novel perspective and stimulates a re-evaluation of existing defensive strategies.  While limited to linear subspaces and smaller model sizes, its thorough methodology, consistent findings across multiple models, and clear articulation of the limitations contribute to its overall significance. While not representing a revolutionary breakthrough, its impact is substantive, likely influencing future research directions in safety and alignment.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unraveling Interwoven Roles of Large Language Models in Authorship Privacy: Obfuscation, Mimicking, and Verification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14195v1)
- **Authors**: Tuc Nguyen, Yifan Hu, Thai Le
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in large language models (LLMs) have been fueled by large scale training corpora drawn from diverse sources such as websites, news articles, and books. These datasets often contain explicit user information, such as person names and addresses, that LLMs may unintentionally reproduce in their generated outputs. Beyond such explicit content, LLMs can also leak identity revealing cues through implicit signals such as distinctive writing styles, raising significant concerns about authorship privacy. There are three major automated tasks in authorship privacy, namely authorship obfuscation (AO), authorship mimicking (AM), and authorship verification (AV). Prior research has studied AO, AM, and AV independently. However, their interplays remain under explored, which leaves a major research gap, especially in the era of LLMs, where they are profoundly shaping how we curate and share user generated content, and the distinction between machine generated and human authored text is also increasingly blurred. This work then presents the first unified framework for analyzing the dynamic relationships among LLM enabled AO, AM, and AV in the context of authorship privacy. We quantify how they interact with each other to transform human authored text, examining effects at a single point in time and iteratively over time. We also examine the role of demographic metadata, such as gender, academic background, in modulating their performances, inter-task dynamics, and privacy risks. All source code will be publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a unified framework to analyze the interwoven roles of Large Language Models (LLMs) in authorship privacy, specifically focusing on authorship obfuscation (AO), authorship mimicking (AM), and authorship verification (AV).  It moves beyond the isolated study of these tasks, examining their bidirectional effects and triplet-wise interdependencies. The authors empirically evaluate various commercial LLMs, revealing task-specific strengths, and analyze the influence of demographic metadata on these interactions. They demonstrate that obfuscation tends to outperform mimicking in interactive settings, disrupting authorial signals, but mimicking can partially reverse obfuscation over successive cycles.  The study also identifies how models with stronger reasoning abilities excel at verification and style concealment but are less effective at faithful style replication.  The authors ultimately look at longer chains of these effects to analyze potential long-term shifts in verification success and the linguistic distributions of human-generated text under AI transformations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its unified framework for analyzing the interplay between AO, AM, and AV in the context of LLMs. Prior research has largely treated these tasks in isolation, making this work a valuable contribution.  The exploration of pairwise and triplet-wise interdependencies, as well as iterative cycles of text transformation, represents a step forward.  However, the individual tasks (AO, AM, AV) themselves are not fundamentally new, and the LLM techniques applied are largely leveraging existing capabilities in a new configuration of tasks.  The finding that obfuscation and mimicking tend to be something of a tug-of-war effect is valuable.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a timely and important problem: authorship privacy in the age of powerful LLMs.  The increasing ability of LLMs to mimic and deanonymize writing styles raises serious concerns about privacy, particularly for vulnerable individuals like whistleblowers.  The paper's framework provides a valuable tool for understanding and mitigating these risks. The empirical findings offer practical insights for users and developers of LLMs. However, the practical impact remains somewhat limited by the scope of analysis (focusing on specific models and datasets) and the absence of human-in-the-loop evaluations.  While the automated metrics are useful, understanding the perceived naturalness and effectiveness of these transformations by human readers is critical for real-world impact. The analysis of topic drift over repeated transformations is also a useful, long-term assessment.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined framework for analyzing the interdependencies between AO, AM, and AV.
    *   Comprehensive empirical evaluation using multiple LLMs and datasets.
    *   Identification of task-specific strengths of different LLMs.
    *   Analysis of the influence of demographic metadata on authorship privacy.
    *   Exploration of iterative cycles of text transformation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Lack of human-centered evaluation to assess perceived naturalness and effectiveness.
    *   Limited generalizability due to reliance on specific LLMs and datasets.
    *   The practical implementation is not clear from the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on authorship privacy and LLMs. The unified framework could serve as a foundation for further investigations into the complex interactions between different AI-powered text transformation tasks. The empirical findings could inform the development of more robust privacy-preserving techniques and authorship detection tools. However, to have a more profound impact, future work should focus on addressing the identified limitations, such as incorporating human evaluations and expanding the scope of analysis to include a wider range of LLMs and real-world scenarios.

*   **Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper has good novelty in its approach and highlights a vital and evolving challenge, but the absence of human-centered evaluation and practical implications is a weakness. Given the timely relevance and potential for future work to build upon these findings, a score of 7 is reasonable. The paper provides a significant step towards understanding the multi-faceted nature of AI-driven authorship privacy.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Omnidirectional Reasoning with 360-R1: A Dataset, Benchmark, and GRPO-based Method
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14197v1)
- **Authors**: Xinshen Zhang, Zhen Ye, Xu Zheng
- **Abstract**: Omnidirectional images (ODIs), with their 360{\deg} field of view, provide unparalleled spatial awareness for immersive applications like augmented reality and embodied AI. However, the capability of existing multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) to comprehend and reason about such panoramic scenes remains underexplored. This paper addresses this gap by introducing OmniVQA, the first dataset and conducting the first benchmark for omnidirectional visual question answering. Our evaluation of state-of-the-art MLLMs reveals significant limitations in handling omnidirectional visual question answering, highlighting persistent challenges in object localization, feature extraction, and hallucination suppression within panoramic contexts. These results underscore the disconnect between current MLLM capabilities and the demands of omnidirectional visual understanding, which calls for dedicated architectural or training innovations tailored to 360{\deg} imagery. Building on the OmniVQA dataset and benchmark, we further introduce a rule-based reinforcement learning method, 360-R1, based on Qwen2.5-VL-Instruct. Concretely, we modify the group relative policy optimization (GRPO) by proposing three novel reward functions: (1) reasoning process similarity reward, (2) answer semantic accuracy reward, and (3) structured format compliance reward. Extensive experiments on our OmniVQA demonstrate the superiority of our proposed method in omnidirectional space (+6% improvement).
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Towards Omnidirectional Reasoning with 360-R1: A Dataset, Benchmark, and GRPO-based Method":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces OmniVQA, the first open-source dataset and benchmark for omnidirectional visual question answering (OVQA). The authors highlight the limitations of existing multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) in understanding and reasoning about 360° panoramic scenes. The OmniVQA dataset is built on equirectangular projection (ERP) panoramic images and focuses on tasks such as object identification, attribute analysis, and spatial relationship reasoning, particularly in polar regions, which are most susceptible to distortion. The authors also propose a rule-based reinforcement learning method called 360-R1, based on Qwen2.5-VL-Instruct, and modifies group relative policy optimization (GRPO) with novel reward functions tailored for 360° visual comprehension. Experiments demonstrate the superiority of their proposed method on the OmniVQA dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novel Dataset and Benchmark:** The introduction of OmniVQA fills a significant gap in the field by providing a dedicated resource for OVQA. The focus on challenging aspects of 360° imagery, such as polar distortion and spatial reasoning, makes it a valuable tool for advancing research in this area. This is especially useful considering the increasing interest in AR/VR and embodied AI.
    *   **Targeted Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing MLLMs in handling omnidirectional images. It specifically addresses the challenges of object recognition, attribute analysis, and spatial relationship reasoning in these images.
    *   **Methodological Contribution:** The proposed 360-R1 framework, with its structured reward functions and GRPO-based optimization, is a sound approach to improving the performance of MLLMs on OVQA. The use of specific rewards for reasoning consistency, answer accuracy, and structured format compliance is well-justified.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a thorough evaluation of state-of-the-art MLLMs on the OmniVQA benchmark, demonstrating the effectiveness of the 360-R1 method. The ablation studies provide further insights into the contributions of different components of the proposed framework.
    *   The paper thoroughly outlines the methodology, the dataset creation process, and the experimental setup, increasing the reproducibility and overall quality of the research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Scale:** While being the first open-source dataset for OVQA, OmniVQA's scale (approximately 4.8K QA pairs) is relatively small compared to other VQA datasets. This might limit the generalizability of the findings and the potential for training more powerful models.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The dataset is currently limited to indoor panoramic scenes. Expanding the dataset to include outdoor environments and more diverse scenarios would enhance its practical relevance.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The reliance on reinforcement learning with GRPO can be computationally expensive. The paper acknowledges this limitation but doesn't propose alternative, more efficient training strategies.
    *   **Dependence on Qwen:** The method is based on a specific MLLM (Qwen2.5-VL-Instruct), which could limit its broader applicability. It would be beneficial to explore the performance of 360-R1 with other MLLMs.
    * The datasets were privately held/unpublished and no code released for the benchmarks. These actions drastically reduce the potential impact to the community.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The paper makes a definite contribution by providing a novel dataset and benchmark for omnidirectional VQA. This significantly advances the field by focusing research toward a specific and challenging domain.
    *   The 360-R1 framework offers a practical approach to improving MLLM performance on OVQA, addressing the limitations of existing models and provides a solid base for future research.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The OmniVQA dataset and benchmark are likely to stimulate further research in OVQA, leading to the development of more sophisticated MLLMs capable of understanding and reasoning about panoramic scenes.
    *   The 360-R1 framework could be extended and adapted to other tasks involving omnidirectional images, such as scene understanding, object detection, and robot navigation.

*   **Rationale for Score:**

The paper introduces a valuable contribution by addressing a niche and underexplored area of research. The strengths of introducing the dataset and framework are evident, but the dataset is too small, too limited, the code isn't released, and the dataset is kept to the authors so it can be effectively used by only them.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Capturing the Effects of Quantization on Trojans in Code LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14200v1)
- **Authors**: Aftab Hussain, Sadegh AlMahdi Kazemi Zarkouei, Md Rafiqul Islam Rabin, Mohammad Amin Alipour, Sen Lin, Bowen Xu
- **Abstract**: Large language models of code exhibit high capability in performing diverse software engineering tasks, such as code translation, defect detection, text-to-code generation, and code summarization. While their ability to enhance developer productivity has spurred widespread use, these models have also seen substantial growth in size, often reaching billions of parameters. This scale demands efficient memory resource usage, prompting practitioners to use optimization techniques such as model quantization. Quantization uses smaller bit representations for the model parameters, reducing the precision of the weights. In this work, we investigate the impact of quantization on the risk of data poisoning attacks on these models, specifically examining whether it mitigates or exacerbates such vulnerabilities. We focus on two large language models, Meta's Llama-2-7b and CodeLlama-7b, applied to an SQL code generation task. Additionally, we introduce a new metric for measuring trojan signals in compromised models. We find that quantization has differing effects on code-generating LLMs: while reducing precision does not significantly alter Llama-2's behavior, it boosts performance and reduces attack success rates in CodeLlama, particularly at 4-bit precision.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of model quantization on data poisoning attacks (trojans) within large language models of code (Code LLMs).  It focuses on the text-to-SQL task using Meta's Llama-2-7b and CodeLlama-7b models. The authors examine the effects of quantization applied at two distinct dependency adjustment points (DAP): pre-finetuning and pre-inferencing. A novel metric, *payload signal strength*, is introduced to quantify the presence of "lurking trojans" - subtle trojan behaviors where models are nearing malicious outputs even without a direct trigger. The study finds that quantization has differing effects: while it doesn't significantly alter Llama-2's behavior, it *improves* performance and *reduces* attack success rates in Code Llama, particularly at 4-bit precision.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes several interesting contributions, but their level of novelty is arguably limited. While the application of quantization to mitigate trojans in Code LLMs is a reasonably unexplored area, existing research has examined quantization and general robustness, and similar techniques have been used on LLMs. The notion of "lurking trojans" is a sensible extension, but its novelty is incremental as it builds on existing trojan-centric research. The payload signal strength metric is also a sensible tool for trojan characterization, but can be thought as a specific realization of more general measures of how the model's internals activate in response to prompts. The key is that, while there is value in this work, the novel components build heavily upon prior work.

*   **Significance:** The findings regarding Code Llama's improved performance and reduced vulnerability to trojans with 4-bit quantization are potentially significant. This insight could influence model deployment strategies, suggesting that quantization isn't purely an optimization technique but can also offer a degree of security hardening. However, the inconsistent results between Llama-2 and Code Llama weakens this claim somewhat; this result requires additional validation. The introduced "payload signal strength" metric provides a useful tool for analyzing trojan effects and could potentially be used in future research. However, the real impact hinges on its ability to consistently identify subtle vulnerabilities that traditional ASR measures miss, and how easy it is to extend to other trojan contexts.
    The lack of comparison to other methods for identifying trojans also decreases the significance as it doesn't measure the added value that this metric offers compared to pre-existing metrics.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and research questions.
    *   Systematic experimental setup with multiple evaluation metrics.
    *   Introduction of a useful new metric, payload signal strength.
    *   Identifies an interesting observation about Code Llama's behavior with quantization.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited novelty in terms of core concepts.
    *   Lack of generalizability of Code Llama to the Llama-2 model diminishes the impact of claims.
    *   Findings are somewhat specific to the SQL code generation task and may not directly translate to other coding domains.
    *   The study focuses solely on *pre-inferencing* quantization, omitting potential insights from quantizing during training or finetuning.
    *   The experimental setting is also not well-motivated, as it doesn't offer any justification for quantizing only at inference time, which is not a typical scenario.
    *   Limited comparison to other trojan analysis or detection methods.
    *   Lack of justification of using 50% of poisoned data in fine-tuning.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence research in the areas of quantization, trojan detection, and security hardening of Code LLMs. The findings regarding Code Llama's vulnerability reduction may encourage practitioners to explore quantization as a defense mechanism. The payload signal strength metric could be adopted in other studies to analyze trojan effects. However, its long-term influence depends on the extent to which these insights can be generalized to other models, tasks, and attack scenarios.

*   **Missing details:**
    *   No code is shared with the paper, which makes it more difficult to verify the results of the experiments.
    *   The paper also lacks of a rigorous discussion about the limitations of the approach.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a well-executed empirical study with some interesting observations. The novelty is incremental, relying heavily on prior work. The results are potentially significant but require further validation and generalization. The introduced metric is useful, but its real-world impact is currently uncertain. Therefore, a score of 6 reflects its solid but not groundbreaking contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Challenges and Limitations in the Synthetic Generation of mHealth Sensor Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14206v1)
- **Authors**: Flavio Di Martino, Franca Delmastro
- **Abstract**: The widespread adoption of mobile sensors has the potential to provide massive and heterogeneous time series data, driving Artificial Intelligence applications in mHealth. However, data collection remains limited due to stringent ethical regulations, privacy concerns, and other constraints, hindering progress in the field. Synthetic data generation, particularly through Generative Adversarial Networks and Diffusion Models, has emerged as a promising solution to address both data scarcity and privacy issues. Yet, these models are often limited to short-term, unimodal signal patterns. This paper presents a systematic evaluation of state-of-the-art generative models for time series synthesis, with a focus on their ability to jointly handle multi-modality, long-range dependencies, and conditional generation-key challenges in the mHealth domain. To ensure a fair comparison, we introduce a novel evaluation framework designed to measure both the intrinsic quality of synthetic data and its utility in downstream predictive tasks. Our findings reveal critical limitations in the existing approaches, particularly in maintaining cross-modal consistency, preserving temporal coherence, and ensuring robust performance in train-on-synthetic, test-on-real, and data augmentation scenarios. Finally, we present our future research directions to enhance synthetic time series generation and improve the applicability of generative models in mHealth.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the challenges and limitations in generating synthetic mHealth sensor data using Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) and Diffusion Models (DDPMs). It systematically assesses state-of-the-art models for time series synthesis, focusing on their ability to handle multi-modality, long-range dependencies (LRD), and conditional generation – all critical aspects in mHealth. The authors propose a novel evaluation framework tailored for mHealth sensor data, considering both the intrinsic quality and utility of synthetic data in downstream predictive tasks. The study identifies limitations in existing approaches, particularly in maintaining cross-modal consistency, preserving temporal coherence, and achieving robust performance in various scenarios like train-on-synthetic/test-on-real.  Finally, the authors offer future research directions to enhance synthetic time series generation and improve the applicability of generative models in mHealth.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its comprehensive assessment of existing time series generative models, specifically targeting the unique constraints of mHealth sensor data. While GANs and Diffusion Models are widely explored, their application and evaluation in the context of complex, multimodal, long-range dependent, and conditionally generated mHealth data are relatively less studied. The proposed evaluation framework, explicitly designed for mHealth, is a valuable contribution, as is the systematic comparison of SoTA models under these specific conditions. The identification of key challenges like cross-modal consistency and temporal coherence is also insightful.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem in mHealth research: data scarcity due to privacy concerns and ethical regulations.  Overcoming this barrier is crucial for unlocking the full potential of AI in healthcare. The paper provides concrete insights into the limitations of current approaches, paving the way for the development of more effective synthetic data generation techniques. The evaluation framework itself can serve as a benchmark for future research. The systematic approach and detailed analysis make it a valuable resource for researchers in the field.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Scope:**  The paper tackles a well-defined and important problem.
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:**  The evaluation methodology is well-designed and rigorously applied.
    *   **Clear Identification of Limitations:**  The paper identifies specific weaknesses in existing models.
    *   **Practical Recommendations:** The authors provide concrete directions for future research.
    *   **mHealth focus:** The mHealth focus enhances the relevancy and utility of the research findings.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novel Model Development:**  The paper primarily focuses on evaluation and analysis rather than introducing completely new generative models.  While valuable, this limits the paper's potential impact.
    *   **Dependence on Existing Benchmarks:** It uses existing metrics for evaluation; while they develop a specific framework, they don’t create substantially novel metrics.
    *  **Check Point Selection method** The check point selection relies on generator loss instead of a true metric.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the direction of research in synthetic data generation for mHealth.  By identifying the limitations of current models and providing a robust evaluation framework, it can guide the development of more effective and reliable techniques.  The explicit focus on multi-modality, LRD, and conditional generation is particularly relevant for real-world applications.

*   **Justification of Score:** While the paper presents a very thorough analysis and evaluation, it does not introduce a breakthrough methodological advancement. The significant contribution lies in its systematic analysis and tailoring of existing techniques to a specific domain (mHealth), along with the insights gleaned from the experiments. For these reasons, the paper represents a solid, but not exceptional, advance in the field.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Automatic Dataset Generation for Knowledge Intensive Question Answering Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14212v1)
- **Authors**: Sizhe Yuen, Ting Su, Ziyang Wang, Yali Du, Adam J. Sobey
- **Abstract**: A question-answering (QA) system is to search suitable answers within a knowledge base. Current QA systems struggle with queries requiring complex reasoning or real-time knowledge integration. They are often supplemented with retrieval techniques on a data source such as Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). However, RAG continues to face challenges in handling complex reasoning and logical connections between multiple sources of information. A novel approach for enhancing Large Language Models (LLMs) in knowledge-intensive QA tasks is presented through the automated generation of context-based QA pairs. This methodology leverages LLMs to create fine-tuning data, reducing reliance on human labelling and improving model comprehension and reasoning capabilities. The proposed system includes an automated QA generator and a model fine-tuner, evaluated using perplexity, ROUGE, BLEU, and BERTScore. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate improvements in logical coherence and factual accuracy, with implications for developing adaptable Artificial Intelligence (AI) systems. Mistral-7b-v0.3 outperforms Llama-3-8b with BERT F1, BLEU, and ROUGE scores 0.858, 0.172, and 0.260 of for the LLM generated QA pairs compared to scores of 0.836, 0.083, and 0.139 for the human annotated QA pairs.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary of the paper followed by a critical evaluation of its novelty, significance, and a corresponding score.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach for improving the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in knowledge-intensive Question Answering (QA) tasks. The core idea is to automatically generate context-based QA pairs from external documents using an LLM, and then use these synthetically generated QA pairs to fine-tune another LLM. This approach aims to reduce the reliance on expensive and time-consuming human labeling. The paper details the automated QA generation process, which involves question generation, answer generation, and post-processing steps (to filter out irrelevant or poorly formed QA pairs). The framework is evaluated by fine-tuning two LLMs (Llama-3-8b and Mistral-7b-v0.3) on both the original and the synthetically generated datasets, and comparing the performance using various metrics like perplexity, ROUGE, BLEU, and BERTScore on the TechQA dataset. The results indicate that while fine-tuning on the original (human-labeled) dataset generally yields the best performance when the correct contextual document is provided, fine-tuning on the synthetic data can still improve performance compared to the baseline model. The performance of Mistral-7b-v0.3 is better than Llama-3-8b when evaluated on the synthetic QA pairs.
**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of using LLMs to generate QA pairs for fine-tuning other LLMs isn't entirely novel. Synthetic data generation for improving LLM performance has been explored in other contexts, such as for text classification, code generation and dialogue inpainting. However, the application of this approach to knowledge-intensive QA, particularly in a technical domain like IBM Technotes (TechQA dataset), and the specific QA generation and filtering methodology presented here, adds a layer of novelty. The authors introduce a reasonably well-defined pipeline encompassing question generation, answer generation with specific prompts and post processing steps for refining the QA pairs, which could be considered a key contribution.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work lies in reducing the cost and effort associated with creating labeled datasets for knowledge-intensive QA. If this approach can consistently generate high-quality synthetic data, it could democratize access to fine-tuning LLMs for specific domains, even when human-labeled data is scarce or expensive. The paper demonstrates the performance of automated fine-tuning approaches of LLMs that may be used in smaller scale applications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly describes the automated QA generation and fine-tuning process, making it relatively easy to understand and potentially replicate.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper uses a variety of metrics (perplexity, ROUGE, BLEU, BERTScore) to evaluate the performance of the fine-tuned models.
    *   **Relevance to Real-World Applications:** The focus on a technical QA dataset (TechQA) makes the work relevant to real-world applications where internal documentation needs to be processed.
    *   **Analysis of Context vs. No Context Results**: Provides useful insights into the limitations of each approach by separately evaluating models when context documents are and are not provided.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The experiments are limited to a single dataset (TechQA) and two LLMs (Llama-3-8b and Mistral-7b-v0.3).  It's unclear how well this approach would generalize to other datasets and larger models.
    *   **Quality of Synthetic Data:** While the paper includes post-processing steps, the quality of the synthetic QA pairs remains a concern.  The results show that fine-tuning on synthetic data doesn't always outperform the baseline model, indicating potential issues with the data quality. Further, no human evaluation is performed of the quality of the synthetically created data. The impact of the dataset size and diversity of synthetic QA pairs on fine-tuning performance has not been evaluated as well.
    *   **Lack of comparison with other data augmentation techniques**: The paper would benefit from comparing this approach with other data augmentation methods to highlight its potential advantages.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs for Data Generation:** The reliance on an LLM to generate the data introduces a potential bias and limits the diversity of the training data to the knowledge and reasoning ability of the LLM used for generation.
    *   **Missing comparison with Unsupervised Fine Tuning**: The approach for automatic dataset generation might be compared with unsupervised or self-supervised fine-tuning of LLMs for a more comprehensive benchmarking.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in the area of LLM fine-tuning and automated data generation. It provides a practical approach for reducing the cost of creating labeled data and could inspire others to explore more sophisticated QA generation and filtering techniques. However, its influence will depend on the successful replication and extension of this work by other researchers.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to an important problem (reducing the cost of labeled data for LLM fine-tuning). However, the limited scope of evaluation, concerns about the quality of the synthetic data, and lack of comparisons with unsupervised learning approaches reduce the paper's overall significance. I would rate it a **6** as the paper provides a clear methodology, is well written, and has the potential to spark additional research. However the lack of comparison with existing methodologies makes it difficult to completely assess the impact and contribution of this study.

**Score: 6**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### "Haet Bhasha aur Diskrimineshun": Phonetic Perturbations in Code-Mixed Hinglish to Red-Team LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14226v1)
- **Authors**: Darpan Aswal, Siddharth D Jaiswal
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have become increasingly powerful, with multilingual and multimodal capabilities improving by the day. These models are being evaluated through audits, alignment studies and red-teaming efforts to expose model vulnerabilities towards generating harmful, biased and unfair content. Existing red-teaming efforts have previously focused on the English language, using fixed template-based attacks; thus, models continue to be susceptible to multilingual jailbreaking strategies, especially in the multimodal context. In this study, we introduce a novel strategy that leverages code-mixing and phonetic perturbations to jailbreak LLMs for both text and image generation tasks. We also introduce two new jailbreak strategies that show higher effectiveness than baseline strategies. Our work presents a method to effectively bypass safety filters in LLMs while maintaining interpretability by applying phonetic misspellings to sensitive words in code-mixed prompts. Our novel prompts achieve a 99% Attack Success Rate for text generation and 78% for image generation, with Attack Relevance Rate of 100% for text generation and 95% for image generation when using the phonetically perturbed code-mixed prompts. Our interpretability experiments reveal that phonetic perturbations impact word tokenization, leading to jailbreak success. Our study motivates increasing the focus towards more generalizable safety alignment for multilingual multimodal models, especially in real-world settings wherein prompts can have misspelt words.
- **Summary**: This paper explores a novel jailbreaking strategy for Large Language Models (LLMs) and Multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) that combines code-mixing (mixing English and Hindi) and phonetic perturbations (misspellings that maintain pronunciation) of sensitive words in prompts. The authors introduce two new jailbreak templates ("Sandbox" and "VisGPT") and demonstrate that this approach effectively bypasses safety filters, achieving high attack success rates (up to 99% for text and 78% for image generation), even against state-of-the-art models like Llama 3 and ChatGPT 4o-mini. The study also examines the impact of phonetic perturbations on word tokenization and conducts interpretability experiments to understand why the approach is successful.  The results reveal that phonetic perturbations alter tokenization, impacting the ability of safety filters to identify sensitive words.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combined use of code-mixing, phonetic perturbations, and new jailbreak templates specifically targeted at multilingual LLMs and MLLMs. While previous research has explored code-mixing and adversarial attacks on LLMs, the synergistic combination and focus on phonetic perturbations in a multilingual context, especially for image generation, is relatively new. The introduction of "Sandbox" and "VisGPT" templates also adds incremental novelty. The exploration of tokenization-level impacts is a good effort.

**Significance:** The findings highlight a significant vulnerability in LLMs and MLLMs, particularly concerning their ability to handle real-world scenarios involving non-native speakers and misspelled words. This has practical implications for deployment and security of these models in diverse linguistic environments.  The paper's focus on both text and image generation also broadens the scope of the vulnerability assessment.  The study's conclusion that standard template-based defenses fail is an important contribution. However, a stronger justification would be to see how other sophisticated defense mechanisms work or fail. The interpretability analysis offers insights into why the attacks succeed at the tokenization level.

**Strengths:**
*   **Clearly defined research questions:**  The paper explicitly states its research questions, providing a clear roadmap for the investigation.
*   **Comprehensive experimental setup:**  The experiments are well-designed, covering a range of LLMs/MLLMs, datasets, jailbreak templates, and input formats.
*   **Quantitative and Qualitative Analysis:** The study presents both quantitative (AASR, AARR) and qualitative results (example generations, interpretability analysis).
*   **Interpretability analysis:** The attempt to explain *why* the attacks are effective is valuable and contributes to a deeper understanding of LLM vulnerabilities.
*   **Focus on a realistic scenario:** By focusing on code-mixing and phonetic perturbations, the paper addresses a vulnerability that is likely to be encountered in real-world usage.

**Weaknesses:**
*   **Limited Language Support:** The code-mixing is primarily limited to English and Hindi.  Extending this to more languages would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.  Lack of language variety makes generalization difficult.
*   **Manual Prompt Generation:** The manual creation of code-mixed and phonetically perturbed prompts limits the scalability of the approach, although the authors acknowledge this and discuss potential automation.
*   **Evaluation Metric Limitations:** While AARR is a reasonable metric, using GPT-40-mini as a judge, even for English versions, could introduce bias. A more robust evaluation, perhaps involving human evaluators for at least a subset of the results, could be done.
*   **Limited defense mechanisms attempted:** The paper focuses on baseline templates. If the authors had investigated a range of more advanced defense mechanisms the conclusions would be more rigorous.
*   **Lack of specific code-switching phenomena consideration:** Code-switching adheres to particular grammatical and sociolinguistic constraints, which are not addressed in the study. The authors do not consider or discuss this area in detail.

**Potential Influence:**  The paper's findings are likely to influence future research on LLM safety and alignment, particularly in multilingual and multimodal contexts. It motivates the development of more robust input tokenization strategies and safety filters that are less susceptible to phonetic perturbations and code-mixing. The paper's insights could inform the design of more generalizable red-teaming strategies.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel approach and demonstrates significant vulnerabilities in LLMs/MLLMs, its limitations in terms of language support, prompt generation, and the limited range of defense mechanisms tested temper its overall impact. The interpretability analysis, while insightful, could be more in-depth to fully understand the tokenization process.  Given these strengths and weaknesses, a score of 7 seems appropriate.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### UniVG-R1: Reasoning Guided Universal Visual Grounding with Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14231v1)
- **Authors**: Sule Bai, Mingxing Li, Yong Liu, Jing Tang, Haoji Zhang, Lei Sun, Xiangxiang Chu, Yansong Tang
- **Abstract**: Traditional visual grounding methods primarily focus on single-image scenarios with simple textual references. However, extending these methods to real-world scenarios that involve implicit and complex instructions, particularly in conjunction with multiple images, poses significant challenges, which is mainly due to the lack of advanced reasoning ability across diverse multi-modal contexts. In this work, we aim to address the more practical universal grounding task, and propose UniVG-R1, a reasoning guided multimodal large language model (MLLM) for universal visual grounding, which enhances reasoning capabilities through reinforcement learning (RL) combined with cold-start data. Specifically, we first construct a high-quality Chain-of-Thought (CoT) grounding dataset, annotated with detailed reasoning chains, to guide the model towards correct reasoning paths via supervised fine-tuning. Subsequently, we perform rule-based reinforcement learning to encourage the model to identify correct reasoning chains, thereby incentivizing its reasoning capabilities. In addition, we identify a difficulty bias arising from the prevalence of easy samples as RL training progresses, and we propose a difficulty-aware weight adjustment strategy to further strengthen the performance. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of UniVG-R1, which achieves state-of-the-art performance on MIG-Bench with a 9.1% improvement over the previous method. Furthermore, our model exhibits strong generalizability, achieving an average improvement of 23.4% in zero-shot performance across four image and video reasoning grounding benchmarks. The project page can be accessed at https://amap-ml.github.io/UniVG-R1-page/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "UniVG-R1: Reasoning Guided Universal Visual Grounding with Reinforcement Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UniVG-R1, a novel approach to universal visual grounding that aims to extend traditional methods beyond single-image scenarios with simple text references. UniVG-R1 tackles the challenge of complex, implicit instructions and multi-image contexts by incorporating a reasoning-guided multimodal large language model (MLLM) enhanced with reinforcement learning (RL).  The authors first construct a high-quality Chain-of-Thought (CoT) grounding dataset annotated with detailed reasoning chains for supervised fine-tuning. Subsequently, they employ rule-based reinforcement learning (GRPO) to encourage the model to identify correct reasoning chains. A difficulty-aware weight adjustment strategy is introduced to mitigate bias towards easier samples during RL training. The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on MIG-Bench and strong generalization in zero-shot settings across several image and video reasoning grounding benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Addressing a Relevant Problem:** The paper tackles a crucial limitation in existing visual grounding methods: the inability to handle complex, multi-image contexts and implicit instructions. This is a significant step toward real-world applicability.
    * **Well-Designed Approach:** The combination of CoT-based supervised fine-tuning and GRPO-based reinforcement learning is a sound strategy. The CoT dataset provides a strong initial knowledge base, while RL fine-tunes the model to select optimal reasoning paths.
    * **Novelty in RL Training:** Identifying and addressing the difficulty bias during RL training with the proposed weight adjustment strategy is a novel and important contribution.  It addresses a common issue in RL where the model prioritizes easy samples, hindering overall performance.
    * **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper presents compelling experimental results, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance on MIG-Bench and significant zero-shot generalization capabilities. The improvements over previous methods are substantial and well-documented.
    * **High-Quality Dataset:**  The construction of the high-quality CoT dataset is a significant contribution, enabling reasoning-based grounding.  The rigorous verification process ensures the dataset's reliability.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Pre-trained Models:**  The approach relies on a strong pre-trained MLLM (Qwen2-VL).  While leveraging pre-trained models is common, the specific dependence on Qwen2-VL might limit generalizability or adoption if alternative MLLMs become more prevalent. It would be beneficial to analyze how much is truly novel within the RL framework (the GRPO+difficulty weighting) versus inherited from the foundation model.
    * **Complexity:** While effective, the approach involves a complex training pipeline with multiple stages (CoT data generation, supervised fine-tuning, GRPO training, difficulty-aware weighting).  Simplification or distillation could be a valuable direction for future research.
    * **Limited Ablation:** While the paper includes some ablation studies, it would be more insightful to see a more granular breakdown. For example, separate ablations showing just the impact of the CoT dataset vs. solely RL, or different difficulty weighting strategies in the RL stage would be useful.
    * **Evaluation Metrics:** While standard, the exclusive use of Acc@0.5 may not fully capture the nuances of grounding accuracy. Examining Intersection over Union (IoU) at different thresholds could provide a more comprehensive assessment.
    * **Limited Scope of Difficulty Bias Analysis:** The difficulty bias analysis is limited to a single metric (mIoU). A more detailed analysis considering other potential factors impacting sample difficulty would strengthen this aspect.

* **Significance and Potential Impact:**

The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of visual grounding.  By addressing the limitations of existing methods and introducing a robust reasoning-guided approach, UniVG-R1 paves the way for more practical and versatile applications in real-world scenarios. The CoT dataset and the difficulty-aware RL training strategy offer valuable resources and insights for future research in this area.  The demonstrated zero-shot generalization is particularly promising, suggesting that the model can be readily applied to new tasks and domains.

* **Novelty:** The overall approach and particularly the difficulty-aware weighting are the most novel aspects. Constructing a CoT dataset is useful but a fairly standard procedure now.
* **Rigor:** Methodological description is strong, and the quantitative analysis is convincing.
* **Reproducibility:** The project page availability increases confidence in reproducibility and further use by the research community.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of visual grounding by tackling the challenge of complex, multi-image contexts. The approach is well-designed, and the experimental results are strong. The novelty primarily lies in the difficulty-aware RL training strategy and its application to universal visual grounding. While the reliance on a pre-trained MLLM and the complexity of the pipeline are minor weaknesses, the overall significance of the paper warrants a score of 7.5. The paper builds upon existing techniques such as CoT, GRPO, and pre-trained MLLMs. The main novelty lies in carefully integrating these elements and adding a difficulty weighting scheme into a well-justified training paradigm for universal visual grounding, leading to strong empirical results. It doesn't fundamentally redefine the field, but is a substantial and impactful advance.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### ABBA: Highly Expressive Hadamard Product Adaptation for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14238v1)
- **Authors**: Raghav Singhal, Kaustubh Ponkshe, Rohit Vartak, Praneeth Vepakomma
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models have demonstrated strong performance across a wide range of tasks, but adapting them efficiently to new domains remains a key challenge. Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) methods address this by introducing lightweight, trainable modules while keeping most pre-trained weights fixed. The prevailing approach, LoRA, models updates using a low-rank decomposition, but its expressivity is inherently constrained by the rank. Recent methods like HiRA aim to increase expressivity by incorporating a Hadamard product with the frozen weights, but still rely on the structure of the pre-trained model. We introduce ABBA, a new PEFT architecture that reparameterizes the update as a Hadamard product of two independently learnable low-rank matrices. In contrast to prior work, ABBA fully decouples the update from the pre-trained weights, enabling both components to be optimized freely. This leads to significantly higher expressivity under the same parameter budget. We formally analyze ABBA's expressive capacity and validate its advantages through matrix reconstruction experiments. Empirically, ABBA achieves state-of-the-art results on arithmetic and commonsense reasoning benchmarks, consistently outperforming existing PEFT methods by a significant margin across multiple models. Our code is publicly available at: https://github.com/CERT-Lab/abba.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ABBA, a new Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) method for Large Language Models (LLMs). ABBA reparameterizes the weight update as a Hadamard product of two independently learnable low-rank matrices. This approach aims to overcome the expressivity limitations of existing methods like LoRA, which models updates directly as low-rank matrices. By decoupling the update from the pre-trained weights (unlike HiRA), ABBA allows for more flexible optimization and improved expressivity under the same parameter budget. The authors provide theoretical analysis, matrix reconstruction experiments, and empirical validation on arithmetic and commonsense reasoning benchmarks, demonstrating that ABBA achieves state-of-the-art results compared to other PEFT methods. They also address memory efficiency by using Khatri-Rao factorization and present a method for initializing ABBA adapters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using a Hadamard product of two *independently* learned low-rank matrices for PEFT is novel. While HiRA also employs a Hadamard product, it multiplies a low-rank matrix with the pre-trained weights, which limits its expressiveness. ABBA's decoupling from the original weights is a key differentiating factor. The specific Khatri-Rao factorization for memory efficiency, while leveraging existing knowledge, is well-integrated into the method and contributes to its practical viability. The derivation of the scaling factor is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve the performance of PEFT methods while maintaining parameter efficiency. If ABBA consistently outperforms LoRA and its variants across various tasks and models, it could become a preferred method for adapting LLMs to new domains. The gains shown on arithmetic and commonsense reasoning tasks are promising.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a strong theoretical foundation, including an analysis of ABBA's expressive capacity and a derivation of the appropriate scaling factor.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results on several benchmarks and models are compelling. ABBA consistently outperforms other PEFT methods. The ablation studies (e.g., initialization strategies, rank selection) are valuable.
    *   **Practical Considerations:** The paper addresses practical issues such as memory efficiency and inference costs. The Khatri-Rao factorization is a key contribution in this regard. The explanation of initialization failures is also valuable.
    *   **Clear Writing and Organization:** The paper is well-written and organized, making it easy to understand the proposed method and its advantages.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Tasks:** While the paper presents strong results on arithmetic and commonsense reasoning, it would benefit from evaluations on a broader range of NLP tasks, including those that are known to be more challenging for PEFT methods (e.g., tasks requiring significant linguistic understanding or generation).
    *   **Ablation of Component Combinations:** While many components are ablated and analyzed individually (SVD init of just one adapter pair etc.), a complete traversal of the design space involving all combinations of init- and scaling strategies could provide more exhaustive insights.

    *   **Overfitting potential** Although the paper is great and results are encouraging, there needs to be a discussion (backed with data) on potential overfitting. Even if the results on the paper are good, it is important to discuss if there's a trade-off between increased expressive capacity and generalizability. How does performance vary across domains?

    *   **Lack of Comparative Inference Speed Metrics:** The paper claims efficient inference, but lacks explicit metrics comparing it to LoRA. An ablation on this aspect helps further strengthen the claimed efficiency.

*   **Potential Impact:** ABBA has the potential to become a widely used PEFT method if it continues to demonstrate superior performance across various tasks and models. The theoretical analysis and practical considerations in the paper make it a valuable contribution to the field.
*   **Justification of score:** ABBA makes an important contribution to PEFT methods by introducing the idea of hadamard structure using indepenently learned low rank matrices. The theoretical, practical and empirical studies further strengthen the claims. The paper could benefit from more discussion on overfitting potential. and more comparative experiments. The lack of such comprehensive evaluations justifies scoring the paper at 8, with the potential for it to be very influential in PEFT.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### TransBench: Benchmarking Machine Translation for Industrial-Scale Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14244v1)
- **Authors**: Haijun Li, Tianqi Shi, Zifu Shang, Yuxuan Han, Xueyu Zhao, Hao Wang, Yu Qian, Zhiqiang Qian, Linlong Xu, Minghao Wu, Chenyang Lyu, Longyue Wang, Gongbo Tang, Weihua Luo, Zhao Xu, Kaifu Zhang
- **Abstract**: Machine translation (MT) has become indispensable for cross-border communication in globalized industries like e-commerce, finance, and legal services, with recent advancements in large language models (LLMs) significantly enhancing translation quality. However, applying general-purpose MT models to industrial scenarios reveals critical limitations due to domain-specific terminology, cultural nuances, and stylistic conventions absent in generic benchmarks. Existing evaluation frameworks inadequately assess performance in specialized contexts, creating a gap between academic benchmarks and real-world efficacy. To address this, we propose a three-level translation capability framework: (1) Basic Linguistic Competence, (2) Domain-Specific Proficiency, and (3) Cultural Adaptation, emphasizing the need for holistic evaluation across these dimensions. We introduce TransBench, a benchmark tailored for industrial MT, initially targeting international e-commerce with 17,000 professionally translated sentences spanning 4 main scenarios and 33 language pairs. TransBench integrates traditional metrics (BLEU, TER) with Marco-MOS, a domain-specific evaluation model, and provides guidelines for reproducible benchmark construction. Our contributions include: (1) a structured framework for industrial MT evaluation, (2) the first publicly available benchmark for e-commerce translation, (3) novel metrics probing multi-level translation quality, and (4) open-sourced evaluation tools. This work bridges the evaluation gap, enabling researchers and practitioners to systematically assess and enhance MT systems for industry-specific needs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TransBench, a new benchmark designed for evaluating machine translation (MT) systems specifically in industrial-scale applications. It argues that existing benchmarks are inadequate for assessing the nuanced requirements of real-world scenarios, particularly in domains like e-commerce, finance, and legal services. TransBench proposes a three-level framework for translation capability: Basic Linguistic Competence, Domain-Specific Proficiency, and Cultural Adaptation. The benchmark itself comprises 17,000 professionally translated sentences across 4 e-commerce scenarios and 33 language pairs, along with a suite of evaluation metrics including traditional metrics and a domain-specific evaluation model called Marco-MOS. The paper details the data construction process, including data cleaning, annotation, and specialized processing for sensitive information. The authors contribute a structured framework for MT evaluation, a publicly available e-commerce translation benchmark, novel metrics, and open-sourced evaluation tools.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The main novelty lies in the creation of a benchmark specifically tailored for industrial applications, particularly focusing on e-commerce translation. While domain-specific benchmarks exist, the explicit focus on commercial scenarios, the three-level capability framework, and the multi-faceted dataset construction with emphasis on cultural adaptation are differentiating factors.  The Marco-MOS model, a translation quality assessment model for e-commerce, also represents a novel contribution. The explicit emphasis on the three interconnected levels of Basic Linguistic Competence, Domain-Specific Proficiency, and Cultural Adaptation, provides a structured means to analyze translation challenges that are not traditionally broken down in existing benchmarks.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical gap in the MT evaluation landscape. General-purpose MT models often fail to capture the specific requirements of industrial applications, leading to a disconnect between academic performance and real-world effectiveness. TransBench provides a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners to develop and evaluate MT systems tailored for commercial contexts. The open-sourcing of the benchmark and evaluation tools encourages further research and development in this area. The detailed data collection and annotation process highlights the challenges in creating high-quality, domain-specific datasets.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined Framework:** The three-level translation capability framework provides a clear and structured way to understand and evaluate MT performance in industrial settings.
    * **Practical Relevance:** The focus on real-world e-commerce scenarios makes the benchmark highly relevant to practitioners.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation Suite:** The inclusion of traditional metrics, domain-specific evaluation models, and hallucination detection methods provides a thorough assessment of MT quality.
    * **Open-Source Availability:** The public release of the benchmark and evaluation tools promotes reproducibility and further research.
    * **Detailed Data Construction:**  The paper meticulously describes the data collection, cleaning, annotation, and ethical considerations, ensuring a high-quality dataset.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Domain Coverage:** While the initial focus is on e-commerce, the framework could be extended to other industrial domains like finance and law, as initially planned by the authors.
    * **Marco-MOS Dependence:** The reliance on Marco-MOS for scenario adaptability assessment means that the effectiveness of this evaluation relies on its performance. Independent validation is necessary.
    * **Scalability:**  The manual annotation and specialized processing steps can be expensive and time-consuming, potentially limiting the scalability of the benchmark to other domains or languages.
    * **Cultural Adaptation Quantification:**  While highlighting cultural adaptation, the paper's metrics in this area ("accuracy") seem simplistic. Developing more nuanced metrics that capture the subtleties of cultural adaptation would improve the benchmark.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development of MT systems for industrial applications. It can drive research towards domain-specific adaptation, cultural sensitivity, and the creation of more robust and reliable translation models. The benchmark can also serve as a valuable tool for evaluating the performance of commercial MT services.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper provides a substantial contribution to the field by addressing a significant gap in MT evaluation and providing a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners. The rigorous data collection, novel framework, and comprehensive evaluation metrics are strong points. However, the limited domain coverage, potential dependence on Marco-MOS, and less sophisticated cultural adaptation metrics are weaknesses that prevent it from achieving a higher score.

### Instructing Text-to-Image Diffusion Models via Classifier-Guided Semantic Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14254v1)
- **Authors**: Yuanyuan Chang, Yinghua Yao, Tao Qin, Mengmeng Wang, Ivor Tsang, Guang Dai
- **Abstract**: Text-to-image diffusion models have emerged as powerful tools for high-quality image generation and editing. Many existing approaches rely on text prompts as editing guidance. However, these methods are constrained by the need for manual prompt crafting, which can be time-consuming, introduce irrelevant details, and significantly limit editing performance. In this work, we propose optimizing semantic embeddings guided by attribute classifiers to steer text-to-image models toward desired edits, without relying on text prompts or requiring any training or fine-tuning of the diffusion model. We utilize classifiers to learn precise semantic embeddings at the dataset level. The learned embeddings are theoretically justified as the optimal representation of attribute semantics, enabling disentangled and accurate edits. Experiments further demonstrate that our method achieves high levels of disentanglement and strong generalization across different domains of data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel method, Classifier-Guided Semantic Optimization (CASO), to edit images generated by text-to-image diffusion models like Stable Diffusion. Unlike existing approaches that rely on carefully crafted text prompts, CASO optimizes continuous semantic embeddings using gradients from attribute classifiers. This allows for more precise and disentangled edits without requiring fine-tuning or retraining the diffusion model. The method leverages classifiers to learn semantic embeddings at the dataset level, demonstrating strong generalization and edit quality across various data domains. The core idea is to use a classifier to guide the latent space exploration, ensuring the edit reflects the semantic intent without unintended side effects.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using a classifier to guide the semantic optimization in the latent space of a diffusion model is innovative. It addresses a significant limitation of text-prompt based editing, where prompt engineering becomes a bottleneck. The theoretical justification connecting the learned embeddings to attribute class means adds depth and supports the method's effectiveness. The method's disentanglement capabilities, avoiding unintended changes, and its generalizability across different data domains constitute significant advances. Also, the single-step edit and interpolation experiment demonstrate the effectiveness and controllability of CASO.

*   **Significance:** Diffusion models have revolutionized image generation and editing, but their controllability remains a challenge. CASO offers a practical solution to refine the editing process, providing fine-grained control over specific attributes. The ability to perform edits without relying on text prompts opens up new possibilities for users who may lack expertise in prompt engineering. If CASO proves robust and scalable, it could become a valuable tool for image editing and manipulation, benefiting various applications such as content creation, artistic expression, and data augmentation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Disentanglement:** Demonstrates a better control over the edited attributes without altering other unrelated parts of the image.
    *   **Generalization:** Showcases superior performance across different data domains and image styles, including real-world images, anime-style faces, and object manipulations.
    *   **Efficiency:**  It does not require fine-tuning the diffusion model, making it computationally efficient.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:**  The link between the learned semantic embeddings and the attribute class mean is well-justified.
    *   **Fine-grained control:** Enables users to implement fine-grained editing and bidirectional editing.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on Classifiers:** The performance of CASO hinges on the quality of the attribute classifiers.  The method is limited to attributes for which reliable classifiers exist. The method requires careful engineering and well-trained classifiers to perform optimally.
    *   **Potential for Misuse:** As with any image editing tool, CASO could be used for malicious purposes such as creating deepfakes or manipulating images to spread misinformation. While the paper mentions responsible use, it doesn't provide concrete mechanisms to prevent misuse.
    *   **Computational overhead for training the Classifier:** Despite not requiring fine-tuning the diffusion model, CASO requires training an external classifier, which requires computational resources and large amounts of labelled data.

*   **Potential Influence:** CASO could influence future research in controllable image generation, pushing towards more direct and interpretable editing techniques. It also highlights the potential of combining discriminative (classifiers) and generative (diffusion models) approaches to achieve more powerful results. Future work could explore self-supervised or weakly supervised learning techniques to reduce reliance on labeled data for training classifiers.

*   **Rigor:** The experiments are well-designed and the results are presented clearly. The comparisons with existing methods are comprehensive. The ablation studies help to understand the contribution of each component of the proposed method.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of controllable image generation, introducing an effective and versatile method for editing images generated by diffusion models. While the reliance on classifiers and the potential for misuse are limitations, the strengths in disentanglement, generalization, and efficiency outweigh these concerns.  The theoretical justification and rigorous experimental validation further solidify the paper's merit. The approach represents a meaningful step toward more direct and controllable image editing and its potential to inspire new research directions. Therefore, a score of '8' is justified.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### FuxiMT: Sparsifying Large Language Models for Chinese-Centric Multilingual Machine Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14256v1)
- **Authors**: Shaolin Zhu, Tianyu Dong, Bo Li, Deyi Xiong
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we present FuxiMT, a novel Chinese-centric multilingual machine translation model powered by a sparsified large language model (LLM). We adopt a two-stage strategy to train FuxiMT. We first pre-train the model on a massive Chinese corpus and then conduct multilingual fine-tuning on a large parallel dataset encompassing 65 languages. FuxiMT incorporates Mixture-of-Experts (MoEs) and employs a curriculum learning strategy for robust performance across various resource levels. Experimental results demonstrate that FuxiMT significantly outperforms strong baselines, including state-of-the-art LLMs and machine translation models, particularly under low-resource scenarios. Furthermore, FuxiMT exhibits remarkable zero-shot translation capabilities for unseen language pairs, indicating its potential to bridge communication gaps where parallel data are scarce or unavailable.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FuxiMT, a Chinese-centric multilingual machine translation model built upon a sparsified Large Language Model (LLM). It employs a two-stage training process: first, pre-training on a massive Chinese corpus to instill deep Chinese language understanding, and second, multilingual fine-tuning on a large parallel corpus covering 65 languages. FuxiMT incorporates a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture for efficient computation and curriculum learning to handle diverse resource levels robustly. Experimental results demonstrate that FuxiMT outperforms strong baselines (including LLMs and traditional MT models), particularly in low-resource scenarios, and exhibits notable zero-shot translation capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Chinese-Centric Approach:** The paper explicitly addresses the need for more Chinese-centric multilingual MT, distinguishing it from the predominantly English-focused research. This is a practical and socially impactful contribution.
    *   **MoE Integration:** The use of MoEs within the BLOOMz architecture is a well-justified design choice. This choice enables scaling to a larger language corpus while dynamically routing inputs to specialized experts, increasing efficiency and potentially mitigating catastrophic forgetting.
    *   **Two-Stage Training Strategy:** The combination of Chinese pre-training and multilingual fine-tuning is a sound strategy. Pre-training ensures strong Chinese language understanding, while fine-tuning adapts the model to multilingual translation. The curriculum learning approach in the fine-tuning stage is also well-reasoned.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents substantial experimental evidence demonstrating that FuxiMT outperforms strong baselines across varying resource levels, with a particularly strong showing in low-resource and zero-shot scenarios. Ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of different components.
    *   **Practical Application:** The paper presents clear advantages over existing LLMs and MT models by focusing on a broad language coverage. Its capacity for low-resource and zero-shot translation suggests it can bridge communication gaps where resources are limited.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:** While the combination of techniques (sparsification, pre-training, MoE, curriculum learning) is strong, none of these elements are fundamentally new. The novelty primarily lies in the specific configuration and application to the Chinese-centric multilingual translation problem.
    *   **Dataset Details:** While the paper describes dataset collection and filtering processes, it could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the data. Specifically, more details on the types of content present in the Chinese datasets and on the quality assessment methodologies would be helpful.
    *   **Limited Qualitative Analysis:** The paper primarily relies on quantitative metrics (BLEU, CHRF). Adding a qualitative analysis showcasing specific translation examples, particularly for challenging cases, would further strengthen the evaluation.
    *   **MoE Implementation Details:** While the use of MoEs is described, the paper could benefit from more details regarding expert diversity, load balancing, and routing strategies to better understand the effectiveness of the MoE component.
*   **Significance and Influence:**

    *   The paper highlights the potential of combining existing techniques into an effective and specialized system. Its findings could influence future research in multilingual MT, particularly towards resource-efficient models and strategies tailored to specific language families.
    *   The emphasis on Chinese-centric MT makes this paper relevant and applicable to a significant segment of the world's population. Its zero-shot translation capabilities provide potential for improving cross-cultural communication and fostering inclusivity.
    *   The study demonstrates a concrete application of MoEs to multilingual MT and shows how they can be adapted for resource-efficient training, potentially paving the way for using MoEs with existing LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

While FuxiMT presents a practical, socially beneficial, and empirically supported approach to Chinese-centric multilingual MT, the core novelty is somewhat incremental. The paper combines and optimizes existing techniques rather than introducing fundamentally new ones. The clear strengths in low-resource performance, the well-reasoned design choices, and the demonstration of real-world applicability weigh heavily in its favor. Therefore, it warrants a positive rating that reflects these contributions, despite its lack of substantial innovation.
