### Speculative Decoding Reimagined for Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14260v1)
- **Authors**: Luxi Lin, Zhihang Lin, Zhanpeng Zeng, Rongrong Ji
- **Abstract**: This paper introduces Multimodal Speculative Decoding (MSD) to accelerate Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) inference. Speculative decoding has been shown to accelerate Large Language Models (LLMs) without sacrificing accuracy. However, current speculative decoding methods for MLLMs fail to achieve the same speedup as they do for LLMs. To address this, we reimagine speculative decoding specifically for MLLMs. Our analysis of MLLM characteristics reveals two key design principles for MSD: (1) Text and visual tokens have fundamentally different characteristics and need to be processed separately during drafting. (2) Both language modeling ability and visual perception capability are crucial for the draft model. For the first principle, MSD decouples text and visual tokens in the draft model, allowing each to be handled based on its own characteristics. For the second principle, MSD uses a two-stage training strategy: In stage one, the draft model is trained on text-only instruction-tuning datasets to improve its language modeling ability. In stage two, MSD gradually introduces multimodal data to enhance the visual perception capability of the draft model. Experiments show that MSD boosts inference speed by up to $2.29\times$ for LLaVA-1.5-7B and up to $2.46\times$ for LLaVA-1.5-13B on multimodal benchmarks, demonstrating its effectiveness. Our code is available at https://github.com/Lyn-Lucy/MSD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Multimodal Speculative Decoding (MSD), a novel approach to accelerate the inference speed of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). MSD addresses the limitations of directly applying speculative decoding techniques (originally designed for LLMs) to MLLMs. The core idea is based on two key insights: (1) Text and visual tokens have distinct characteristics and should be processed separately during drafting and (2) both language modeling and visual perception are crucial for the draft model. To this end, MSD decouples text and visual token processing and employs a two-stage training strategy. The first stage focuses on text-only instruction tuning to enhance language modeling, while the second stage gradually introduces multimodal data to improve visual perception. Experiments on various multimodal benchmarks show that MSD significantly boosts inference speed compared to baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses an Important Problem:** Efficient inference is a critical bottleneck for MLLMs, hindering their real-world applicability. The paper tackles this directly.
    *   **Insightful Analysis:** The paper presents a solid analysis of the differences between LLMs and MLLMs, leading to the two key design principles for MSD. The observations regarding the different nature of visual and text tokens are well-justified.
    *   **Principled Design:** The proposed MSD method is well-motivated by the analysis and the two design principles. Decoupling token processing and using a two-stage training strategy are logical and effective solutions.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper includes thorough experiments on various multimodal benchmarks with different MLLMs. Ablation studies are conducted to validate the contributions of individual components.
    *   **Performance Gains:** The reported speedups are significant (up to 2.46x), demonstrating the practical benefits of MSD.
    *   **Addresses limitations of prior works:** The paper clearly highlights the inadequacy of existing SD methods on MLLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of the ideas is novel, the individual components (feature level speculative decoding, separate token processing) are known from other contexts.
    *   **Limited Scalability Evaluation:** The experiments are only performed on LLaVA-1.5-7B and LLaVA-1.5-13B. Although these are widely used models, the performance of MSD on larger models (e.g., 34B or 70B parameters) is not explored.
    *   **Dependency on Specific Architecture:** The method is presented with LLaVA as an example. However, the results may vary on other MLLM architectures.
    *   **Unclear Generalizability to Other Modalities:** The paper primarily focuses on visual and text modalities. The generalizability of MSD to other modalities, like audio or video, is not discussed and tested.
    *   **Modest novelty in specific algorithm:** The algorithm has a couple of changes in input tokens and training strategy. Although the changes are not that hard, the analysis is very important.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper provides a practical and effective solution to accelerate MLLM inference, potentially widening their adoption in real-world applications.
    *   The analysis and insights regarding the differences between LLMs and MLLMs contribute to a deeper understanding of these models and can inform future research.
    *   The two-stage training approach can be a generalizable strategy for other multimodal learning scenarios.

**Justification of Score:**

MSD provides an effective, well-reasoned approach for lossless MLLM acceleration. While there isn't revolutionary algorithm breakthrough in MSD, the key is that it is carefully designed and implemented based on a very insightful observation about MLLMs. The comprehensive experiments validate its effectiveness. While there are limitations in terms of scalability evaluation and modality generalizability, the paper's contributions are significant. Therefore, the following aspects are considered: insight of observations, practicality and effectiveness.
The improvements are not groundbreaking.

### AAPO: Enhance the Reasoning Capabilities of LLMs with Advantage Momentum
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14264v1)
- **Authors**: Jian Xiong, Jingbo Zhou, Jingyong Ye, Dejing Dou
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as an effective approach for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs), especially in scenarios where supervised fine-tuning (SFT) falls short due to limited chain-of-thought (CoT) data. Among RL-based post-training methods, group relative advantage estimation, as exemplified by Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), has attracted considerable attention for eliminating the dependency on the value model, thereby simplifying training compared to traditional approaches like Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO). However, we observe that exsiting group relative advantage estimation method still suffers from training inefficiencies, particularly when the estimated advantage approaches zero. To address this limitation, we propose Advantage-Augmented Policy Optimization (AAPO), a novel RL algorithm that optimizes the cross-entropy (CE) loss using advantages enhanced through a momentum-based estimation scheme. This approach effectively mitigates the inefficiencies associated with group relative advantage estimation. Experimental results on multiple mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate the superior performance of AAPO.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AAPO: Enhance the Reasoning Capabilities of LLMs with Advantage Momentum":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Advantage-Augmented Policy Optimization (AAPO), a novel reinforcement learning (RL) algorithm for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). AAPO addresses limitations in existing group relative advantage estimation methods, particularly their inefficiency when estimated advantages approach zero. The algorithm optimizes the cross-entropy loss using advantages enhanced by a momentum-based estimation scheme.  This mitigates the inefficiencies of group relative advantage estimation. Experimental results on mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate AAPO's superior performance compared to baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Identification:** The paper clearly identifies a real problem with existing group relative advantage estimation methods: vanishing gradients when reward variance within a group is low, leading to training inefficiencies.
    *   **Technical Novelty:** AAPO introduces a novel concept of "advantage momentum," which incorporates a reference gradient into the advantage estimation process. This is a reasonably intuitive and potentially effective way to address the identified problem.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides experimental results on multiple mathematical reasoning benchmarks, demonstrating that AAPO outperforms established methods like GRPO and GPG. This provides empirical evidence supporting the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation study provides some evidence for the importance of the clip operation of the algorithm.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the idea of "advantage momentum" is new, it builds heavily upon existing techniques in RL and group relative advantage estimation.  The core idea – adding a form of regularization or comparison with a fixed model – is not entirely groundbreaking.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a high-level intuition for why AAPO should work, but it lacks a formal theoretical analysis guaranteeing convergence, stability, or other desirable properties. There is some discussion in section 5.1 and 5.2, but it is not fully rigorous.
    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The experiments are limited to mathematical reasoning benchmarks.  It's unclear whether AAPO would generalize well to other types of reasoning tasks or to non-reasoning tasks.
    *   **Dependency on Hyperparameter Tuning:** The clip range are fixed, but it may not result in the same performance with different tasks or models. The paper did not provide any specific guidelines on how to tune these hyperparameters, which could impact its usability in practice.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper contributes to the growing body of research on using RL to improve LLM reasoning.
    *   The identified problem with group relative advantage estimation is important, and AAPO offers a practical solution.
    *   The experimental results are convincing and suggest that AAPO could be a valuable tool for training LLMs for specific reasoning tasks.

*   **Novelty:** The main contribution, the "advantage momentum" technique, is a useful refinement of existing group relative advantage estimation methods. It can be considered more as an *improvement* instead of a *breakthrough.*

**Justification of Score:**

The paper demonstrates clear empirical gains over existing approaches for RL finetuning LLMs for mathematical reasoning, specifically addressing limitations within group relative advantage estimation. The "advantage momentum" concept offers a practical, albeit incremental, advancement in advantage estimation. However, the novelty is limited, it lacks rigorous theoretical validation, and the scope of evaluation is relatively narrow. As such, while the paper is a valuable contribution to the field, it doesn't warrant a very high score.

### Think-J: Learning to Think for Generative LLM-as-a-Judge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14268v1)
- **Authors**: Hui Huang, Yancheng He, Hongli Zhou, Rui Zhang, Wei Liu, Weixun Wang, Wenbo Su, Bo Zheng, Jiaheng Liu
- **Abstract**: LLM-as-a-Judge refers to the automatic modeling of preferences for responses generated by Large Language Models (LLMs), which is of significant importance for both LLM evaluation and reward modeling. Although generative LLMs have made substantial progress in various tasks, their performance as LLM-Judge still falls short of expectations. In this work, we propose Think-J, which improves generative LLM-as-a-Judge by learning how to think. We first utilized a small amount of curated data to develop the model with initial judgment thinking capabilities. Subsequently, we optimize the judgment thinking traces based on reinforcement learning (RL). We propose two methods for judgment thinking optimization, based on offline and online RL, respectively. The offline RL requires training a critic model to construct positive and negative examples for learning. The online method defines rule-based reward as feedback for optimization. Experimental results showed that our approach can significantly enhance the evaluation capability of generative LLM-Judge, surpassing both generative and classifier-based LLM-Judge without requiring extra human annotations.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

The paper introduces "Think-J," a novel approach to improve generative Large Language Models (LLMs) as judges for evaluating response quality. The method focuses on enhancing the judgment thinking capabilities of LLMs. It utilizes a small amount of curated data to initialize judgment thinking capabilities and then optimizes judgment thinking traces based on Reinforcement Learning (RL). Two optimization methods are proposed: Critic-guided Offline Learning (using a critic model to generate positive and negative examples) and Rule-based Online Learning (using rule-based rewards for optimization). The experimental results demonstrate that Think-J significantly enhances the evaluation capability of generative LLMs, outperforming both generative and classifier-based LLM-Judges without requiring extra human annotations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents an interesting approach to improving LLMs as judges. The core idea of explicitly learning and optimizing judgment thinking traces is novel and addresses a limitation of existing methods that either rely on direct prompting or supervised finetuning without intermediate reasoning.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of learning and optimizing judgment thinking traces is innovative. The two proposed RL methods (Critic-guided Offline Learning and Rule-based Online Learning) offer alternative strategies to achieve this optimization.
*   **Performance:** The experimental results show promising results, with Think-J outperforming other LLM-Judge methods. The ablation studies and comparisons with various baselines provide evidence for the effectiveness of the approach.
*   **Clarity:** The paper provides a clear and well-structured description of the proposed method, the experimental setup, and the results.
*   **Focus on Explainability:** The inclusion of thinking traces adds a layer of explainability to the judgments, which is often lacking in classifier-based methods.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Data:** The method relies on a small, curated dataset (LIMJ707) for initialization. The generalizability of the learned thinking capabilities to more diverse or complex scenarios could be a concern.
*   **Dependence on Deepseek-R1 and Proprietary Models:** The initialization step relies on the thinking traces generated by Deepseek-R1. Similarly, the critic model is mentioned to be trained on proprietary models, which limits reproducibility and accessibility.
*   **Limited Validation Data:** While the paper shows improvements, there are concerns that the "positive samples" for optimization are, in effect, more model-generated samples, and this could cause confirmation bias, with optimization focusing on model metrics as opposed to human preferences.
*   **Lack of In-depth Error Analysis:** While performance metrics are provided, a deeper error analysis of the types of errors Think-J still makes would be valuable to understand its limitations and future directions.
*   **Limited comparisons/analyses of online and offline methods:** There is not much discussion of why to choose one over the other.

**Significance:**

The paper contributes to the growing field of LLM evaluation and preference modeling. Improving LLMs as judges is crucial for reliable model development and deployment. The proposed Think-J approach offers a promising direction for enhancing the reasoning and judgment abilities of LLMs, leading to more accurate and explainable evaluations. However, further research is needed to address the limitations mentioned above and validate the approach on larger, more diverse datasets.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

The assigned score reflects a balancing act between the paper's strengths and weaknesses. While the proposed method is novel and shows promising performance gains, the dependence on a limited curated dataset and the reliance on specific models for initialization raise concerns about generalizability and reproducibility. The limitations in model and data analyses need to be addressed to show the full promise of the approach.

**Score: 6.5**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### YESciEval: Robust LLM-as-a-Judge for Scientific Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14279v1)
- **Authors**: Jennifer D'Souza, Hamed Babaei Giglou, Quentin Münch
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) drive scientific question-answering on modern search engines, yet their evaluation robustness remains underexplored. We introduce YESciEval, an open-source framework that combines fine-grained rubric-based assessment with reinforcement learning to mitigate optimism bias in LLM evaluators. We release multidisciplinary scienceQ&A datasets, including adversarial variants, with evaluation scores from multiple LLMs. Independent of proprietary models and human feedback, our approach enables scalable, cost-free evaluation. By advancing reliable LLM-as-a-judge models, this work supports AI alignment and fosters robust, transparent evaluation essential for scientific inquiry and artificial general intelligence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "YESciEval: Robust LLM-as-a-Judge for Scientific Question Answering":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces YESciEval, an open-source framework for evaluating Large Language Models (LLMs) in the context of scientific question answering (scienceQ&A). It addresses the problem that while LLMs are increasingly used for generating answers to scientific questions in search engines, their evaluation robustness remains under-explored. YESciEval combines fine-grained, rubric-based assessment (nine rubrics categorized into Linguistic & Stylistic Quality, Logical & Structural Integrity, and Content Accuracy & Informativeness) with reinforcement learning (RLHF) to mitigate the optimism bias often observed in LLM evaluators. The authors release a new multidisciplinary scienceQ&A dataset containing both benign and adversarial examples (systematically perturbed instances designed to mislead LLMs), and evaluation scores from multiple LLMs. Their approach aims to provide a scalable, cost-free, transparent, and reliable evaluation system, independent of proprietary models and human feedback. The paper investigates how different LLM families respond across a given scienceQ&A.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates novelty in several aspects. First, the creation and release of the YESciEval multidisciplinary scienceQ&A corpus with adversarial examples fill a gap in publicly available resources. Second, the integration of RLHF with fine-grained rubrics in an open-source LLM-as-a-judge methodology is a valuable contribution, showing potential to address optimism bias. The systematic adversarial testing approach offers a novel method for evaluating LLM robustness, focusing on rubric-level degradations to evaluate the faithfulness in the judgements. Third, the framework uses open-source LLMs, reducing reliance on proprietary systems and enabling more cost-effective evaluation.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant for several reasons. The proliferation of LLMs in scientific search emphasizes the importance of reliable evaluation methods, fostering transparency and trust in these systems. YESciEval contributes towards AI alignment by improving the reliability and robustness of LLM evaluators. The emphasis on open-source models and cost-free evaluation makes the framework accessible to a broader research community. By identifying LLM fragilities under adversarial attacks, the work highlights areas for improvement in LLM design and evaluation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a well-defined and comprehensive evaluation framework with clear rubrics and adversarial test cases.
    *   The methodology is reproducible and the resources (datasets, code) are made publicly available.
    *   It directly tackles the crucial problem of optimism bias in LLM evaluators.
    *   It focuses on the increasingly important domain of scienceQ&A.
    *   The release of the LLMeval scores increases the transparency of the LLMs' behaviours.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The adversarial attacks, while effective, are relatively simple, heuristic-based perturbations. More sophisticated, adaptive attacks could further challenge LLM robustness.
    *   The reliance on open-source LLMs, while promoting accessibility, may limit the overall performance of the evaluators compared to proprietary state-of-the-art models.
    *   The evaluations are still subject to biases present in the training data of the LLMs used as judges, which requires careful monitoring.

*   **Potential Influence:** The YESciEval framework has the potential to influence future research in scienceQ&A and LLM evaluation by providing a standardized benchmark and a cost-effective, transparent evaluation methodology. The dataset could stimulate research on robust LLMs and adversarial defense techniques in scientific domains. The rubric driven reinforcement learning might translate to real world applications.
* The paper doesn't make use of chain-of-thought prompting which is proven to increase model judgement.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:** The paper represents a solid contribution to the field by addressing a practical and significant problem in LLM evaluation. The introduction of YESciEval and its associated resources fills a gap in the existing literature, promoting more reliable and accessible evaluation of LLMs in scienceQ&A. The emphasis on open-source models and adversarial testing aligns with the growing need for transparent and robust AI systems.
However, the limitations regarding the simplicity of adversarial attacks and the dependence on open-source LLM capabilities prevent a higher score. While the contribution is valuable, the framework could benefit from incorporating more advanced attack strategies and exploring more powerful base LLMs as they become available.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Universal Acoustic Adversarial Attacks for Flexible Control of Speech-LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14286v1)
- **Authors**: Rao Ma, Mengjie Qian, Vyas Raina, Mark Gales, Kate Knill
- **Abstract**: The combination of pre-trained speech encoders with large language models has enabled the development of speech LLMs that can handle a wide range of spoken language processing tasks. While these models are powerful and flexible, this very flexibility may make them more vulnerable to adversarial attacks. To examine the extent of this problem, in this work we investigate universal acoustic adversarial attacks on speech LLMs. Here a fixed, universal, adversarial audio segment is prepended to the original input audio. We initially investigate attacks that cause the model to either produce no output or to perform a modified task overriding the original prompt. We then extend the nature of the attack to be selective so that it activates only when specific input attributes, such as a speaker gender or spoken language, are present. Inputs without the targeted attribute should be unaffected, allowing fine-grained control over the model outputs. Our findings reveal critical vulnerabilities in Qwen2-Audio and Granite-Speech and suggest that similar speech LLMs may be susceptible to universal adversarial attacks. This highlights the need for more robust training strategies and improved resistance to adversarial attacks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates universal acoustic adversarial attacks on speech Large Language Models (LLMs). It explores how a fixed, short audio segment prepended to input audio can manipulate the LLM's output. The study examines two types of attacks: general attacks (muting the output or controlling the task) and selective attacks (affecting output only when specific attributes, like speaker gender or language, are present). The paper finds that both general and selective attacks are effective against Qwen2-Audio and Granite-Speech, highlighting vulnerabilities in these models and the need for robust training strategies.  The authors evaluate transferability of these attacks across different datasets, languages, and prompts. They also analyze the impact of restricting the amplitude of the adversarial signal to improve imperceptibility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The concept of universal acoustic adversarial attacks isn't entirely new, as previous work explored it for Whisper and other ASR systems. However, the **extension to speech LLMs and the introduction of *selective* universal attacks** are novel contributions. Selective attacks, conditioning on input attributes, represent a significant advance in adversarial manipulation and raise serious security and ethical concerns.  Exploring attack methods targeting the more complex reasoning and instruction-following abilities of speech LLMs marks a clear departure from prior work primarily concerned with ASR. This includes exploring attacks on different tasks beyond just transcription or translation. The analysis of Attack-ref and Attack-hyp represents a methodical approach to manipulating the LLM's functionality.
* **Significance:** The findings are significant because they demonstrate the vulnerability of state-of-the-art speech LLMs to relatively simple attacks. Given the increasing deployment of such models in real-world applications, these vulnerabilities pose real security and ethical risks, potentially allowing adversaries to bypass moderation systems, manipulate responses based on user characteristics, or introduce biases. The work convincingly demonstrates a clear failure mode in these complex models. This raises the alarm about deploying such models in safety-critical applications without further security enhancements. The systematic approach to evaluation, covering various datasets, languages, prompts, and imperceptibility constraints adds considerable strength to the paper.
* **Strengths:**
    * The paper is well-written and clearly explains the different types of attacks and their implications.
    * The experimental setup is rigorous, with thorough evaluation on multiple datasets and models.
    * The analysis of transferability and imperceptibility is insightful and adds depth to the study.
    * The introduction of the selective attack is a significant contribution.
    * The paper convincingly demonstrates a weakness in existing speech LLMs.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The attacks rely on white-box access to the model's weights during training, which might not be realistic in all scenarios.  While acknowledged, exploring the black-box transferability from a model trained in a white-box setting would increase the paper's impact.
    * The paper primarily focuses on two specific speech LLMs. While these are representative, a broader range of models should be investigated to establish the generalizability of the findings.
    * There isn't any analysis of potential defense mechanisms. Exploring even simple mitigation strategies would improve the paper's impact.
    *  While the amplitude constraint is considered for imperceptibility, perceptual studies are absent.  A listening test, even on a small scale, to assess whether humans can actually detect the manipulated audio under varying amplitude constraints would have improved the validity of the analysis.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to stimulate further research on adversarial robustness in speech LLMs, particularly focusing on developing defense mechanisms against universal and selective attacks. It could also prompt developers to consider security and ethical implications during the design and training of these models.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel extension to existing work on adversarial attacks in speech processing by demonstrating the vulnerability of complex speech LLMs to universal and selective attacks. The introduction of selective attacks is a significant contribution, highlighting the potential for malicious actors to manipulate these models in a fine-grained and discriminatory way. The rigorous experimental evaluation, covering various datasets and languages, strengthens the validity of the findings. However, the reliance on white-box access and the lack of analysis on defense mechanisms or perceptual studies are limitations that prevent a higher score. The demonstrated weaknesses in these models justify the score, signifying a significant finding for the community.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Towards Generating Realistic Underwater Images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14296v1)
- **Authors**: Abdul-Kazeem Shamba
- **Abstract**: This paper explores the use of contrastive learning and generative adversarial networks for generating realistic underwater images from synthetic images with uniform lighting. We investigate the performance of image translation models for generating realistic underwater images using the VAROS dataset. Two key evaluation metrics, Fr\'echet Inception Distance (FID) and Structural Similarity Index Measure (SSIM), provide insights into the trade-offs between perceptual quality and structural preservation. For paired image translation, pix2pix achieves the best FID scores due to its paired supervision and PatchGAN discriminator, while the autoencoder model attains the highest SSIM, suggesting better structural fidelity despite producing blurrier outputs. Among unpaired methods, CycleGAN achieves a competitive FID score by leveraging cycle-consistency loss, whereas CUT, which replaces cycle-consistency with contrastive learning, attains higher SSIM, indicating improved spatial similarity retention. Notably, incorporating depth information into CUT results in the lowest overall FID score, demonstrating that depth cues enhance realism. However, the slight decrease in SSIM suggests that depth-aware learning may introduce structural variations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of contrastive learning and generative adversarial networks (GANs) for generating realistic underwater images from synthetic images with uniform lighting. It investigates the performance of various image translation models on the VAROS dataset. The study uses Fréchet Inception Distance (FID) and Structural Similarity Index Measure (SSIM) to evaluate the trade-offs between perceptual quality and structural preservation. The results show that pix2pix performs well for paired image translation, while CycleGAN and Contrastive Unpaired Translation (CUT) achieve competitive results for unpaired translation. Notably, incorporating depth information into CUT further improves realism, although it may introduce slight structural variations.  The paper contributes to the field by exploring state-of-the-art image translation techniques for generating realistic underwater images and by investigating the impact of depth information on the quality of generated images.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has incremental novelty. While the application of GANs and contrastive learning to underwater image generation is not entirely new, the authors explore a range of existing techniques (pix2pix, CycleGAN, CUT) and investigate the impact of incorporating depth information into the CUT framework. The adaptation of existing techniques to a specific domain (underwater imaging) represents a valuable contribution, especially when accompanied by thorough experimentation and analysis. However, no entirely novel architecture or theoretical contribution is presented.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its practical application and the insights it provides for generating realistic underwater images, which can benefit marine robotics, underwater archaeology, and oceanography. The use of synthetic images for training and testing helps mitigate the challenges of acquiring real underwater data. The comparative evaluation of different techniques and the investigation of depth information are valuable contributions for practitioners in the field. Furthermore, this paper advances the state of the art underwater image synthesis, while leveraging the benefits of contrastive learning to produce results that could rival physics-based methods using less memory and training time.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Systematic evaluation of different image translation techniques using standard metrics (FID, SSIM).
    *   Investigation of the impact of depth information on underwater image generation.
    *   Practical application with potential benefits for various fields.
    *   Detailed model descriptions and training details.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Incremental novelty; mostly an application and adaptation of existing techniques.
    *   Limited discussion of the limitations of the generated images and potential artifacts.
    *   The improvements achieved with depth information, while positive in terms of FID, are relatively modest, and the decrease in SSIM suggests potential trade-offs.
    *   The lack of a user study to validate perceptual quality of the output images.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence researchers and practitioners in the field of underwater imaging by providing a benchmark for different image translation techniques and highlighting the benefits of incorporating depth information. It can inspire future work on developing more advanced and robust techniques for generating realistic underwater images.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a thorough and valuable exploration of existing image translation techniques for generating realistic underwater images. The adaptation and evaluation of these techniques, along with the investigation of depth information, contribute to the state-of-the-art in this specific domain. However, the incremental novelty and the limited discussion of limitations and potential artifacts prevent it from achieving a higher score.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Cross-Lingual Optimization for Language Transfer in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14297v1)
- **Authors**: Jungseob Lee, Seongtae Hong, Hyeonseok Moon, Heuiseok Lim
- **Abstract**: Adapting large language models to other languages typically employs supervised fine-tuning (SFT) as a standard approach. However, it often suffers from an overemphasis on English performance, a phenomenon that is especially pronounced in data-constrained environments. To overcome these challenges, we propose \textbf{Cross-Lingual Optimization (CLO)} that efficiently transfers an English-centric LLM to a target language while preserving its English capabilities. CLO utilizes publicly available English SFT data and a translation model to enable cross-lingual transfer. We conduct experiments using five models on six languages, each possessing varying levels of resource. Our results show that CLO consistently outperforms SFT in both acquiring target language proficiency and maintaining English performance. Remarkably, in low-resource languages, CLO with only 3,200 samples surpasses SFT with 6,400 samples, demonstrating that CLO can achieve better performance with less data. Furthermore, we find that SFT is particularly sensitive to data quantity in medium and low-resource languages, whereas CLO remains robust. Our comprehensive analysis emphasizes the limitations of SFT and incorporates additional training strategies in CLO to enhance efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Cross-Lingual Optimization (CLO), a novel strategy for efficiently adapting English-centric large language models (LLMs) to other languages while preserving English capabilities. CLO utilizes publicly available English supervised fine-tuning (SFT) data and a translation model to enable cross-lingual transfer, modifying Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to prioritize responses in the target language when prompted in that language, while discouraging responses in other languages.  The authors conduct experiments on six languages (Chinese, German, Korean, Indonesian, Swahili, Yoruba) and five LLMs. The results demonstrate that CLO consistently outperforms SFT in acquiring target language proficiency and maintaining English performance, especially in low-resource settings.  Further analyses explore the impact of data quantity and ablation studies of various components of the framework.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of using a modified DPO loss to promote target language responses and discourage responses in other languages (while leveraging English SFT data through translation) demonstrates good novelty. Existing approaches often require substantial target language data or complex architectures, whereas CLO offers a relatively simple and efficient method. The specific combination of DPO, translation, and targeted attention layer fine-tuning contributes to novelty.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial issue: the English-centric nature of LLMs and the challenges of adapting them to other languages, particularly in low-resource scenarios. CLO's ability to achieve better performance with less data compared to standard SFT has practical implications. This could make multilingual LLMs more accessible and effective in resource-constrained contexts. The thorough empirical evaluation across multiple languages and models strengthens the paper's significance.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of SFT for language transfer, especially in data-scarce environments.
*   **Well-motivated approach:** The CLO strategy is logically derived from the identified shortcomings of SFT.
*   **Comprehensive evaluation:**  The experiments are extensive, covering multiple languages, models, and evaluation metrics (instruction following, MRC, reasoning). The inclusion of high-, medium-, and low-resource languages is particularly valuable.
*   **Detailed analysis:** The paper explores various aspects of CLO, including the impact of data quantity, the role of attention layers, and the effect of different NLL loss formulations.
*   **Clear writing and presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized, making it easy to follow the methodology and results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Translation Quality:** The quality of the translation model is a critical factor in CLO's performance. The paper acknowledges that translation errors can occur. The effect of translation errors on performance could be assessed more systematically.
*   **Scope of Multilinguality:** While the paper covers multiple languages, it primarily focuses on adapting from English to *one* target language at a time. A truly multilingual LLM would handle multiple languages concurrently. Addressing this limitation would strengthen the paper further.
*  **Specificity of the Experiments:**  Experiments could explore more diverse datasets or real-world applications and whether the observed performance trends hold in different contexts.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and efficient method (CLO) for cross-lingual transfer in LLMs, which has significant potential to improve the performance of multilingual models in low-resource settings. The thorough experimentation and detailed analysis strengthen the paper's claims. However, the reliance on translation quality, the limited scope of multilinguality (single target language adaptation), and the somewhat narrow experimentation scope prevent it from achieving a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Empowering LLMs in Task-Oriented Dialogues: A Domain-Independent Multi-Agent Framework and Fine-Tuning Strategy
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14299v1)
- **Authors**: Zihao Feng, Xiaoxue Wang, Bowen Wu, Weihong Zhong, Zhen Xu, Hailong Cao, Tiejun Zhao, Ying Li, Baoxun Wang
- **Abstract**: Task-oriented dialogue systems based on Large Language Models (LLMs) have gained increasing attention across various industries and achieved significant results. Current approaches condense complex procedural workflows into a single agent to achieve satisfactory performance on large-scale LLMs. However, these approaches face challenges to achieve comparable performance on fine-tuned lightweight LLMs, due to their limited capabilities in handling multiple complex logic. In this work, we design a Domain-Independent Multi-Agent Framework (DIMF), which contains Intent Classification Agent, Slot Filling Agent and Response Agent. This approach simplifies the learning complexity and enhances the generalization ability by separating the tasks into domain-independent components. In this framework, we enhance the capabilities in contextual understanding using the Direct Preference Optimisation (DPO) method, and propose a simple and effective Data Distribution Adaptation (DDA) method to mitigate degradation issues during DPO training. Experiments conducted on the MultiWOZ datasets show that our proposed method achieves a better average performance among all the baselines. Extensive analysis also demonstrates that our proposed framework exhibits excellent generalizability and zero-shot capability.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper presents a Domain-Independent Multi-Agent Framework (DIMF) for task-oriented dialogue (TOD) systems. The framework decomposes the TOD task into three independent agents: Intent Classification Agent, Slot Filling Agent, and Response Agent. This modular design aims to simplify the complexity of each agent, enhance generalization, and improve performance on fine-tuned lightweight LLMs. The authors also propose a Data Distribution Adaptation (DDA) method to mitigate the degradation of performance during Direct Preference Optimisation (DPO) training. Experiments on the MultiWOZ dataset demonstrate that the proposed method achieves better average performance compared to baseline models, exhibits excellent generalizability, and has zero-shot capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of this paper lies primarily in its domain-independent multi-agent framework, combined with a specific fine-tuning strategy using DPO and DDA. While multi-agent systems are not entirely new in the TOD domain, the emphasis on domain independence and the specific approach to training and adaptation are distinctive. The decomposition into three agents seems reasonable, but the individual agents' architecture and prompting strategies are standard. The DDA method to handle DPO degradation is a valuable practical contribution, though not fundamentally groundbreaking. The framework focuses on light-weight models which are interesting due to computational complexity of other LLM models.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its practical approach to improving the performance of lightweight LLMs in TOD systems. By decoupling the workflow into domain-independent components and employing targeted training strategies, the proposed framework offers a pathway to achieve comparable or better performance than larger models, while maintaining scalability and generalizability. The improved zero-shot capabilities and generalizability is interesting for practical applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges of applying lightweight LLMs to complex TOD tasks.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The DIMF framework is well-defined and modular, allowing for focused optimization of each agent.
    *   **Practical Training Strategy:** The DDA method is simple and effective in mitigating DPO degradation.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments on MultiWOZ show significant performance improvements over existing baseline methods, including LLM-based ones.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:**  The analysis of the results are thorough and provide good insight into the behavior of DPO training, especially when training for small datasets.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** The paper is mostly practical. The motivation and the solution lack a rigorous theoretical foundation. It does not investigate other reinforcement learning strategies.
    *   **Reliance on MultiWOZ:** The evaluation is primarily limited to MultiWOZ dataset, a relatively simple dataset. Further evaluation on more complex, real-world datasets may be needed.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the results are compelling, the techniques are incremental. It builds upon existing methods (multi-agent systems, DPO) and adds a relatively straightforward adaptation technique.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the above analysis, the score assigned is **7**.

*   The paper proposes a well-engineered practical system for TOD using lightweight LLMs with demonstrable results on a standard dataset. The domain-independent design, combined with the DDA training strategy, is a significant contribution that improves performance and generalizability.

*   However, the paper lacks a significant leap in theoretical understanding or innovation. While the proposed approach has practical utility, it is primarily an incremental improvement of existing methods. The impact will depend on whether other researchers and practitioners are able to easily reproduce and extend the work.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.MA
- **Score**: 7/10

### SafetyNet: Detecting Harmful Outputs in LLMs by Modeling and Monitoring Deceptive Behaviors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14300v1)
- **Authors**: Maheep Chaudhary, Fazl Barez
- **Abstract**: High-risk industries like nuclear and aviation use real-time monitoring to detect dangerous system conditions. Similarly, Large Language Models (LLMs) need monitoring safeguards. We propose a real-time framework to predict harmful AI outputs before they occur by using an unsupervised approach that treats normal behavior as the baseline and harmful outputs as outliers. Our study focuses specifically on backdoor-triggered responses -- where specific input phrases activate hidden vulnerabilities causing the model to generate unsafe content like violence, pornography, or hate speech. We address two key challenges: (1) identifying true causal indicators rather than surface correlations, and (2) preventing advanced models from deception -- deliberately evading monitoring systems. Hence, we approach this problem from an unsupervised lens by drawing parallels to human deception: just as humans exhibit physical indicators while lying, we investigate whether LLMs display distinct internal behavioral signatures when generating harmful content. Our study addresses two critical challenges: 1) designing monitoring systems that capture true causal indicators rather than superficial correlations; and 2)preventing intentional evasion by increasingly capable "Future models''. Our findings show that models can produce harmful content through causal mechanisms and can become deceptive by: (a) alternating between linear and non-linear representations, and (b) modifying feature relationships. To counter this, we developed Safety-Net -- a multi-detector framework that monitors different representation dimensions, successfully detecting harmful behavior even when information is shifted across representational spaces to evade individual monitors. Our evaluation shows 96% accuracy in detecting harmful cases using our unsupervised ensemble approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "SafetyNet: Detecting Harmful Outputs in LLMs by Modeling and Monitoring Deceptive Behaviors" proposes a real-time framework to detect harmful outputs in Large Language Models (LLMs) before they are generated. The approach focuses on backdoor-triggered responses, where specific inputs trigger the generation of unsafe content. SafetyNet uses an unsupervised method that treats normal LLM behavior as a baseline and identifies harmful outputs as outliers, drawing parallels to how humans detect deception. The system monitors internal states of LLMs – attention matrices and MLP activations – to identify distinct "behavioral signatures" associated with harmful content generation. To combat models with deceptive capabilities that intentionally evade monitoring, the authors employ a red-team/blue-team adversarial methodology. The red team develops methods to infuse deception into LLMs, and the blue team builds safety measures to counter these deceptive techniques.  Safety-Net is a multi-detector framework with complementary monitors like Mahalanobis distance and autoencoders. The evaluation shows high accuracy in detecting harmful cases using the unsupervised ensemble approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects:

*   **Unsupervised Deception Detection:** The primary innovation lies in the unsupervised methodology for detecting potentially harmful outputs, drawing parallels to human deception.  This moves beyond traditional supervised methods that rely on pre-labeled harmful content and allows for the discovery of *novel* harmful behaviors.
*   **Behavioral Signatures Focus:** Identifying specific internal behavioral signatures in LLMs associated with harmful content generation is a key contribution.  This approach aims to capture *causal* indicators rather than superficial correlations.
*   **Red-Team/Blue-Team Deception:** The incorporation of an adversarial approach to study how LLMs can be made deceptive is a significant step. By studying *how* models might attempt to circumvent monitoring, the researchers can design more robust safeguards.
*   **SafetyNet Ensemble Detector:**  The multi-detector ensemble is a crucial part of the framework because the experimentation reveals how easily fooled individual detectors are. The ensemble approach is necessary to create a more resistant system.

**Significance:**

*   **Real-time Monitoring:** Addresses a critical need for real-time monitoring of LLMs, essential for deploying these models in high-risk environments.
*   **Beyond Supervised Learning:** The unsupervised approach is particularly relevant given the limitations of supervised methods in capturing the full spectrum of potential harmful behaviors.
*   **Future-Proofing LLM Safety:** The work explicitly tackles the problem of *future* models that might be situationally aware and intentionally deceptive, addressing a key challenge for long-term AI safety.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** A significant strength is the comprehensive experimentation, exploring different detection methods, deception techniques, and model architectures (Llama-2 and Llama-3).

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Harmful Behaviors:** The primary focus is on backdoor-triggered responses. While this is an important area, it doesn't address all potential types of harmful outputs from LLMs.
*   **Domain Specificity of Behavioral Signatures:** The behavioral signatures identified might be specific to the models and datasets used. More research is needed to determine how well these signatures generalize across different LLMs and tasks.
*   **Computational Complexity:**  Monitoring internal states of LLMs in real-time can be computationally expensive, which may limit the scalability of the approach. The paper would benefit from a discussion of practical deployment considerations, such as latency and resource consumption.
*   **Dependency on Architectural Insights:** The choice of layers (9-12) for intervention experiments seems somewhat ad hoc. While justified empirically, more theoretical rationale for targeting these layers could strengthen the work.
*   **Generality concerns:** The study heavily relies on specific models (Llama 2/3) and datasets. Concerns remain as to how effective this system will be on other model families, especially those which vary greatly in architecture. The results are convincing, but also feel very tuned to a specific context.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM safety. The novel unsupervised approach, the focus on behavioral signatures, and the red-team/blue-team deception experiments are all strengths.  The work addresses a significant challenge (detecting harmful content in a *dynamic* and adversarial setting) with an innovative solution.

However, the limitations regarding the scope of harmful behaviors, the potential domain specificity of signatures, and the computational complexity prevent a higher score. While thoroughly executed, the findings need broader validation and a more in-depth discussion of practical deployment hurdles.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Scaling Law for Quantization-Aware Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14302v1)
- **Authors**: Mengzhao Chen, Chaoyi Zhang, Jing Liu, Yutao Zeng, Zeyue Xue, Zhiheng Liu, Yunshui Li, Jin Ma, Jie Huang, Xun Zhou, Ping Luo
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) demand substantial computational and memory resources, creating deployment challenges. Quantization-aware training (QAT) addresses these challenges by reducing model precision while maintaining performance. However, the scaling behavior of QAT, especially at 4-bit precision (W4A4), is not well understood. Existing QAT scaling laws often ignore key factors such as the number of training tokens and quantization granularity, which limits their applicability. This paper proposes a unified scaling law for QAT that models quantization error as a function of model size, training data volume, and quantization group size. Through 268 QAT experiments, we show that quantization error decreases as model size increases, but rises with more training tokens and coarser quantization granularity. To identify the sources of W4A4 quantization error, we decompose it into weight and activation components. Both components follow the overall trend of W4A4 quantization error, but with different sensitivities. Specifically, weight quantization error increases more rapidly with more training tokens. Further analysis shows that the activation quantization error in the FC2 layer, caused by outliers, is the primary bottleneck of W4A4 QAT quantization error. By applying mixed-precision quantization to address this bottleneck, we demonstrate that weight and activation quantization errors can converge to similar levels. Additionally, with more training data, weight quantization error eventually exceeds activation quantization error, suggesting that reducing weight quantization error is also important in such scenarios. These findings offer key insights for improving QAT research and development.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates scaling laws for quantization-aware training (QAT), particularly at 4-bit precision (W4A4), which is important for deploying large language models (LLMs) efficiently. The authors propose a unified scaling law that models quantization error as a function of model size, training data volume, and quantization group size. Through extensive experiments, they demonstrate that quantization error decreases with model size but increases with more training tokens and coarser quantization granularity. They decompose the error into weight and activation components, finding that weight quantization error is more sensitive to training tokens. A key finding is that activation quantization error in the FC2 layer (due to outliers) is a major bottleneck. Addressing this with mixed-precision quantization (8-bit FC2 input) shows improved convergence and highlights the need to consider both weight and activation quantization in QAT design.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its unified treatment of model size, training data volume, and quantization granularity within a QAT scaling law. While previous works considered some of these factors, this paper integrates them comprehensively. The decomposition of quantization error into weight and activation components and the identification of the FC2 layer as a major bottleneck are also valuable insights. Previous works tended to focus on either weight or activation quantization separately, and the emphasis on the *relative* contribution of each as a function of the data-to-parameter ratio is a significant contribution.

* **Significance:** The paper's findings have significant implications for the design and training of quantized LLMs. By understanding the interplay of these factors, practitioners can make informed decisions about model size, data usage, and quantization granularity. The identification of the FC2 layer bottleneck provides a concrete target for optimization, as demonstrated by the effectiveness of mixed-precision quantization. The shift from activation-centric to balanced weight/activation optimization is a valuable direction. The scaling law provides a framework for predicting the performance of QAT models, enabling more efficient resource allocation and model selection.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Experimental Setup:** The paper presents a large-scale empirical study with 268 QAT experiments, providing strong support for its claims.  The systematic exploration of various model sizes, dataset sizes, and quantization settings lends credibility to the findings.
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of QAT at ultra-low bit-widths and the limitations of existing scaling laws.
    * **Actionable Insights:** The paper provides concrete recommendations for improving QAT, such as using mixed-precision quantization for the FC2 layer and balancing weight and activation optimization.
    * **Rigorous Analysis:** The paper employs a variety of analytical techniques, including error decomposition, kurtosis analysis, and bottleneck layer analysis, to gain a deep understanding of the QAT process.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Model Limitations:** The experiments are limited to Llama-3 style models and the OLMo2-Mix-1124 dataset. While this is a common setup, exploring other architectures and datasets would increase the generalizability of the findings.  Extending to MoE architectures, as the authors themselves acknowledge, would also be beneficial.
    * **Simplifications:**  The evaluation metric uses smoothed training loss instead of validation loss. While justified for simplicity and consistency with prior work, this introduces a potential bias. Direct validation loss estimates would be more robust.
    * **Limited Scope of Solutions:** The paper mainly focuses on the FC2 layer outlier issue and suggests mixed precision as a solution. Other outlier mitigation techniques or alternative architectural changes could be explored more extensively.
    * **Lack of Direct Comparison to SOTA:**  While the paper presents the limitations of current scaling laws and showcases improvements, a direct comparison against the best existing QAT methods might have significantly elevated the contribution.

* **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to influence future research in QAT by highlighting the importance of considering all three factors (model size, data volume, and quantization granularity). It also contributes to the understanding of weight and activation quantization errors in low-bit models. The specific suggestions about mixed-precision training and outlier mitigation can be readily adopted and validated by other researchers.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**. The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of QAT by providing a unified scaling law and insights into the error sources in low-bit models. While it addresses critical challenges and offers actionable solutions, the study's scope is somewhat limited by focusing on a specific model architecture and relying on smoothed training loss. Also, while promising, the gains may be significantly impacted by the model architecture employed.  The potential for impact is good, but a score of 8+ would necessitate more comprehensive experimentation and comparisons. The work is novel and impactful enough to merit a score above the average, but falls short of the highest accolades due to the limitations noted above.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### JOLT-SQL: Joint Loss Tuning of Text-to-SQL with Confusion-aware Noisy Schema Sampling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14305v1)
- **Authors**: Jinwang Song, Hongying Zan, Kunli Zhang, Lingling Mu, Yingjie Han, Haobo Hua, Min Peng
- **Abstract**: Text-to-SQL, which maps natural language to SQL queries, has benefited greatly from recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs). While LLMs offer various paradigms for this task, including prompting and supervised fine-tuning (SFT), SFT approaches still face challenges such as complex multi-stage pipelines and poor robustness to noisy schema information. To address these limitations, we present JOLT-SQL, a streamlined single-stage SFT framework that jointly optimizes schema linking and SQL generation via a unified loss. JOLT-SQL employs discriminative schema linking, enhanced by local bidirectional attention, alongside a confusion-aware noisy schema sampling strategy with selective attention to improve robustness under noisy schema conditions. Experiments on the Spider and BIRD benchmarks demonstrate that JOLT-SQL achieves state-of-the-art execution accuracy among comparable-size open-source models, while significantly improving both training and inference efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces JOLT-SQL, a streamlined single-stage Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) framework for text-to-SQL conversion. The key contributions are: 1) a discriminative schema linking method enhanced with local bidirectional attention; 2) a joint loss training strategy combining schema linking and SQL generation, avoiding separate fine-tuning stages; and 3) a confusion-aware noisy schema sampling technique that improves robustness against imperfect schema linking results by selectively attending to schema items that the model is likely to misclassify.  Experiments on the Spider and BIRD datasets show that JOLT-SQL achieves state-of-the-art execution accuracy among comparable-size open-source models and improves both training and inference efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of several techniques within a single SFT framework, tailored for text-to-SQL. The discriminative schema linking with bidirectional attention is a well-grounded idea, building on previous work like LS-LLaMA. The joint loss tuning is a logical step towards simplifying the training pipeline and optimizing the interaction between schema linking and SQL generation. The most interesting component is the confusion-aware noisy schema sampling. Actively sampling noisy schema based on the model's own "confusion" during training seems promising and potentially beneficial for robustness.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is primarily in demonstrating a practical and efficient approach for SFT-based text-to-SQL. Achieving state-of-the-art results with a relatively small model (Qwen2.5-Coder-14B) and improved efficiency is valuable, especially for deployment scenarios where resource constraints matter. The ablation studies provide insights into the contributions of each component, although more detailed analysis could be beneficial. The case study, included in Appendix C, helps to illustrate the effectiveness of the confusion-aware noisy schema sampling.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Efficiency:** The main strength is the achieved efficiency in both training and inference, making the method more practical for real-world deployment.
    *   **Performance:** Achieving competitive results, especially given the model size, is a significant contribution, showcasing the effectiveness of the proposed techniques.
    *   **Clear Presentation:**  The paper is well-written and the concepts are clearly explained.  The ablation studies and case study provide further validation of the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Innovation:** While the combination of techniques is novel, each individual technique (bidirectional attention, joint loss, noisy sampling) has precedents in the literature. The paper builds upon established concepts rather than introducing fundamentally new breakthroughs.
    *   **Limited Model Size:** The evaluation is limited to 7B and 14B models. While this is understandable given resource constraints, it would be stronger to show the method's scalability to larger models (e.g., a 30B+ model) and compare to other methods tested on larger models. The absence of results on larger models somewhat restricts the generality of the findings.
    *   **Scope of Evaluation:** The focus is primarily on Spider and BIRD. While these are established datasets, it would be beneficial to evaluate the method on other, more recent datasets to ensure robustness across different types of SQL queries and database schemas.
    *   **Ablation Study Depth:** The ablation studies, while helpful, could be more detailed. For instance, varying the beta parameter in the noisy schema sampling or providing more in-depth analysis of different kinds of confusion would provide greater insight.
* **Missing Comparisons:** The paper lacks any form of statistical significance testing or error rate analysis, which would have strengthened the significance of the findings.
* **Missing discussion regarding the model parameters and computational requirements:** The authors provide basic implementation details; however, a table that outlines the trainable parameters and computational requirements of the model would assist with replicability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The JOLT-SQL framework could influence future research in text-to-SQL by providing a more efficient and robust alternative to multi-stage pipelines.  The confusion-aware noisy schema sampling strategy has the potential to be generalized and applied to other tasks where dealing with noisy or imperfect inputs is critical.

**Score:** 7/10

**Rationale:**
The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of text-to-SQL by demonstrating an efficient and robust SFT framework. It achieves competitive results with smaller models and improves upon existing pipelines, making it a practically valuable approach. However, the lack of significant individual breakthroughs, the limitation of model size evaluation, and scope of its evaluation prevent it from achieving a higher score. Additionally, it could benefit from providing more in-depth ablation analyses and statistical testing.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### HausaNLP: Current Status, Challenges and Future Directions for Hausa Natural Language Processing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14311v1)
- **Authors**: Shamsuddeen Hassan Muhammad, Ibrahim Said Ahmad, Idris Abdulmumin, Falalu Ibrahim Lawan, Babangida Sani, Sukairaj Hafiz Imam, Yusuf Aliyu, Sani Abdullahi Sani, Ali Usman Umar, Kenneth Church, Vukosi Marivate
- **Abstract**: Hausa Natural Language Processing (NLP) has gained increasing attention in recent years, yet remains understudied as a low-resource language despite having over 120 million first-language (L1) and 80 million second-language (L2) speakers worldwide. While significant advances have been made in high-resource languages, Hausa NLP faces persistent challenges, including limited open-source datasets and inadequate model representation. This paper presents an overview of the current state of Hausa NLP, systematically examining existing resources, research contributions, and gaps across fundamental NLP tasks: text classification, machine translation, named entity recognition, speech recognition, and question answering. We introduce HausaNLP (https://catalog.hausanlp.org), a curated catalog that aggregates datasets, tools, and research works to enhance accessibility and drive further development. Furthermore, we discuss challenges in integrating Hausa into large language models (LLMs), addressing issues of suboptimal tokenization and dialectal variation. Finally, we propose strategic research directions emphasizing dataset expansion, improved language modeling approaches, and strengthened community collaboration to advance Hausa NLP. Our work provides both a foundation for accelerating Hausa NLP progress and valuable insights for broader multilingual NLP research.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper presents a comprehensive overview of the current state of Hausa Natural Language Processing (NLP). It highlights the challenges faced by Hausa NLP due to its status as a low-resource language, despite having a significant number of speakers. The authors introduce HAUSANLP, a curated catalog designed to aggregate datasets, tools, and research related to Hausa NLP to improve accessibility and accelerate development. The paper systematically reviews existing resources and research contributions across various NLP tasks (text classification, machine translation, NER, speech recognition, QA). It also discusses challenges in integrating Hausa into large language models (LLMs), particularly around suboptimal tokenization and dialectal variation. Finally, it proposes strategic research directions, emphasizing the importance of dataset expansion, improved language modeling approaches, and strengthened community collaboration to advance Hausa NLP.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the creation and introduction of the HAUSANLP catalog. While reviews of the current state of NLP for specific languages aren't uncommon, the catalog provides a tangible resource for researchers and practitioners. The discussion of challenges specific to Hausa in LLMs and the proposed future research directions contribute to the paper's novelty to some extent. However, much of the content is a survey and synthesis of existing work, reducing the degree of groundbreaking original contribution.

**Significance:** The paper is significant because it brings attention to a neglected language within the NLP field. Hausa's large speaker base warrants more attention than it currently receives. By highlighting the gaps and challenges, the authors make a strong case for increased research efforts. The HAUSANLP catalog has the potential to significantly lower the barrier to entry for researchers interested in Hausa NLP, thereby fostering further development. The specific discussion on LLMs helps to identify where future advancements need to be made. Overall, the paper serves as a valuable resource and advocacy piece for Hausa NLP.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Survey:** The paper offers a well-structured and relatively detailed overview of existing Hausa NLP research.
*   **Practical Resource (HAUSANLP Catalog):** The catalog is a concrete contribution that can directly benefit the community.
*   **Clear Identification of Challenges:** The paper clearly articulates the main challenges hindering Hausa NLP progress.
*   **Actionable Future Directions:** The proposed research directions provide a roadmap for future work.
* **Advocacy and awareness:** Increases awareness of the challenges and unique issues specific to the Hausa language NLP

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Depth of Technical Contributions:** The paper is primarily a survey and perspective piece, lacking significant novel technical contributions or experiments.
*   **Uneven Coverage:** Some sections, while valuable, are quite brief and could benefit from further elaboration. For example, the discussion of emotional analysis, while interesting, feels a bit underdeveloped.
*   **Lack of Empirical Validation:** While the authors propose strategies for LLM integration, they don't present any empirical validation or preliminary experiments.
*   **Community Driven:**  The paper is community driven. Although it is helpful in low resource areas, it might not contain significant ground-breaking research.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable synthesis of current knowledge and a concrete resource in the form of the HAUSANLP catalog. The identification of challenges and proposed future directions are helpful. However, the lack of significant novel technical contributions and the survey-like nature of much of the content prevent it from achieving a higher score. While significant for the Hausa NLP community, its broader impact on the overall NLP field is limited by the lack of broadly applicable methodological innovations.

Score: 6

**Rationale for Score**
The novelty is somewhat constrained by the lack of deep technical development and experimentation beyond the aggregation of the HUSANLP catalogue. The work's overall significance for the area is high given the low resource nature. These factors justify a score of 6, indicating a valuable but incrementally innovative contribution that needs further validation to demonstrate broader impact.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### A MIND for Reasoning: Meta-learning for In-context Deduction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14313v1)
- **Authors**: Leonardo Bertolazzi, Manuel Vargas Guzmán, Raffaella Bernardi, Maciej Malicki, Jakub Szymanik
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly evaluated on formal tasks, where strong reasoning abilities define the state of the art. However, their ability to generalize to out-of-distribution problems remains limited. In this paper, we investigate how LLMs can achieve a systematic understanding of deductive rules. Our focus is on the task of identifying the appropriate subset of premises within a knowledge base needed to derive a given hypothesis. To tackle this challenge, we propose Meta-learning for In-context Deduction (MIND), a novel few-shot meta-learning fine-tuning approach. The goal of MIND is to enable models to generalize more effectively to unseen knowledge bases and to systematically apply inference rules. Our results show that MIND significantly improves generalization in small LMs ranging from 1.5B to 7B parameters. The benefits are especially pronounced in smaller models and low-data settings. Remarkably, small models fine-tuned with MIND outperform state-of-the-art LLMs, such as GPT-4o and o3-mini, on this task.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can summarize and critically evaluate the paper "A MIND for Reasoning: Meta-learning for In-context Deduction."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Meta-learning for IN-context Deduction (MIND), a new few-shot meta-learning fine-tuning approach designed to improve the deductive reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The core task the paper addresses is premise selection – identifying the minimal subset of premises within a knowledge base required to logically entail a given hypothesis. MIND works by fine-tuning models on episodes consisting of knowledge bases, study examples (demonstrations of premise selection for similar hypotheses), and query hypotheses.  The authors test MIND on the syllogistic fragment of first-order logic, using a newly synthesized dataset of syllogisms. Experiments with small LLMs from the Qwen-2.5 family (1.5B to 7B parameters) demonstrate that MIND significantly improves generalization to unseen knowledge bases and inferences, even outperforming larger, state-of-the-art LLMs like GPT-4o and 03-mini.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel meta-learning approach (MIND) specifically tailored for improving deductive reasoning and premise selection within LLMs. While meta-learning and in-context learning are not individually new concepts, the combination of these techniques to tackle syllogistic reasoning in LLMs and the specific adaptation to the premise selection task is a novel contribution. The creation of a new synthetic dataset focused on syllogistic inference is also a positive contribution, as it provides a controlled environment for studying reasoning generalization.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation of LLMs: their struggle with out-of-distribution reasoning, even in formal domains. The finding that a relatively simple meta-learning fine-tuning approach can significantly boost deductive capabilities in smaller models, allowing them to surpass larger, more general-purpose LLMs, has important implications. It suggests that targeted training can be a very effective way to improve specific reasoning skills. This may lead to the design of more efficient and specialized LLMs or allow for better fine-tuning methods that target reasoning abilities more effectively. Furthermore, their study shows that small models could be enhanced through structured meta-learning tasks to achieve better performance compared to large, general-purpose, potentially resource-intensive models on certain reasoning challenges.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the premise selection problem and its relevance to deductive reasoning.
    *   **Well-Designed Approach:** MIND seems logically structured and has the potential to be adapted for wider range of logical tasks.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments are thorough and convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of MIND, particularly in low-data regimes. Outperforming larger models is a notable achievement.
    *   **New Dataset:** The synthetic dataset offers a valuable resource for future research in syllogistic reasoning in LLMs.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The error analysis is insightful, revealing different error patterns in MIND vs. baseline models. This contributes to a better understanding of the models' reasoning process.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The focus on syllogistic logic, while allowing for controlled experiments, also limits the generalizability of the findings. Syllogistic logic is a relatively simple form of reasoning compared to the complexity of real-world reasoning tasks.
