### Is Your Prompt Safe? Investigating Prompt Injection Attacks Against Open-Source LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14368v1)
- **Authors**: Jiawen Wang, Pritha Gupta, Ivan Habernal, Eyke Hüllermeier
- **Abstract**: Recent studies demonstrate that Large Language Models (LLMs) are vulnerable to different prompt-based attacks, generating harmful content or sensitive information. Both closed-source and open-source LLMs are underinvestigated for these attacks. This paper studies effective prompt injection attacks against the $\mathbf{14}$ most popular open-source LLMs on five attack benchmarks. Current metrics only consider successful attacks, whereas our proposed Attack Success Probability (ASP) also captures uncertainty in the model's response, reflecting ambiguity in attack feasibility. By comprehensively analyzing the effectiveness of prompt injection attacks, we propose a simple and effective hypnotism attack; results show that this attack causes aligned language models, including Stablelm2, Mistral, Openchat, and Vicuna, to generate objectionable behaviors, achieving around $90$% ASP. They also indicate that our ignore prefix attacks can break all $\mathbf{14}$ open-source LLMs, achieving over $60$% ASP on a multi-categorical dataset. We find that moderately well-known LLMs exhibit higher vulnerability to prompt injection attacks, highlighting the need to raise public awareness and prioritize efficient mitigation strategies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the research paper "Is Your Prompt Safe? Investigating Prompt Injection Attacks Against Open-Source LLMs."

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the vulnerability of 14 popular open-source Large Language Models (LLMs) to prompt injection attacks.  It evaluates the effectiveness of these attacks using five different benchmark datasets. The authors introduce a novel evaluation metric called Attack Success Probability (ASP), which considers the uncertainty in model responses, going beyond simple binary success/failure evaluations. They propose a new prompt injection attack method called the "hypnotism attack" and compare its effectiveness against baseline attacks like ignore-prefix and role-playing attacks.  The findings suggest that many open-source LLMs are still susceptible to prompt injection attacks, highlighting the urgent need for raising awareness and developing effective mitigation strategies, in contrast to closed-source models like those in the Llama family.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper offers several novel contributions. The ASP metric is a valuable improvement over existing binary success rate metrics because it attempts to quantify the ambiguity and uncertainty in model responses, providing a more nuanced assessment of attack feasibility. The "hypnotism attack," while simple in concept, presents a new way to manipulate LLMs by exploiting cognitive-style influences. The systematic evaluation of 14 open-source LLMs, many of which are less well-studied than models like Llama, is also a significant contribution. Prior work often focuses on defending closed-source LLMs or popular open-source models (Llama, Gemma).  Addressing these lesser-known models widens the scope and relevance of security research.

*   **Significance:**  The research addresses a critical concern in the generative AI community: the security and reliability of widely deployed open-source LLMs. As these models are increasingly used in real-world applications, their vulnerability to prompt injection attacks can have serious consequences. By demonstrating the susceptibility of several popular open-source models, the paper underscores the need for prioritizing security during the development and deployment of these systems. The results should motivate further research into robust defense mechanisms and raise awareness among practitioners.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper provides a systematic and quantitative evaluation of a large set of open-source LLMs using multiple benchmarks.
    *   **Novelty in Evaluation:** The introduction of the ASP metric is a significant step forward in assessing prompt injection attacks.
    *   **New Attack Method:** The "hypnotism attack" adds a novel tactic to the prompt injection arsenal.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The findings are highly relevant to developers and organizations using open-source LLMs.
    *   **Clear and well-organized:** The paper is written in a well-structured and easy-to-follow manner.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simplicity of Hypnotism Attack:** While novel, the hypnotism attack relies on a very simple premise. The generalizability of this attack needs further investigation, with other cognitive biasing tactics or adversarial examples.
    *   **Limited Mitigation Strategies:** While the paper effectively demonstrates vulnerabilities, it only touches briefly on mitigation strategies. Future research could explore more practical defenses against the identified attacks.
    *   **Evaluation Metric Limitations:** The success of attacks is still evaluated via substring matching and human inspection which may be error-prone.
    *   **Lack of Deeper Mechanistic Explanation:** While the paper demonstrates *that* the attacks work, it offers less insight into *why* specific models are more vulnerable than others. This could involve analyzing architecture, training data, or fine-tuning strategies.

*   **Potential Impact:** This work could increase public awareness of open-source LLM vulnerabilities. This could lead to an increased focus on prompt injection attack defense strategies and overall security awareness when deploying LLMs. It could also stimulate additional research to explore better benchmarks, evaluation methods, and defense methods.

*   **Overall:** This paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM security by highlighting the vulnerabilities of open-source models and introducing a new attack and evaluation metric. While it is limited in explaining underlying mechanisms and suggesting detailed mitigation strategies, the paper provides a strong foundation for future research and has clear practical implications.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**
The paper introduces valuable contributions, including the ASP metric and "hypnotism attack," and provides a comprehensive evaluation of open-source LLMs. However, the simplicity of the "hypnotism attack," the limited focus on mitigation strategies, and the lack of in-depth mechanistic explanations temper its overall score. Its relevance to the current state of open-source AI development is substantial.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### AutoRev: Automatic Peer Review System for Academic Research Papers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14376v1)
- **Authors**: Maitreya Prafulla Chitale, Ketaki Mangesh Shetye, Harshit Gupta, Manav Chaudhary, Vasudeva Varma
- **Abstract**: Generating a review for an academic research paper is a complex task that requires a deep understanding of the document's content and the interdependencies between its sections. It demands not only insight into technical details but also an appreciation of the paper's overall coherence and structure. Recent methods have predominantly focused on fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) to address this challenge. However, they often overlook the computational and performance limitations imposed by long input token lengths. To address this, we introduce AutoRev, an Automatic Peer Review System for Academic Research Papers. Our novel framework represents an academic document as a graph, enabling the extraction of the most critical passages that contribute significantly to the review. This graph-based approach demonstrates effectiveness for review generation and is potentially adaptable to various downstream tasks, such as question answering, summarization, and document representation. When applied to review generation, our method outperforms SOTA baselines by an average of 58.72% across all evaluation metrics. We hope that our work will stimulate further research in applying graph-based extraction techniques to other downstream tasks in NLP. We plan to make our code public upon acceptance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AutoRev: Automatic Peer Review System for Academic Research Papers":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AutoRev, an automatic peer review system designed for academic research papers. AutoRev addresses the challenge of generating reviews efficiently by representing a paper as a graph, extracting key passages using a graph neural network (GNN), and then using a fine-tuned large language model (LLM) to generate a structured review. The system aims to overcome the limitations of directly fine-tuning LLMs on entire papers, which can be computationally expensive due to long token lengths. The authors claim that AutoRev outperforms existing baselines in review generation, while also reducing training time.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach by combining graph-based representation and LLMs for automated peer review. While other works have explored graph-based methods and LLMs separately for NLP tasks, their integration for review generation is potentially a valuable contribution. The novelty lies in the particular architecture: the construction of the graph, the use of a GNN for passage selection, and the subsequent use of an LLM. The emphasis on reducing token length for increased efficiency is also significant.

*   **Significance:** Automated peer review systems have the potential to assist researchers and reviewers, streamline the peer review process, and improve the quality of academic publications. AutoRev demonstrates effectiveness in generating structured reviews and provides a system that is more efficient than fine-tuning an LLM on full-length papers. The claimed performance boost compared to baselines is noteworthy. If the system can effectively generate high-quality reviews, it will contribute to increasing the efficiency of peer reviews.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a relevant problem: Efficient and automated peer review generation.
    *   Novel approach: Combines graph representation, GNNs, and LLMs.
    *   Performance: Shows improved scores compared to baselines.
    *   Efficiency: Reduces computational costs by extracting only relevant passages.
    *   Autonomous: Can be used on papers without prior human evaluation.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited to ICLR papers: The system is trained and evaluated primarily on ICLR data, which may limit its generalization to other fields with different paper structures and content styles. The reliance on a specific conference's dataset may affect generalization to other areas.
    *   Bias: The system may inherit and amplify biases present in the training data (existing peer reviews).
    *   Potential for misuse: Over-reliance on AutoRev may undermine the critical thinking central to peer review.
    *   Lack of transparency: The paper acknowledges the need for transparency in AI involvement but does not elaborate on specific safeguards to preserve academic integrity.
*   **Justification for Score:**

    Despite its limitations, AutoRev provides a promising direction for automatic peer review systems. The integration of graph-based representation, GNNs, and LLMs, combined with the focus on efficiency, contributes meaningfully to the field. The system's ability to reduce token length in order to reduce computational costs is a relevant factor. It does have some notable weaknesses: its limited generalizability, its potential to amplify bias from the pre-existing training data, and the risk of it being misused if it is relied on too heavily in peer-review processes. The significance and novelty are also slightly limited by the exclusive reliance on the pre-trained LLMs, which are, in and of themselves, black boxes.
Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### SCAN: Semantic Document Layout Analysis for Textual and Visual Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14381v1)
- **Authors**: Yuyang Dong, Nobuhiro Ueda, Krisztián Boros, Daiki Ito, Takuya Sera, Masafumi Oyamada
- **Abstract**: With the increasing adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs), rich document analysis technologies for applications like Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and visual RAG are gaining significant attention. Recent research indicates that using VLMs can achieve better RAG performance, but processing rich documents still remains a challenge since a single page contains large amounts of information. In this paper, we present SCAN (\textbf{S}emanti\textbf{C} Document Layout \textbf{AN}alysis), a novel approach enhancing both textual and visual Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems working with visually rich documents. It is a VLM-friendly approach that identifies document components with appropriate semantic granularity, balancing context preservation with processing efficiency. SCAN uses a coarse-grained semantic approach that divides documents into coherent regions covering continuous components. We trained the SCAN model by fine-tuning object detection models with sophisticated annotation datasets. Our experimental results across English and Japanese datasets demonstrate that applying SCAN improves end-to-end textual RAG performance by up to 9.0\% and visual RAG performance by up to 6.4\%, outperforming conventional approaches and even commercial document processing solutions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SCAN (SemantiC Document Layout ANalysis), a novel VLM-friendly approach for enhancing both textual and visual Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems working with visually rich documents. SCAN semantically divides document pages into coherent regions ("semantic chunks") covering continuous components to strike a balance between context preservation and processing efficiency for downstream VLMs. The model is trained by fine-tuning object detection models using a newly annotated dataset of over 24k document pages with semantic layout labels. Experiments on English and Japanese datasets demonstrate that SCAN improves end-to-end textual and visual RAG performance, outperforming conventional layout analysis approaches and even commercial document processing solutions. The paper demonstrates that despite requiring more individual VLM processes, SCAN maintains comparable total computation time and reduces total token usage due to a coarse-grained segmentation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the concept of "semantic document layout analysis." While document layout analysis is a well-established field, the paper's focus on creating semantically coherent chunks, as opposed to more granular structural components, is a distinct contribution. The creation and utilization of a dedicated dataset labeled for semantic layouts also adds to the novelty. The adaptive selection strategy for bounding boxes based on coverage and non-overlap is another original component.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the improved RAG performance (both textual and visual) achieved by SCAN. The results demonstrate that pre-processing documents with semantic layout analysis can lead to substantial gains in end-to-end RAG accuracy, surpassing established approaches. The potential for SCAN to enable smaller VLMs to achieve comparable performance to larger ones suggests practical benefits in resource-constrained environments. The findings regarding the superiority of SCAN for preserving spatial relationships, especially in structured content, are also meaningful.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined approach with a specific training methodology and adaptive selection strategy.
    *   Extensive experimental validation across multiple datasets and languages.
    *   Comparison against strong baselines, including commercial systems.
    *   Ablation studies to analyze the impact of different components.
    *   Cost analysis demonstrating improved token efficiency without significant performance slowdown.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper focuses primarily on spatial layout and doesn't address cases where semantically related content is physically separated.
    *   The model is primarily trained on Japanese documents, and despite demonstrating improved performance in English, the model might not be optimal for all languages.
    *   The performance gain on tasks can be tied to the degree to which spatial relationship needs to be preserved by the document, thus it is not generalizable for all documents.
    *   SCAN is not designed for simpler pages, this limits the usability in all contexts.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in document understanding and RAG systems. The concept of semantic layout analysis could inspire new approaches to document pre-processing, leading to more accurate and efficient RAG pipelines. The findings regarding token usage and computation time could also guide the design of more resource-efficient RAG systems.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper tackles an important and relevant problem with a well-executed solution, it has some limitations in generalizability and addresses a rather mature field. The contribution of creating a new training dataset and a different parsing methodology are valuable additions to the field, however.

### Knowledge Graph Based Repository-Level Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14394v1)
- **Authors**: Mihir Athale, Vishal Vaddina
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) have transformed code generation from natural language queries. However, despite their extensive knowledge and ability to produce high-quality code, LLMs often struggle with contextual accuracy, particularly in evolving codebases. Current code search and retrieval methods frequently lack robustness in both the quality and contextual relevance of retrieved results, leading to suboptimal code generation. This paper introduces a novel knowledge graph-based approach to improve code search and retrieval leading to better quality of code generation in the context of repository-level tasks. The proposed approach represents code repositories as graphs, capturing structural and relational information for enhanced context-aware code generation. Our framework employs a hybrid approach for code retrieval to improve contextual relevance, track inter-file modular dependencies, generate more robust code and ensure consistency with the existing codebase. We benchmark the proposed approach on the Evolutionary Code Benchmark (EvoCodeBench) dataset, a repository-level code generation benchmark, and demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms the baseline approach. These findings suggest that knowledge graph based code generation could advance robust, context-sensitive coding assistance tools.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel knowledge graph-based approach to improve code generation in the context of repository-level tasks. It addresses the limitations of Large Language Models (LLMs) which often struggle with contextual accuracy and consistency when applied to evolving codebases.  The framework constructs a knowledge graph from a code repository, capturing structural and relational information (dependencies, usage patterns, hierarchy). A hybrid retrieval system combines full-text semantic searches with graph-based queries to retrieve relevant code context (sub-graphs).  Finally, an LLM uses the retrieved sub-graph to generate code adhering to project structure, coding conventions and dependencies. The authors benchmark their approach on the EvoCodeBench dataset, demonstrating improved performance compared to baseline methods and other graph based models like CodeXGraph.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using knowledge graphs for code generation isn't entirely new, as evidenced by the comparison to CodeXGraph. However, this paper's novelty lies in its specific hybrid retrieval mechanism that combines semantic, lexical and graph based search using n-hop sub-graph traversal. The approach to capture and provide context to LLMs and the specific integration of graph traversal and retrieval systems and hybrid approach to indexing and searching the graph are the key novel parts of the work. Also the evaluation setup with a very detailed evaluation of a complex codebase provides unique contribution to the paper.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a very practical and important problem: how to make LLM-based code generation more context-aware and consistent within existing codebases. The reported improvements on EvoCodeBench, especially compared to baselines that don't use context, suggests a real benefit to this approach. A significant bottleneck of LLM usage in real world software setting is addressed by the paper, demonstrating how a knowledge graph based context extraction and retrieval can contribute to make code generation practical.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly articulates the limitations of LLMs in repository-level code generation.
    *   Well-defined methodology: The knowledge graph construction, hybrid retrieval system, and code generation steps are explained in detail.
    *   Experimental validation: The EvoCodeBench dataset provides a good benchmark, and the reported improvements compared to baselines provide quantitative evidence for the approach's effectiveness.
    *   Clear Comparison: With other Knowledge graph based methods like CodeXGraph which has been shown to have the most effective results currently.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Computational Cost: The paper acknowledges that graph traversal and filtering can be computationally intensive, especially for large repositories. While the approach can potentially retrieve the right data in a way that other algorithms and LLMs cannot, there may be tradeoffs with regard to latency and cost of operations.
    *   Limited Scope of Evaluation: While EvoCodeBench is a good start, it would be beneficial to evaluate the approach on a wider range of repositories and tasks.
    *   Schema Design: The knowledge graph schema seems fairly basic. A more complex schema might capture even more nuanced relationships and improve retrieval accuracy, but it would add more complexity to the overall methodology.
    *   The approach focuses on python programming language and that limits the potential of the work.
    *   Practical issues are not extensively studied. For instance, how easily can this graph be constructed for a real world usecase.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development of more robust and context-aware code generation tools. By providing a structured way to incorporate repository-level context, this approach could reduce the need for manual adjustments and improve the overall efficiency of software development. However, the computational cost and complexity of implementing the framework need to be addressed.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-defined methodology for enhancing LLM-based code generation using a knowledge graph. The experimental results on EvoCodeBench show meaningful improvements over baselines and comparisons with best other state of the art Knowledge Graph based approaches. However, the method needs more complex datasets and more rigorous evaluation of the practical setup and scaling for very large real-world code repositories. While the idea of a graph based indexing on code repository isn't novel, the specific techniques used for implementing knowledge graph indexing and context capturing are novel and contribute to the impact that this paper will have. The paper is very good but not exceptional.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### MUG-Eval: A Proxy Evaluation Framework for Multilingual Generation Capabilities in Any Language
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14395v1)
- **Authors**: Seyoung Song, Seogyeong Jeong, Eunsu Kim, Jiho Jin, Dongkwan Kim, Jay Shin, Alice Oh
- **Abstract**: Evaluating text generation capabilities of large language models (LLMs) is challenging, particularly for low-resource languages where methods for direct assessment are scarce. We propose MUG-Eval, a novel framework that evaluates LLMs' multilingual generation capabilities by transforming existing benchmarks into conversational tasks and measuring the LLMs' accuracies on those tasks. We specifically designed these conversational tasks to require effective communication in the target language. Then, we simply use task success rate as a proxy of successful conversation generation. Our approach offers two key advantages: it is independent of language-specific NLP tools or annotated datasets, which are limited for most languages, and it does not rely on LLMs-as-judges, whose evaluation quality degrades outside a few high-resource languages. We evaluate 8 LLMs across 30 languages spanning high, mid, and low-resource categories, and we find that MUG-Eval correlates strongly with established benchmarks ($r$ > 0.75) while enabling standardized comparisons across languages and models. Our framework provides a robust and resource-efficient solution for evaluating multilingual generation that can be extended to thousands of languages.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the MUG-Eval paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MUG-Eval, a novel framework for evaluating the multilingual generation capabilities of large language models (LLMs). It addresses the challenges of assessing LLMs across diverse languages, especially low-resource ones, where traditional evaluation methods relying on human-annotated data or language-specific NLP tools are limited. MUG-Eval cleverly transforms existing datasets into conversational tasks where two instances of the same LLM communicate in a target language to complete an information-gap scenario. The success rate of these tasks serves as a proxy for generation quality, allowing for language-agnostic evaluation without relying on LLMs-as-judges or native speaker annotations. The authors test MUG-Eval with eight LLMs across 30 languages, demonstrating its correlation with established benchmarks and scalability. The paper also includes an analysis of language resource substitution strategies when native language passages are unavailable.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using self-communication between LLMs to evaluate generation capabilities is interesting and provides a workaround to the common reliance on human-annotated datasets or LLMs-as-judges, which is the most significant contribution. The translation of existing datasets into conversational settings adds another layer of novelty, creating information-gap tasks that require both generation and comprehension. However, the individual tasks used (Easy Twenty Questions, MCQ Conversation, Code Reconstruction) are adaptations of existing benchmarks, so their individual novelty is limited. The exploration of using proxy tasks for more difficult to evaluate generation settings is also helpful.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to enable scalable and cost-effective evaluation of multilingual LLMs, especially for low-resource languages. By removing the need for language-specific NLP tools, human annotations, and relying less on LLMs-as-judges, MUG-Eval offers a more accessible and equitable approach to assessing LLM performance across a broader spectrum of languages. The framework has implications for the development and improvement of multilingual LLMs, encouraging research and development in under-resourced languages. The findings around substituting resources are useful for the community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Language-agnostic design:** The framework's independence from language-specific NLP tools and annotations is a major strength, making it applicable to a vast array of languages.
    *   **Scalability:** MUG-Eval's design allows for easy scaling to thousands of languages, addressing a key limitation of existing evaluation methods.
    *   **Correlation with established benchmarks:** The strong correlation between MUG-Eval and existing benchmarks validates its effectiveness.
    *   **Analysis of language resource substitution:** The analysis of when English or other high-resource languages work as good substitutes for low-resource languages is valuable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on task success as a proxy:** While clever, using task success as a proxy for generation quality might not capture nuanced aspects of generation, such as fluency, style, or cultural appropriateness. The fact that only function code is tested further reduces the confidence.
    *   **Potential biases:** Despite the language-agnostic design, certain aspects of the implementation (e.g., English-centric instructions, code task using Latin script) could introduce biases.
    *   **Limited qualitative evaluation:** The qualitative error analysis, while providing initial validation, could be more extensive and cover a broader range of languages. In-depth evaluation from linguistic experts would further strengthen this.
    *   **Task difficulty:** Certain tasks may show saturation at either low or high, which may affect the granularity to distinguish some models.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   MUG-Eval could become a widely adopted framework for evaluating multilingual LLMs, particularly in low-resource settings.
    *   The paper's findings could inform the development of new multilingual LLMs and evaluation methods, leading to more equitable and accessible AI technologies.
    *   The framework's self-communication approach could inspire other innovative methods for evaluating and improving AI systems.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

MUG-Eval presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of multilingual LLM evaluation. The framework's language-agnostic design and scalability address key limitations of existing methods, making it a valuable tool for researchers and developers working with diverse languages. However, the reliance on task success as a proxy for generation quality, potential biases in implementation, and limited qualitative evaluation prevent a higher score. The reliance on modifying previous benchmarks also affects the novelty score. While MUG-Eval is not without its limitations, its potential to enable scalable, cost-effective, and equitable multilingual LLM evaluation makes it a significant contribution worthy of a rating of 7.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Causal Cartographer: From Mapping to Reasoning Over Counterfactual Worlds
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14396v1)
- **Authors**: Gaël Gendron, Jože M. Rožanec, Michael Witbrock, Gillian Dobbie
- **Abstract**: Causal world models are systems that can answer counterfactual questions about an environment of interest, i.e. predict how it would have evolved if an arbitrary subset of events had been realized differently. It requires understanding the underlying causes behind chains of events and conducting causal inference for arbitrary unseen distributions. So far, this task eludes foundation models, notably large language models (LLMs), which do not have demonstrated causal reasoning capabilities beyond the memorization of existing causal relationships. Furthermore, evaluating counterfactuals in real-world applications is challenging since only the factual world is observed, limiting evaluation to synthetic datasets. We address these problems by explicitly extracting and modeling causal relationships and propose the Causal Cartographer framework. First, we introduce a graph retrieval-augmented generation agent tasked to retrieve causal relationships from data. This approach allows us to construct a large network of real-world causal relationships that can serve as a repository of causal knowledge and build real-world counterfactuals. In addition, we create a counterfactual reasoning agent constrained by causal relationships to perform reliable step-by-step causal inference. We show that our approach can extract causal knowledge and improve the robustness of LLMs for causal reasoning tasks while reducing inference costs and spurious correlations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Causal Cartographer: From Mapping to Reasoning Over Counterfactual Worlds":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the "Causal Cartographer," a two-part framework for enhancing large language models (LLMs) with causal reasoning capabilities. The system consists of: 1) a graph retrieval-augmented generation (graphRAG) agent (CTG-Extract) that extracts causal relationships from real-world news articles to build a causal knowledge graph called "CausalWorld"; and 2) a counterfactual reasoning agent (CTG-Reason) that leverages this causal graph for step-by-step causal inference. The authors demonstrate that their approach can effectively extract causal knowledge, improve LLMs' robustness in causal reasoning tasks, reduce inference costs, and avoid spurious correlations. The paper also introduces the theoretical concept of "causal blankets" and proves its use in estimating counterfactuals, as well as contributes a real-world counterfactual reasoning dataset based on the extracted causal graph.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to address the limitations of LLMs in causal reasoning. The combination of graphRAG for causal knowledge extraction and a step-by-step causal inference agent constrained by the extracted knowledge is a valuable contribution. While previous research has explored causal extraction and RAG, the specific application to building a real-world causal graph for counterfactual reasoning is unique. The theoretical foundation with the notion of causal blankets provides a valuable framework for analysis and evaluation.

**Significance:**

The work addresses a significant problem: the lack of robust causal reasoning in LLMs, especially for counterfactual inference. The practical demonstration of building a causal knowledge graph from real-world data opens avenues for more reliable and explainable AI systems. The reduced inference cost by constraining the LLM with causal information also has important practical implications. It demonstrates a path towards better leveraging existing LLMs while addressing their inherent limitations through structured knowledge. The created CausalWorld dataset also adds a valuable resource for the community.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem definition and motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of LLMs in causal reasoning and the importance of addressing this for robust AI.
*   **Well-defined methodology:** The Causal Cartographer framework is well-explained with clear descriptions of each component.
*   **Empirical validation:** The paper provides empirical evidence through experiments demonstrating the effectiveness of the approach in extracting causal knowledge, improving robustness, and reducing inference costs.
*   **Theoretical grounding:** The introduction and analysis of causal blankets adds a theoretical dimension to the work.
*   **Creation of a valuable dataset:** The CausalWorld dataset, based on real-world news events, provides a challenging and relevant benchmark for counterfactual reasoning.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Source Data Accuracy:** The framework's reliance on the accuracy and lack of bias in the source news articles is a potential weakness. The paper acknowledges this in the limitations. However, a more in-depth discussion of the potential impact of source bias and mitigation strategies is needed.
*   **Limited LLM Generalization:**  The experimental results show some improvements, but the LLMs still show limitations on the task, and better generalization may be needed. In addition, certain LLMs used (llama-3.1) experienced timeouts.
*   **Potential Simplifications:**  The extraction process might overly simplify complex causal relationships.
*   **Causal Blanket Limitations:** Although the Causal Blanket theorem is useful, the conditions surrounding identifiability and what it guarantees are potentially limiting.
*   **Limited Novelty:** The various components of the framework (graph RAG, Chain of thought prompting) are individually well-established. The novelty lies in the composition and application.

**Score:**

**7/10**

**Justification:**

The paper offers a solid contribution to the field by proposing a practical framework for enhancing LLMs with causal reasoning. The combination of graphRAG and step-by-step inference, coupled with the real-world dataset, makes the work relevant and valuable. The empirical results demonstrate some improvement, although performance variations exist between the LLMs tested. The limitations regarding reliance on source data accuracy and the potential simplifications in the extraction process need to be addressed in future work. While the individual components are not entirely novel, the innovative combination and application to counterfactual reasoning make the paper worthy of recognition. Its strengths lie in its practical approach, well-defined methodology, and contributions to the field of causal reasoning and AI. Future work could further enhance the framework by addressing the data bias issue, expanding the types of relationships to be considered, and exploring ways to incorporate uncertainty in the causal graph.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 0/10

### Log-Augmented Generation: Scaling Test-Time Reasoning with Reusable Computation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14398v1)
- **Authors**: Peter Baile Chen, Yi Zhang, Dan Roth, Samuel Madden, Jacob Andreas, Michael Cafarella
- **Abstract**: While humans naturally learn and adapt from past experiences, large language models (LLMs) and their agentic counterparts struggle to retain reasoning from previous tasks and apply them in future contexts. To address this limitation, we propose a novel framework, log-augmented generation (LAG) that directly reuses prior computation and reasoning from past logs at test time to enhance model's ability to learn from previous tasks and perform better on new, unseen challenges, all while keeping the system efficient and scalable. Specifically, our system represents task logs using key-value (KV) caches, encoding the full reasoning context of prior tasks while storing KV caches for only a selected subset of tokens. When a new task arises, LAG retrieves the KV values from relevant logs to augment generation. Our approach differs from reflection-based memory mechanisms by directly reusing prior reasoning and computations without requiring additional steps for knowledge extraction or distillation. Our method also goes beyond existing KV caching techniques, which primarily target efficiency gains rather than improving accuracy. Experiments on knowledge- and reasoning-intensive datasets demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms standard agentic systems that do not utilize logs, as well as existing solutions based on reflection and KV cache techniques.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Log-Augmented Generation: Scaling Test-Time Reasoning with Reusable Computation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework called Log-Augmented Generation (LAG) designed to improve the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) by enabling the reuse of prior computations and reasoning traces at inference time. LAG utilizes Key-Value (KV) caches to represent logs of past reasoning processes, storing the KV values for a selected subset of tokens to retain the essence of the full reasoning context. When a new task arises, LAG retrieves relevant logs based on semantic similarity and integrates the corresponding KV values into the LLM's context. This approach allows the model to leverage past experiences, improve accuracy, and reduce redundant computation. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of LAG through experiments on knowledge-intensive and reasoning-intensive datasets, showing significant improvements over standard agentic systems, reflection-based methods, and existing KV caching techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core idea of reusing past computations to enhance LLM reasoning isn't entirely new. However, the paper's approach of using KV caches to represent reasoning traces and selectively storing KV values for a subset of tokens offers a fresh take. The integration of this KV cache with the LLM in a way that addresses positional embeddings and focuses on contextual information is also a novel aspect. The distinction from reflection-based methods (direct reuse vs. distillation) and other KV caching methods (reasoning trace caching vs instruction/document caching) is clearly articulated, highlighting the innovation. However, the positional encoding fix for KV caching is similar to previous research, so that does detract a bit from novelty.

**Significance:**

The potential impact of LAG is significant. Enhancing LLM reasoning through efficient reuse of prior computations can lead to more accurate, efficient, and scalable AI systems. The experimental results demonstrate substantial improvements in accuracy and efficiency on various datasets. If LAG proves robust and generalizable across more tasks and models, it could become a valuable tool for scaling LLM performance. The demonstrated ability to overcome the limitations of standard agentic systems and even existing reflection techniques indicates a valuable contribution. Also, the ablation and analytical experiments (e.g., varying k, varying what is stored) strengthens the significance by adding credibility.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper effectively identifies the limitation of LLMs in reusing past reasoning and presents LAG as a direct solution.
*   **Novel Approach:** The use of KV caches for reasoning trace representation and selective token storage are innovative elements.
*   **Thorough Evaluation:** The experiments cover a range of datasets, and comprehensive baselines are compared against with a comprehensive suite of ablation tests and experiments.
*   **Well-Written and Structured:** The paper is well-organized and clearly explains the method and its components.
*   **Detailed Algorithm:** Clear pseudocode showing how the approach fits into an agentic pipeline.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Overhead:** While the paper emphasizes efficiency, the overhead of retrieving, processing, and integrating KV caches during inference needs to be carefully considered in real-world deployments. Storing KV values for every token will take a considerable amount of space as well.
*   **Log Relevance:** The paper doesn't explore advanced log retrieval strategies beyond semantic similarity. There may be more intelligent ways to identify relevant logs for a given task.
*   **Limited Scope:** The experiments are limited to specific datasets and a single LLM. Generalizability to other models and tasks needs further investigation.
*   **Potential for Negative Transfer:** There's a risk that irrelevant or incorrect logs could negatively impact the model's performance. How to mitigate negative transfer needs further study.
*   **Static Log Store:** Log quality and curation is also an aspect of improving effectiveness of this approach. The static offline mode will not capture all important reasoning traces that the model can reuse in the future.

**Justification for Score:**

Given its novelty and potential significance, the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field. However, the weaknesses outlined above (computational overhead, limited scope, potential for negative transfer, static log store) prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work. The significance of the work hinges on practical scalability of the approach. Taking these factors into account, a score of 7 is warranted. It's a solid contribution that offers a promising new direction for improving LLM reasoning, but needs further research and refinement to achieve its full potential.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ViC-Bench: Benchmarking Visual-Interleaved Chain-of-Thought Capability in MLLMs with Free-Style Intermediate State Representations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14404v1)
- **Authors**: Xuecheng Wu, Jiaxing Liu, Danlei Huang, Xiaoyu Li, Yifan Wang, Chen Chen, Liya Ma, Xuezhi Cao, Junxiao Xue
- **Abstract**: Visual-Interleaved Chain-of-Thought (VI-CoT) enables MLLMs to continually update their understanding and decisions based on step-wise intermediate visual states (IVS), much like a human would, which demonstrates impressive success in various tasks, thereby leading to emerged advancements in related benchmarks. Despite promising progress, current benchmarks provide models with relatively fixed IVS, rather than free-style IVS, whch might forcibly distort the original thinking trajectories, failing to evaluate their intrinsic reasoning capabilities. More importantly, existing benchmarks neglect to systematically explore the impact factors that IVS would impart to untamed reasoning performance. To tackle above gaps, we introduce a specialized benchmark termed ViC-Bench, consisting of four representive tasks: maze navigation, jigsaw puzzle, embodied long-horizon planning, and complex counting, where each task has dedicated free-style IVS generation pipeline supporting function calls. To systematically examine VI-CoT capability, we propose a thorough evaluation suite incorporating a progressive three-stage strategy with targeted new metrics. Besides, we establish Incremental Prompting Information Injection (IPII) strategy to ablatively explore the prompting factors for VI-CoT. We extensively conduct evaluations for 18 advanced MLLMs, revealing key insights into their VI-CoT capability. Our proposed benchmark is publicly open at Huggingface.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ViC-Bench, a new benchmark for evaluating the visual-interleaved chain-of-thought (VI-CoT) capabilities of multi-modal large language models (MLLMs). ViC-Bench consists of four representative tasks: maze navigation, jigsaw puzzle, embodied long-horizon planning, and complex counting. A key feature of the benchmark is its support for free-style intermediate visual state (IVS) representations, generated through function calls, which contrasts with existing benchmarks that often use fixed IVS. The paper also presents a three-stage evaluation strategy and the Incremental Prompting Information Injection (IPII) strategy to analyze the impact of prompting factors on VI-CoT performance. The authors evaluate 18 advanced MLLMs using ViC-Bench, providing insights into their VI-CoT capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable contribution by addressing the limitations of existing VI-CoT benchmarks. The introduction of free-style IVS is a key advancement, as it allows for a more natural and unbiased evaluation of MLLMs' reasoning abilities. The four selected tasks cover a good range of visual reasoning skills. The IPII strategy is also a novel way to systematically explore the influence of different prompting approaches.

*   **Significance:** The development of ViC-Bench is significant because it provides a more comprehensive and realistic assessment of MLLMs' reasoning capabilities in multi-modal settings. This benchmark has the potential to drive progress in the field by identifying the strengths and weaknesses of current models and by encouraging the development of more robust and flexible VI-CoT approaches. It may be especially useful for evaluating MLLMs operating in embodied AI and robotics contexts. However, the dependence on simulated functions in the IVS generation pipeline (especially for embodied long-horizon planning) may limit the realism of the evaluations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses key limitations in existing VI-CoT benchmarks by introducing free-style IVS.
    *   Offers a diverse set of tasks that cover a range of visual reasoning skills.
    *   Presents a systematic evaluation suite with targeted new metrics.
    *   Introduces IPII strategy for ablative exploration of prompting factors.
    *   Provides extensive experimental results and analyses across multiple MLLMs.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The simulated functions for free-style IVS generation might limit the evaluation's realism, especially in the embodied long-horizon planning task.
    *   Some of the tasks (especially complex counting) still reveal basic perception limitations in many models.
    *   Limited exploration of qualitative analysis on complex planning stages (only on stage 3 for embodied long-horizon planning).

*   **Potential Influence:**  ViC-Bench has the potential to become a standard benchmark for VI-CoT evaluation, influencing the direction of research in this field. The insights gained from the evaluations can guide the development of more effective VI-CoT approaches, ultimately leading to more capable and robust MLLMs. The tool itself could be used by the community for further exploration of the VI-CoT method and MLLM abilities. The careful design may also inspire similar benchmarks in other areas of multi-modal machine learning.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**  The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by introducing a novel and well-designed benchmark for VI-CoT evaluation. The use of free-style IVS is a key advancement that addresses the limitations of existing benchmarks. While there are some weaknesses regarding realism and the dependence on simulated functions for IVS generation, the overall significance and potential influence of the paper justify a high score. It is not rated higher because while the benchmark and evaluation are novel, the high level goal is a fairly standard contribution in machine learning.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Pierce the Mists, Greet the Sky: Decipher Knowledge Overshadowing via Knowledge Circuit Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14406v1)
- **Authors**: Haoming Huang, Yibo Yan, Jiahao Huo, Xin Zou, Xinfeng Li, Kun Wang, Xuming Hu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs), despite their remarkable capabilities, are hampered by hallucinations. A particularly challenging variant, knowledge overshadowing, occurs when one piece of activated knowledge inadvertently masks another relevant piece, leading to erroneous outputs even with high-quality training data. Current understanding of overshadowing is largely confined to inference-time observations, lacking deep insights into its origins and internal mechanisms during model training. Therefore, we introduce PhantomCircuit, a novel framework designed to comprehensively analyze and detect knowledge overshadowing. By innovatively employing knowledge circuit analysis, PhantomCircuit dissects the internal workings of attention heads, tracing how competing knowledge pathways contribute to the overshadowing phenomenon and its evolution throughout the training process. Extensive experiments demonstrate PhantomCircuit's effectiveness in identifying such instances, offering novel insights into this elusive hallucination and providing the research community with a new methodological lens for its potential mitigation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Pierce the Mists, Greet the Sky: Decipher Knowledge Overshadowing via Knowledge Circuit Analysis" introduces PHANTOMCIRCUIT, a novel framework for analyzing and detecting knowledge overshadowing in Large Language Models (LLMs).  Knowledge overshadowing is a type of hallucination where a dominant piece of knowledge masks a more relevant but subordinate piece of knowledge, leading to incorrect outputs. PHANTOMCIRCUIT utilizes knowledge circuit analysis to dissect attention heads, tracing competing knowledge pathways and their evolution during training. The framework correlates the emergence of overshadowing with factors like knowledge popularity, model size, and dataset size. It also proposes a circuit-based method to alleviate overshadowing by optimizing the circuit structure. The paper presents experimental results demonstrating the framework's effectiveness in identifying and potentially mitigating knowledge overshadowing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its application of knowledge circuit analysis to the specific problem of knowledge overshadowing. Existing work has explored knowledge circuits in general, but PHANTOMCIRCUIT innovatively adapts and focuses this technique to understand the *mechanisms* behind this specific type of hallucination. The framework's ability to analyze overshadowing *during training* (rather than solely at inference time) is also a significant contribution. However, the underlying techniques of knowledge circuit analysis and edge attribution patching are borrowed from previous works and are not necessarily invented by this work. The novel use of R-PMI is valuable in this context, however it is just an simplification from previous work.
*   **Significance:** Addressing hallucination in LLMs is a crucial area of research, and knowledge overshadowing is a particularly challenging aspect.  By offering a method to analyze and potentially mitigate this issue, the paper has the potential to improve the reliability and trustworthiness of LLMs. The insights gained could inform training strategies and model architectures to reduce the prevalence of this type of hallucination. The ability to analyze dynamics during the training phase is important.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The framework provides a more mechanistic understanding of knowledge overshadowing than previous observation-based approaches.
    *   The analysis of overshadowing dynamics during training offers valuable insights into how and why this phenomenon occurs.
    *   The proposed circuit-based mitigation strategy is a practical step towards addressing the problem.
    *   Extensive experiments support the framework's effectiveness.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The computational cost of analyzing knowledge circuits, as acknowledged in the limitations, could restrict its applicability to very large models or extensive training regimes.
    *   The focus on a specific type of knowledge overshadowing may limit the generalizability of the findings to other hallucination phenomena. While the focus is valuable, a more comprehensive analysis of potential interactions or overlaps with other hallucination types would be beneficial.
    *   The simplification used for the edge pruning technique appears to be a less robust and less generalizable approach, however, it is a necessary step for applicability.
    *   The datasets are simple synthetic datasets, which limit the generalizability to more complex knowledge that is typically handled by LLMs.

*   **Impact:** If the methods are scalable, the paper provides a potential framework that future work can build upon to create more robust and accurate models. The identification of key internal mechanisms contributing to overshadowing provides actionable insights for model design and training.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:** The paper offers a valuable contribution by applying knowledge circuit analysis to the specific problem of knowledge overshadowing, providing a deeper, mechanistic understanding and potential mitigation strategies.  The analysis of training dynamics and framework application are the most novel aspect of the work. However, it heavily relies on existing techniques (edge attribution patching, knowledge circuit analysis) and the limitations of synthetic datasets and computational costs impact its current practicality. There are concerns about the generalizability. Because of the lack of broader impact, there is nothing truly exceptional about this work. Therefore, a lower-end score is assigned, with an emphasis on the valuable insights, but limited applicability.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Towards Non-Euclidean Foundation Models: Advancing AI Beyond Euclidean Frameworks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14417v1)
- **Authors**: Menglin Yang, Yifei Zhang, Jialin Chen, Melanie Weber, Rex Ying
- **Abstract**: In the era of foundation models and Large Language Models (LLMs), Euclidean space is the de facto geometric setting of our machine learning architectures. However, recent literature has demonstrated that this choice comes with fundamental limitations. To that end, non-Euclidean learning is quickly gaining traction, particularly in web-related applications where complex relationships and structures are prevalent. Non-Euclidean spaces, such as hyperbolic, spherical, and mixed-curvature spaces, have been shown to provide more efficient and effective representations for data with intrinsic geometric properties, including web-related data like social network topology, query-document relationships, and user-item interactions. Integrating foundation models with non-Euclidean geometries has great potential to enhance their ability to capture and model the underlying structures, leading to better performance in search, recommendations, and content understanding. This workshop focuses on the intersection of Non-Euclidean Foundation Models and Geometric Learning (NEGEL), exploring its potential benefits, including the potential benefits for advancing web-related technologies, challenges, and future directions. Workshop page: [https://hyperboliclearning.github.io/events/www2025workshop](https://hyperboliclearning.github.io/events/www2025workshop)
- **Summary**: Okay, I can provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper based on the provided text.

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a workshop focused on "Towards Non-Euclidean Foundation Models," aiming to advance AI beyond the traditional Euclidean frameworks currently dominant in machine learning, particularly in the context of foundation models and large language models (LLMs). It highlights the limitations of Euclidean space for representing complex relationships inherent in web-related data (e.g., social networks, query-document interactions) and argues for the benefits of non-Euclidean geometries (hyperbolic, spherical, etc.) in capturing underlying structures more effectively. The workshop will bring together researchers from diverse backgrounds (non-Euclidean learning, geometric deep learning, web applications) to share knowledge, exchange ideas, and discuss challenges and opportunities in this emerging area. The workshop will feature invited talks, poster sessions, a panel discussion, a breakout session, and contributed talks. The organizers emphasize the relevance of this research to web technologies, aiming to improve search, recommendation systems, social network analysis, and multi-modal web content understanding.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The core idea of exploring non-Euclidean geometries in foundation models is moderately novel. While non-Euclidean representation learning itself isn't new, its application to large-scale *foundation models* and its specific focus on *web-related tasks* represents a potentially valuable direction. Previous works have focused more on generic geometric learning or specific applications, but not on the intersection with foundation models in web technologies.
    *   The emphasis on trustworthiness and robustness within non-Euclidean web-related models is another potentially novel point, as fairness, privacy, and adversarial robustness are critical considerations for real-world web deployments.
    *   However, a truly breakthrough level of novelty would involve presenting specific architectural innovations for incorporating non-Euclidean geometry into existing foundational models or demonstrating substantial performance gains on established benchmarks. The text indicates intentions to encourage those developments, but the workshop proposal itself lacks this.
    *   The connections to past workshops, where DiffGeo4DL was mentioned and held at NeurIPS 2020, reveal that the core geometric learning foundation isn't entirely new. However, the focus on foundation models with new benchmarks is a key differentiator.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The potential significance is high. Foundation models are reshaping AI, and addressing the limitations of Euclidean embeddings is crucial. The focus on web-related applications is particularly relevant, given the scale and complexity of data on the web.
    *   Success in this area could lead to more efficient and effective information retrieval, recommendation systems, social network analysis, and a deeper understanding of multi-modal web content. This would have a broad impact across various web-based industries and applications.
    *   The workshop aims to bridge a gap between theoretical research in non-Euclidean geometry and practical applications in web technologies. By bringing together researchers from different backgrounds, it has the potential to foster new collaborations and accelerate progress in this field.
    *   The focus on new benchmarks is important. Progress in this area is currently hampered by the lack of appropriate, standardized benchmarks for evaluating non-Euclidean foundation models on web-related tasks. Establishing such benchmarks would drive innovation and facilitate comparisons between different approaches.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper is a *workshop proposal*. It outlines goals and topics but does not present original research or concrete results. Its value depends on the quality of submissions and discussions at the workshop itself.
    *   It doesn't delve into specific technical challenges of adapting foundation models to non-Euclidean spaces.
    *   The "novelty" relies on the *potential* of the workshop rather than any immediately demonstrated innovation.
    *   The success of the workshop hinges on the quality and participation of researchers in the respective fields. Without strong contributions and active engagement, the impact may be limited.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The proposal is well-motivated, clearly outlining the limitations of current Euclidean approaches for modeling complex web data.
    *   It identifies a relevant and important research direction.
    *   The workshop format is well-structured, including a good mix of invited talks, poster sessions, panel discussions, and contributed talks.
    *   The diversity and inclusion statement adds value.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the above points, the paper warrants a score of **6**.

*   It presents a reasonably novel and significant workshop that is in line with the conference goals.
*   It falls short of a higher score because it's a workshop proposal, not a research paper with demonstrated results. The novelty and significance are *potential* rather than *actual*. It has a high degree of dependence on the success and quality of the workshop.
*   It goes above a "5" because it is well-motivated, tackles an important problem with significant impact if achieved, and has a diverse team and set of organizers.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Hidden Ghost Hand: Unveiling Backdoor Vulnerabilities in MLLM-Powered Mobile GUI Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14418v1)
- **Authors**: Pengzhou Cheng, Haowen Hu, Zheng Wu, Zongru Wu, Tianjie Ju, Daizong Ding, Zhuosheng Zhang, Gongshen Liu
- **Abstract**: Graphical user interface (GUI) agents powered by multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have shown greater promise for human-interaction. However, due to the high fine-tuning cost, users often rely on open-source GUI agents or APIs offered by AI providers, which introduces a critical but underexplored supply chain threat: backdoor attacks. In this work, we first unveil that MLLM-powered GUI agents naturally expose multiple interaction-level triggers, such as historical steps, environment states, and task progress. Based on this observation, we introduce AgentGhost, an effective and stealthy framework for red-teaming backdoor attacks. Specifically, we first construct composite triggers by combining goal and interaction levels, allowing GUI agents to unintentionally activate backdoors while ensuring task utility. Then, we formulate backdoor injection as a Min-Max optimization problem that uses supervised contrastive learning to maximize the feature difference across sample classes at the representation space, improving flexibility of the backdoor. Meanwhile, it adopts supervised fine-tuning to minimize the discrepancy between backdoor and clean behavior generation, enhancing effectiveness and utility. Extensive evaluations of various agent models in two established mobile benchmarks show that AgentGhost is effective and generic, with attack accuracy that reaches 99.7\% on three attack objectives, and shows stealthiness with only 1\% utility degradation. Furthermore, we tailor a defense method against AgentGhost that reduces the attack accuracy to 22.1\%. Our code is available at \texttt{anonymous}.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will summarize the paper and provide a critical evaluation with a justified score.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "AgentGhost," a novel framework for backdoor attacks against multimodal large language model (MLLM)-powered graphical user interface (GUI) agents. The framework leverages the observation that GUI agents naturally expose multiple interaction-level triggers (historical steps, environment states, and task progress) that can be combined with goal-level triggers to activate backdoors stealthily. AgentGhost formulates backdoor injection as a Min-Max optimization problem, using supervised contrastive learning to maximize feature differences between poisoned and clean samples while minimizing discrepancies between backdoor and clean behavior generation through supervised fine-tuning. The authors evaluate AgentGhost on two established mobile benchmarks, demonstrating high attack accuracy (up to 99.7%) with minimal utility degradation (around 1%). They also tailor an existing defense method that significantly reduces attack accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a previously underexplored threat model: backdoor attacks in MLLM-powered GUI agents. While backdoor attacks are not new in general ML, their application in the context of interactive GUI agents controlled by MLLMs presents a unique challenge. Combining interaction and goal-level triggers to stealthily activate backdoors within episodes is a novel contribution. Also, formulating the attack as a min-max optimization with supervised contrastive learning and supervised fine-tuning to orthogonolize the poisoned and normal behaviour is a new take on backdoor attacks.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant because they highlight a critical vulnerability in a rapidly developing field. MLLM-powered GUI agents are becoming increasingly prevalent, and the reliance on open-source models and third-party APIs makes them susceptible to supply chain attacks. The paper demonstrates the feasibility and effectiveness of such attacks, raising important security concerns. The paper also evaluates some existing defenses, demonstrating their limitations, which further emphasises the importance of the problem.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clearly defined threat model and attack framework.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation on established benchmarks.
    *   High attack accuracy with minimal utility degradation.
    *   Discussion of potential defenses.
    *   Addressing the Stealthiness of the attack.
    *   The idea of combining multiple triggers (goal-level and interaction-level) for stealth and effectiveness.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The evaluation relies on simulation of user behavior, rather than real-world user interactions. It’s hard to test the effectiveness or how the interaction-level triggers would behave.
    *   While the paper mentions the types of "malicious" actions (e.g., privacy leakage), the examples are somewhat limited. Exploring more diverse and impactful backdoor objectives would strengthen the analysis.
    *   The defensive strategy was not the focus. There is very little discussion about different defense methods.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to stimulate further research on backdoor attacks and defenses in MLLM-powered GUI agents. It also encourages more rigorous security evaluations of these systems and the development of more robust mitigation strategies.

**Justification:**

The paper addresses a relevant security problem in the context of a rapidly evolving area of research, MLLM-powered GUI agents. It presents a novel attack framework with impressive performance characteristics and raises important security considerations. While the evaluation and threat model can be further improved, it presents a significant contribution to the security field.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### MindVote: How LLMs Predict Human Decision-Making in Social Media Polls
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14422v1)
- **Authors**: Xutao Mao, Ezra Xuanru Tao
- **Abstract**: The increasing complexity of Large Language Models (LLMs) necessitates new benchmarks to assess their ability to predict human decision-making in dynamic social contexts. We introduce MindVote, the first benchmark for evaluating LLMs as "virtual respondents" in social media polling. MindVote comprises 276 poll instances with 1,142 data entry points from three platforms (Weibo, Reddit, Fizz), features bilingual content (Chinese/English), and covers five domains. Our evaluation of 18 LLMs demonstrates that top-performing models achieve an overall score of 0.74, an 80% relative improvement over traditional baselines, and then we analyze LLM world model bias with human preferences across societal bias dimensions. MindVote also uncovers significant disparities related to platform, language, and domain. We present strategies to optimize LLM performance and use LLM-as-a-Judge to assess reasoning in societal contexts. Furthermore, we show that temperature controls can reflect a way of human thinking diversity and opinion shifts in polling. In summary, MindVote offers a scalable framework for evaluating LLMs' social intelligence, with implications for understanding behavioral decision-making. Code and data will be available soon.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MindVote: How LLMs Predict Human Decision-Making in Social Media Polls":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces *MindVote*, a new benchmark for evaluating the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to predict human decision-making in dynamic social media poll contexts. It addresses a critical gap in existing LLM evaluations, which often lack focus on the complexities of social media environments (platform biases, peer influence, informal language).  MindVote comprises a dataset of 276 poll instances from Weibo, Reddit, and Fizz, featuring bilingual content and spanning five domains. The authors evaluated 18 LLMs, optimized LLM performance using various prompting strategies, analyzed world model bias, and explored the effect of temperature control to simulate diverse thinking. They found that top-performing LLMs achieved an overall score of 0.74, a significant improvement over traditional baselines, and identified disparities related to platform, language, and domain.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Strength:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation of a specialized benchmark, MindVote, specifically designed to assess LLMs' *social intelligence* in the context of social media polls. This is a distinct departure from general reasoning or knowledge-based benchmarks. The application of Multiple Discrete-Continuous Choice (MDC) theory to model LLM preferences is a good idea to facilitate a more thorough analysis for LLM preferences.
    *   **Weakness:** While the idea of using LLMs as "virtual respondents" is conceptually interesting, the operationalization and validation of this concept could be stronger. The connection between the LLM's hidden state (token-based model) and the supposed operators they're inferring isn't clearly established theoretically. The argument on the novelty about LLMs are reliable navigate complex human preference landscapes is weakly supported.
    *   **Critical Point:** The dataset's multi-platform, multi-lingual, and multi-domain nature adds to its value, though the inherent biases within those platforms also represent a challenge in the evaluation.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Strength:** The paper addresses a very relevant and increasingly important topic: the role of AI agents in influencing social feedback mechanisms like voting. The identification of platform and language biases is crucial for understanding the potential impact of LLMs on real-world decision-making. The experiment results and the exploration of reasoning and Chain-of-Thought are interesting directions.
    *   **Weakness:**  The connection between the *MindVote* benchmark and real-world consequences could be elaborated further. For example, demonstrating how biases in LLM-generated poll predictions could lead to tangible negative outcomes in specific scenarios would strengthen the paper's significance. The limitations section mentions LLM performance variations which indicates that the capabilities observed may not be universally applicable to all LLMs or all contexts which limits the influence on the field.
    *   **Critical Point:**  The paper's insights into LLM biases and the impact of composition operators (platform, language, domain) are potentially significant for researchers and practitioners developing and deploying LLMs in socially sensitive contexts.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The benchmark is well-motivated and fills a gap in existing LLM evaluation resources.
    *   The dataset is reasonably diverse, covering multiple platforms, languages, and domains.
    *   The experimental design is comprehensive, evaluating a range of LLMs and prompting strategies.
    *   The analysis of LLM biases and the impact of composition operators is valuable.
    *   The investigation of temperature control for simulating thinking diversity is an interesting avenue.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The theoretical grounding connecting LLM hidden states to social behavior operators could be strengthened.
    *   The connection to real-world impact needs further elaboration.
    *   The validation of using LLMs as "virtual respondents" requires more rigorous justification.
    *   Some of the performance gains from optimization strategies are incremental.
    *   The study is limited by the absence of GPT-4.5's evaluation.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The MindVote benchmark could become a valuable tool for the research community to assess and improve the social intelligence of LLMs.
    *   The identified biases could inform the development of debiasing techniques for LLMs.
    *   The insights into composition operators could guide the design of more effective LLM applications in social contexts.
    *   The findings could spark further research into the relationship between LLM reasoning capabilities and human decision-making.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7/10**.

**Rationale:** This paper makes a solid contribution by introducing a novel benchmark that focuses on the under-explored area of LLM social intelligence in the context of social media polls. The identification and analysis of biases and composition operators provides useful insights. The investigation of temperature control as a way to reflect thinking diversity is promising. However, the theoretical connections between LLM inner workings and the inferred social behavior operators need to be more robustly justified. And the absence of state-of-the-art model evaluation and a stronger linkage to real-world consequences limits its immediate impact. It's a valuable contribution but with room for substantial improvement and further research.

- **Classification**: cs.SI
- **Score**: 0/10

### From Templates to Natural Language: Generalization Challenges in Instruction-Tuned LLMs for Spatial Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14425v1)
- **Authors**: Chalamalasetti Kranti, Sherzod Hakimov, David Schlangen
- **Abstract**: Instruction-tuned large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on a variety of tasks; however, generalizing from synthetic to human-authored instructions in grounded environments remains a challenge for them. In this work, we study generalization challenges in spatial grounding tasks where models interpret and translate instructions for building object arrangements on a $2.5$D grid. We fine-tune LLMs using only synthetic instructions and evaluate their performance on a benchmark dataset containing both synthetic and human-written instructions. Our results reveal that while models generalize well on simple tasks, their performance degrades significantly on more complex tasks. We present a detailed error analysis of the gaps in instruction generalization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, aiming for rigor and clarity:

**Summary:**

The paper "From Templates to Natural Language: Generalization Challenges in Instruction-Tuned LLMs for Spatial Reasoning" investigates the ability of instruction-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs) to generalize from synthetic, template-based instructions to human-authored instructions in spatial reasoning tasks.  The authors fine-tune LLMs using only synthetic instructions and evaluate their performance on a dataset containing both synthetic and human-written instructions for building structures on a 2.5D grid.  Their key findings reveal that while LLMs can perform well on simpler tasks with synthetic instructions, their performance degrades significantly on more complex tasks and with human-authored instructions.  The paper identifies referential ambiguity, especially in scenarios with repetitive objects, as a critical bottleneck for generalization. They also perform cross-domain evaluation on TidyBot.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its focused and controlled study of generalization challenges specific to spatial reasoning tasks, using a clearly defined dataset with both synthetic and human-authored instructions. While the general problem of generalization from synthetic to natural data is well-known, this paper provides a deeper, more granular analysis within the context of instruction-following for grounded tasks.
