The error analysis and focus on the impact of referential ambiguity are also valuable contributions.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it highlights the limitations of current instruction-tuning approaches when applied to grounded tasks requiring spatial reasoning. The findings underscore the need for developing more robust training strategies that can bridge the gap between highly structured synthetic data and the nuances of natural language. The observations about referential ambiguity are particularly important for informing the design of future training datasets and model architectures. It also highlights that transfer to out-of-domain instructions (like Tidybot) may be improved when instruction tuning on well-structured, synthetic spatial tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined Experiment:** The experimental setup is clear, controlled, and allows for direct comparison between different instruction types and model architectures.
    *   **Focused Analysis:** The paper focuses on specific generalization challenges, rather than offering a broad overview, which enables a more detailed and insightful analysis.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The qualitative error analysis provides valuable insights into the types of mistakes the models are making, helping to identify areas for improvement.
    *   **Cross-Domain Evaluation:** The cross-domain experiment on TidyBot further validates the findings and demonstrates the potential for transfer learning from synthetic spatial tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Synthetic Data:** The synthetic instructions are generated using a relatively simple template-based approach, which may not fully capture the complexity of real-world language.
    *   **Task Specificity:**  The results are specific to a particular type of spatial reasoning task (structure building on a grid), and it's unclear how well the findings would generalize to other types of grounded tasks or environments.
    *   **Fine-Tuning Procedure Details:** More detail could have been provided regarding the specific prompts and training procedures used for fine-tuning the LLMs.
    *   **Model Selection:** The paper evaluates a range of LLMs, but a more thorough justification for the specific models chosen and the hyperparameters used could have strengthened the analysis.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in instruction-following, grounded language understanding, and spatial reasoning. It provides a clear roadmap for investigating the challenges of generalization in these domains and offers specific insights that can inform the development of more robust and adaptable models. The findings regarding referential ambiguity could also lead to new approaches for generating more informative training data and improving the ability of LLMs to handle complex instructions.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of 7 because the paper is well-executed, provides valuable insights into the limitations of instruction-tuned LLMs for spatial reasoning tasks, and has the potential to influence future research in this area. While the scope of the study is somewhat limited by the task and the simplicity of the synthetic data, the focused analysis and error analysis provide a strong foundation for further investigation. The paper’s contribution is solid but not transformative enough to warrant a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Rank-K: Test-Time Reasoning for Listwise Reranking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14432v1)
- **Authors**: Eugene Yang, Andrew Yates, Kathryn Ricci, Orion Weller, Vivek Chari, Benjamin Van Durme, Dawn Lawrie
- **Abstract**: Retrieve-and-rerank is a popular retrieval pipeline because of its ability to make slow but effective rerankers efficient enough at query time by reducing the number of comparisons. Recent works in neural rerankers take advantage of large language models for their capability in reasoning between queries and passages and have achieved state-of-the-art retrieval effectiveness. However, such rerankers are resource-intensive, even after heavy optimization. In this work, we introduce Rank-K, a listwise passage reranking model that leverages the reasoning capability of the reasoning language model at query time that provides test time scalability to serve hard queries. We show that Rank-K improves retrieval effectiveness by 23\% over the RankZephyr, the state-of-the-art listwise reranker, when reranking a BM25 initial ranked list and 19\% when reranking strong retrieval results by SPLADE-v3. Since Rank-K is inherently a multilingual model, we found that it ranks passages based on queries in different languages as effectively as it does in monolingual retrieval.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Rank-K," a listwise passage reranking model that leverages test-time reasoning. It fine-tunes a QwQ 32B model with knowledge distillation from a DeepSeek R1 model, using explicit ranking instructions.  Rank-K generates a reasoning trace to analyze and compare passages for improved ranking accuracy. The authors demonstrate that Rank-K outperforms the state-of-the-art listwise reranker, RankZephyr, on English passages and exhibits strong cross-language ranking abilities via zero-shot transfer. They release the training data (reasoning traces and rankings), the Rank-K model, and supporting scripts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *integration of test-time reasoning into a listwise reranking model*. Existing generative rerankers focus on directly producing rankings without explicit reasoning. Rank-K attempts to mimic human-like reasoning about relevance, offering a potential pathway to more interpretable and accurate results. Using knowledge distillation to transfer reasoning capabilities from a larger model (DeepSeek R1) to a smaller one (QwQ 32B) is not novel in itself, but its specific application to listwise reranking with explicit ranking instructions is a key contribution.
    The sliding window approach used in Rank-K isn't new either, but the combination of this approach with knowledge distillation applied to listwise reranking with the goal of supporting query-time reasoning is a novel combination.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate. The results show improved retrieval effectiveness compared to RankZephyr, demonstrating the potential of test-time reasoning. The gains on TREC DL datasets are smaller, but the robustness shown on translated NeuCLIR data points towards better generalization. The release of the training data and model could encourage further research in this direction. The cross-language transfer results are also promising, given the increasing importance of multilingual information access. However, it should also be noted that most experiments are conducted on the top 20 passages retrieved by BM25 or SPLADE-v3. The end-to-end retrieval performance might be different in real-world scenarios where the initial ranking is far from ideal. Also, the compute costs involved are significant.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined methodology.
    *   Demonstrated improvements over a strong baseline (RankZephyr).
    *   Release of resources to facilitate further research.
    *   Experiments with cross-language retrieval.
    *   Analysis of ranking progression during reasoning.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   High computational cost (requires large language models). The practicality of deployment in real-world scenarios is questionable without significant optimizations. While better than a larger model (DeepSeek R1), a model with this type of footprint may not be suitable in all application areas.
    *   The gains on TREC DL are incremental, suggesting the benefits might be limited for datasets already heavily optimized for.
    *   Limited exploration of alternative reasoning prompts or distillation techniques. The study could benefit from an ablation study of the reasoning components specifically.
    *   The study could benefit from an end-to-end retrieval comparison to gauge more realistic gains in a real-world search scenario.
    *   Significant cost of data generation (USD$1,900 for 100k samples using Kluster.ai) needs to be taken into account.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by:

    *   Encouraging the development of more efficient methods for incorporating reasoning into reranking models.
    *   Inspiring the exploration of alternative distillation techniques for transferring reasoning abilities.
    *   Motivating the creation of new datasets and benchmarks specifically designed to evaluate reasoning in retrieval.
    *   Spurring interest in cross-language transfer for generative rerankers.
    *   Driving the creation of better prompting strategies for reasoning listwise rankers

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel approach to listwise reranking using test-time reasoning, but the incremental gains on some benchmarks and the high computational cost limit its immediate impact. However, the release of resources and the promising cross-language results warrant a score above average. The idea of incorporating reasoning is solid, and the paper is well-executed, but the limited practicality and modest gains (in some scenarios) prevent a higher score. The limitations of deploying a 32B parameter model given the results obtained has to be taken into account. The paper is a decent contribution that introduces an interesting direction but needs further optimization to be more widely applicable.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Choosing a Model, Shaping a Future: Comparing LLM Perspectives on Sustainability and its Relationship with AI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14435v1)
- **Authors**: Annika Bush, Meltem Aksoy, Markus Pauly, Greta Ontrup
- **Abstract**: As organizations increasingly rely on AI systems for decision support in sustainability contexts, it becomes critical to understand the inherent biases and perspectives embedded in Large Language Models (LLMs). This study systematically investigates how five state-of-the-art LLMs -- Claude, DeepSeek, GPT, LLaMA, and Mistral - conceptualize sustainability and its relationship with AI. We administered validated, psychometric sustainability-related questionnaires - each 100 times per model -- to capture response patterns and variability. Our findings revealed significant inter-model differences: For example, GPT exhibited skepticism about the compatibility of AI and sustainability, whereas LLaMA demonstrated extreme techno-optimism with perfect scores for several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). Models also diverged in attributing institutional responsibility for AI and sustainability integration, a results that holds implications for technology governance approaches. Our results demonstrate that model selection could substantially influence organizational sustainability strategies, highlighting the need for awareness of model-specific biases when deploying LLMs for sustainability-related decision-making.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how five state-of-the-art Large Language Models (LLMs) – Claude, DeepSeek, GPT, LLaMA, and Mistral – conceptualize sustainability and its relationship with AI. It uses validated psychometric questionnaires related to sustainability administered repeatedly to each model.  The study reveals significant inter-model differences. For instance, GPT showed skepticism about the compatibility of AI and sustainability, while LLaMA exhibited extreme techno-optimism.  Models also differed in attributing institutional responsibility for AI and sustainability integration. The authors argue that model selection can significantly influence organizational sustainability strategies, emphasizing the need for awareness of model-specific biases when deploying LLMs for sustainability-related decision-making.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addressing a Critical Gap:** The paper tackles a crucial and timely issue: understanding the biases and perspectives embedded within LLMs concerning sustainability, particularly as organizations increasingly use AI for decision-making in this domain.  The focus on "twin transition" -- the intersection of AI and sustainability -- is particularly relevant.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The use of validated psychometric instruments is a strength. The study's attempts at standardization through careful prompting and the replication of questionnaires 100 times for each model to address variability is commendable. The use of nonparametric statistical tests is appropriate given the unknown distributions of the LLM responses.
    *   **Clear Findings:** The results are clearly presented and reveal significant, actionable differences between the models. The specific examples (GPT's skepticism vs. LLaMA's techno-optimism, variations in SDG assessments) are insightful. The analysis of institutional responsibility and trust adds another layer of understanding.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper directly addresses the practical implications of model selection for organizational sustainability strategies, making it relevant for practitioners and policymakers.
    *   **Extensive Literature Review:** The authors provide a comprehensive overview of the existing research on LLMs and sustainability, positioning their work within the broader context of the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Generalizability of "Attitudes":** The reliance on psychometric instruments designed for *human* attitudes introduces a significant challenge to validity. The authors acknowledge this in the limitations section, yet the entire premise rests on interpreting LLM responses as analogous to human attitudes or beliefs. While the models produce statistically different outputs, to what extent this can be interpreted as 'bias' or 'perspective' is questionable.
    *   **Lack of Mechanistic Explanation:** While the paper identifies *differences* in responses across models, it doesn't provide a clear *explanation* of why these differences exist.  The suggestions about training data being the source of biases are plausible, but the study doesn't empirically test this. A deeper dive into the specific training data or model architectures that contribute to these variations would strengthen the analysis.
    *   **Superficial Exploratory Questions:** The two exploratory questions regarding institutional responsibility and confidence, while interesting, are analyzed in a relatively shallow way. The multiple-choice responses provide an overview, but deeper, perhaps qualitative, analysis into the *reasons* behind the models' choices would be beneficial.
    *   **English-Centric Approach:** The reliance on English-language questionnaires limits the generalizability of the findings to different cultural and linguistic contexts. The impact of cultural nuances on sustainability perspectives is a potentially rich area for further exploration, particularly when considering the different training datasets for different LLMs.
    *   **Snapshot in Time:** The rapidly evolving nature of LLMs is a major limitation. The findings may be outdated quickly as models are updated or retrained. This inherent instability lowers the longevity of the contribution.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The paper *is* novel in its systematic application of psychometric instruments to compare LLMs' perspectives on sustainability.  While others have explored LLMs and sustainability, the rigorous, comparative, and quantitative approach is a valuable contribution.
    *   The study's significance lies in highlighting the potentially substantial impact of model selection on organizational sustainability strategies. This realization is crucial for responsible AI deployment. However, the study provides little insight into what can be done about the issue, other than selecting LLMs carefully.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite the strengths of the paper's methodological approach and clear presentation of findings, its overall novelty and long-term significance are limited by the fundamental questions about the validity of using human-centric psychometric methods on AI systems. The lack of mechanistic explanations for the observed differences and the rapidly evolving nature of LLMs further reduce the paper's potential impact. While the paper serves as a good 'awareness raiser' about the issue, it provides little guidance beyond selecting LLMs carefully. The analysis of responsibility attribution is interesting but too shallow to be a major contribution.

Score: 6

The paper is a solid contribution that provides valuable, though limited, insight and raises important questions for further research. With better methodology and more insight it could be much more valuable.

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 6/10

### Neural Incompatibility: The Unbridgeable Gap of Cross-Scale Parametric Knowledge Transfer in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14436v1)
- **Authors**: Yuqiao Tan, Shizhu He, Kang Liu, Jun Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a transparent brain with accessible parameters that encode extensive knowledge, which can be analyzed, located and transferred. Consequently, a key research challenge is to transcend traditional knowledge transfer paradigms rooted in symbolic language and achieve genuine Parametric Knowledge Transfer (PKT). Significantly, exploring effective methods for transferring knowledge across LLMs of different scales through parameters presents an intriguing and valuable research direction. In this paper, we first demonstrate $\textbf{Alignment}$ in parametric space is the fundamental prerequisite to achieve successful cross-scale PKT. We redefine the previously explored knowledge transfer as Post-Align PKT (PostPKT), which utilizes extracted parameters for LoRA initialization and requires subsequent fine-tune for alignment. Hence, to reduce cost for further fine-tuning, we introduce a novel Pre-Align PKT (PrePKT) paradigm and propose a solution called $\textbf{LaTen}$ ($\textbf{L}$oc$\textbf{a}$te-$\textbf{T}$h$\textbf{e}$n-Alig$\textbf{n}$) that aligns the parametric spaces of LLMs across scales only using several training steps without following training. Comprehensive experiments on four benchmarks demonstrate that both PostPKT and PrePKT face challenges in achieving consistently stable transfer. Through in-depth analysis, we identify $\textbf{Neural Incompatibility}$ as the ethological and parametric structural differences between LLMs of varying scales, presenting fundamental challenges to achieving effective PKT. These findings provide fresh insights into the parametric architectures of LLMs and highlight promising directions for future research on efficient PKT. Our code is available at https://github.com/Trae1ounG/Neural_Incompatibility.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Neural Incompatibility: The Unbridgeable Gap of Cross-Scale Parametric Knowledge Transfer in Large Language Models" investigates the challenges of transferring knowledge directly through parameters (Parametric Knowledge Transfer or PKT) from larger to smaller LLMs. It argues that *alignment* in the parametric space is crucial for successful PKT.  The paper introduces a novel Pre-Align PKT paradigm and a method called LaTen (Locate-Then-Align) to facilitate this alignment *before* knowledge injection. The results indicate that both Post-Align PKT (aligning after injection) and Pre-Align PKT face challenges in achieving stable and consistent transfer due to what the authors define as "Neural Incompatibility," stemming from ethological and parametric structural differences between LLMs of different scales. The paper provides insights into the parametric architectures of LLMs and highlights directions for future research on efficient PKT.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's core contribution lies in explicitly defining the problem of cross-scale parametric knowledge transfer and introducing the concept of "Neural Incompatibility." The distinction between Post-Align and Pre-Align PKT is insightful and provides a clearer framework for future research. LaTen, while not revolutionary in its techniques (using neuron attribution and hypernetworks), represents a novel attempt to address the alignment challenge proactively. However, neuron attribution and hypernetworks aren't very new techniques used in LLMs. The novelty is in the combination and application to this specific problem of cross-scale transfer.

**Significance:**

The work is significant because it addresses a practical and important problem: efficiently leveraging the knowledge of larger, more expensive LLMs to improve smaller, more deployable models. Current approaches to knowledge transfer often rely on language-based distillation, which may not fully capture the richness of the larger model's internal representations. While the paper doesn't provide a complete solution, it makes a valuable contribution by:

1.  **Framing the problem:**  Clearly defining the problem of cross-scale PKT and highlighting the importance of alignment.
2.  **Introducing a Pre-Align paradigm:** Providing a novel perspective that contrasts with existing Post-Align approaches.
3.  **Identifying "Neural Incompatibility":**  Suggesting a fundamental limitation that needs to be addressed for effective PKT. This shifts the focus from simple parameter transfer to understanding the underlying structural differences between LLMs.
4.  **Proposing LaTen:** Providing a concrete method, although not perfect, to address Pre-Alignment, and providing future research for other scientists to utilize.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper articulates the problem of cross-scale PKT very well.
*   **Comprehensive Experimental Setup:** The paper investigates various scenarios with different types of knowledge (world knowledge, reasoning, coding) and compares against relevant baselines.
*   **Insightful Analysis:** The analysis of "Neural Incompatibility" is a valuable contribution, even if it's more of an observation than a fully-developed theory.
*   **Good writing quality:** The paper is generally written to be well understood, even to individuals with less familiarity with LLMs.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Success of LaTen:** While LaTen shows promise, it does not consistently outperform other methods, and the paper acknowledges its limitations (e.g., sensitivity to checkpoint selection).
*   **"Neural Incompatibility" is somewhat high-level:** While insightful, the concept of "Neural Incompatibility" lacks concrete actionable solutions. Further research is needed to understand the specific structural properties that cause this incompatibility and how to overcome them.
*   **Techniques aren't new:** While the paradigm is novel, some of the techniques utilized aren't groundbreaking. For example, neuron attribution has been studied for years and years.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper's focus on alignment and "Neural Incompatibility" could influence future research in several ways:

*   **Encourage more investigation into LLM structure:**  Researchers may focus on understanding the underlying architectural differences between LLMs of different scales and how these differences affect knowledge representation and transfer.
*   **Drive the development of new alignment techniques:** The need for better alignment techniques will likely stimulate research into methods that can effectively map the parameter spaces of different LLMs.
*   **Inspire new transfer learning paradigms:**  The Pre-Align PKT paradigm could serve as a basis for developing entirely new approaches to knowledge transfer that move beyond simple parameter initialization.

**Justification for the score:**

While the paper identifies an important problem and provides valuable insights, the limited success of LaTen and the somewhat high-level nature of the "Neural Incompatibility" concept prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. The novelty lies in defining the problem and paradigm, and it isn't using novel techniques that contribute as much value. The paper makes a significant contribution to the *understanding* of the problem, but less so in terms of providing a practical *solution*. It is more exploratory. Therefore, a score of 6 is appropriate, as it acknowledges the paper's significance in framing the problem and directing future research, while also recognizing its limitations.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### S2SBench: A Benchmark for Quantifying Intelligence Degradation in Speech-to-Speech Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14438v1)
- **Authors**: Yuanbo Fang, Haoze Sun, Jun Liu, Tao Zhang, Zenan Zhou, Weipeng Chen, Xiaofen Xing, Xiangmin Xu
- **Abstract**: End-to-end speech large language models ((LLMs)) extend the capabilities of text-based models to directly process and generate audio tokens. However, this often leads to a decline in reasoning and generation performance compared to text input, a phenomenon referred to as intelligence degradation. To systematically evaluate this gap, we propose S2SBench, a benchmark designed to quantify performance degradation in Speech LLMs. It includes diagnostic datasets targeting sentence continuation and commonsense reasoning under audio input. We further introduce a pairwise evaluation protocol based on perplexity differences between plausible and implausible samples to measure degradation relative to text input. We apply S2SBench to analyze the training process of Baichuan-Audio, which further demonstrates the benchmark's effectiveness. All datasets and evaluation code are available at https://github.com/undobug/S2SBench.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces S2SBench, a benchmark designed to quantify the intelligence degradation observed when large language models (LLMs) are extended to process speech directly (end-to-end speech LLMs) compared to text. S2SBench includes diagnostic datasets focused on sentence continuation and commonsense reasoning, and proposes a pairwise evaluation method based on perplexity differences between plausible and implausible samples. The benchmark is used to analyze the training process of Baichuan-Audio, demonstrating its ability to identify performance gaps and guide model improvement. The datasets and evaluation code are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in explicitly focusing on and quantifying *intelligence degradation* in end-to-end speech LLMs. While other works have touched upon the performance difference between speech and text input, S2SBench provides a dedicated benchmark for measuring this specific degradation. The pairwise evaluation method using perplexity differences is a reasonable approach for assessing this degradation. The creation of the zh-sStoryCloze dataset provides a Chinese language resource tailored for this type of analysis. The construction of the 'sCMMLU' dataset based on GPT-4 re-writing the CMMLU questions, is also noteworthy, although the reliance on GPT-4 introduces its own biases and may not perfectly capture commonsense knowledge.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in the development of end-to-end speech LLMs. The ability to accurately quantify intelligence degradation is crucial for developing better models and understanding the trade-offs involved in directly processing audio. By releasing the benchmark, the authors provide a valuable tool for the research community to evaluate and compare different architectures and training methods. The application of S2SBench to analyze Baichuan-Audio highlights the practical utility of the benchmark in guiding model development. However, the evaluation section could be expanded to include more baselines. Also, while there is an Appendix which features some visualization during training, there are no concrete steps or suggestions to solve intelligence degradation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly identifies and defines the problem of intelligence degradation in end-to-end speech LLMs.
    *   Well-defined benchmark: S2SBench provides a structured and reproducible framework for quantifying this degradation.
    *   Practical application: The benchmark is applied to analyze a real-world model (Baichuan-Audio), demonstrating its practical value.
    *   Resource availability: The release of datasets and code promotes reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited baselines: The evaluation could benefit from including more baseline models for comparison. The 'single stage' vs 'two stage' of the paper is really an experiment of how to train Baichuan-Audio, instead of how well one of these models perform against other models.
    *   Reliance on external APIs: The creation of sCMMLU depends on a proprietary OpenAI API (GPT-4), making it less accessible and potentially introducing bias. Also, the cost of accessing these proprietary APIs can add up.
    *   Lack of generative evaluation: The benchmark focuses on understanding (sentence continuation, commonsense reasoning) but doesn't directly evaluate the generative capabilities of speech LLMs in the speech-to-speech setting.
    *   Lack of concrete solutions: While the benchmark helps identify the problem, the paper does not offer any specific architectural or training solutions to mitigate intelligence degradation. It hints at it, but does not make concrete suggestions.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by providing a dedicated benchmark for quantifying intelligence degradation in end-to-end speech LLMs. While the evaluation could be more comprehensive and the paper doesn't propose solutions to this problem, S2SBench offers a good start and framework for this issue. The availability of the benchmark will likely stimulate further research in this area.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 6/10

### Creative Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14442v1)
- **Authors**: Mete Ismayilzada, Antonio Laverghetta Jr., Simone A. Luchini, Reet Patel, Antoine Bosselut, Lonneke van der Plas, Roger Beaty
- **Abstract**: While Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive performance across natural language generation tasks, their ability to generate truly creative content-characterized by novelty, diversity, surprise, and quality-remains limited. Existing methods for enhancing LLM creativity often focus narrowly on diversity or specific tasks, failing to address creativity's multifaceted nature in a generalizable way. In this work, we propose Creative Preference Optimization (CrPO), a novel alignment method that injects signals from multiple creativity dimensions into the preference optimization objective in a modular fashion. We train and evaluate creativity-augmented versions of several models using CrPO and MuCE, a new large-scale human preference dataset spanning over 200,000 human-generated responses and ratings from more than 30 psychological creativity assessments. Our models outperform strong baselines, including GPT-4o, on both automated and human evaluations, producing more novel, diverse, and surprising generations while maintaining high output quality. Additional evaluations on NoveltyBench further confirm the generalizability of our approach. Together, our results demonstrate that directly optimizing for creativity within preference frameworks is a promising direction for advancing the creative capabilities of LLMs without compromising output quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Creative Preference Optimization (CRPO), a novel method for improving the creativity of Large Language Models (LLMs). CRPO injects signals from multiple creativity dimensions (novelty, diversity, surprise, and quality) into the preference optimization objective of Direct Preference Optimization (DPO).  The authors created a large-scale human preference dataset called MUCE (Multitask Creativity Evaluation) spanning over 200,000 human-generated responses and creativity ratings.  They trained and evaluated CRPO-augmented versions of several models and demonstrated that their approach outperforms strong baselines (including GPT-4o) on both automated and human evaluations. CRPO produces more novel, diverse, and surprising generations while maintaining high output quality. Generalizability is confirmed through additional evaluations on NOVELTYBENCH.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:* The paper's novelty lies in its multifaceted approach to optimizing LLM creativity within a preference learning framework. While previous work has explored diversity or task-specific creativity enhancements, CRPO directly optimizes for novelty, surprise, diversity and quality simultaneously, using a modular preference optimization objective.  Also, the introduction of MUCE is a significant contribution, providing a new, large-scale human preference dataset specifically designed for creativity research, incorporating diverse tasks and human ratings aligned with psychological theories of creativity. It is, to my knowledge, one of the first such large-scale benchmarks built with direct input from creativity research communities.

*Significance:* The results are significant because they show that LLM creativity can be substantially improved by directly optimizing for it during the preference learning stage. The modular design allows researchers to explore tradeoffs between different creativity dimensions.  The experiments demonstrate practical improvements in generated content and generalizability across different tasks and benchmarks. This suggests a promising direction for future research in aligning LLMs with human values and improving their capacity for creative problem-solving and content creation. It also addresses, in part, some criticisms regarding LLM creativity focusing on more diverse metrics beyond simple diversity or output quality, and uses real human ratings tied to human creativity research principles.

*Strengths:*
*   Well-defined problem and clear motivation.
*   Novel method with a modular design.
*   Extensive experiments and comparisons with strong baselines (including commercial LLMs).
*   Introduction of a valuable new dataset (MUCE).
*   Demonstration of generalizability across different creativity tasks and benchmarks (NoveltyBench)
*   Consideration of ethical issues and limitations
*   Good ablation studies exploring the importance of injection weights.

*Weaknesses:*
*   Limited to English language in the experiments (although MUCE is multilingual). While understandable given resource constraints, it limits the generalizability of the findings.
*   Experiments are conducted on relatively smaller, open-weight models. Scaling to larger models and closed-source systems would provide a more convincing demonstration of CRPO's effectiveness.
*   It is unclear, from the paper, how the relative "weight" should be set to the four measured dimensions, and further study should be conducted to optimize these, especially given their findings that injection weights of >0.5 may cause performance degradation.
*   The analysis can be made even stronger by including qualitative examples to showcase the diverse, novel and surprising outputs.

*Potential influence:* The paper could significantly influence the field by:
*   Shifting the focus from generic alignment to creativity-specific optimization in LLMs.
*   Providing a valuable benchmark and methodology for future research on LLM creativity.
*   Inspiring the development of more creative and versatile LLM applications.

**Score: 8.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field with a novel method, extensive experiments, and a valuable new dataset.  The modular design of CRPO and the exploration of tradeoffs between creativity dimensions are particularly noteworthy.  While the limitations regarding language and model size are important considerations, the results provide a compelling demonstration of CRPO's potential and its impact on the field, and the creation of the MUCE dataset makes it significantly more useful to future researchers and developers of creativity-centric LLMs. As such, it earns a high score. The potential for influence is substantial.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Video Compression Commander: Plug-and-Play Inference Acceleration for Video Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14454v1)
- **Authors**: Xuyang Liu, Yiyu Wang, Junpeng Ma, Linfeng Zhang
- **Abstract**: Video large language models (VideoLLM) excel at video understanding, but face efficiency challenges due to the quadratic complexity of abundant visual tokens. Our systematic analysis of token compression methods for VideoLLMs reveals two critical issues: (i) overlooking distinctive visual signals across frames, leading to information loss; (ii) suffering from implementation constraints, causing incompatibility with modern architectures or efficient operators. To address these challenges, we distill three design principles for VideoLLM token compression and propose a plug-and-play inference acceleration framework "Video Compression Commander" (VidCom2). By quantifying each frame's uniqueness, VidCom2 adaptively adjusts compression intensity across frames, effectively preserving essential information while reducing redundancy in video sequences. Extensive experiments across various VideoLLMs and benchmarks demonstrate the superior performance and efficiency of our VidCom2. With only 25% visual tokens, VidCom2 achieves 99.6% of the original performance on LLaVA-OV while reducing 70.8% of the LLM generation latency. Notably, our Frame Compression Adjustment strategy is compatible with other token compression methods to further improve their performance. Our code is available at https://github.com/xuyang-liu16/VidCom2.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Video Compression Commander: Plug-and-Play Inference Acceleration for Video Large Language Models" addresses the computational inefficiency of Video Large Language Models (VideoLLMs) caused by the large number of visual tokens in videos. It identifies two key issues with existing token compression methods: overlooking distinctive visual signals and implementation constraints hindering compatibility with modern architectures.  The authors propose VidCom², a plug-and-play inference acceleration framework that dynamically adjusts compression intensity based on frame uniqueness. It operates in two stages: Frame Compression Adjustment (quantifying and prioritizing unique frames) and Adaptive Token Compression (selecting the most distinctive tokens within those prioritized frames). The paper presents experimental results demonstrating VidCom²'s superior performance and efficiency compared to existing methods across various VideoLLMs and benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The novelty lies in the framework's focus on *frame uniqueness* as the driving principle for token compression.  While other methods consider redundancy, VidCom² specifically prioritizes preserving distinctive information across frames in a way that mimics human visual attention. The two-stage approach of frame-level adjustment followed by token-level compression is also a novel combination.  However,  the individual components (using cosine similarity, attention-based methods, top-k selection) are not entirely novel on their own, but their combination in this specific context *is*.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the practical need to improve the efficiency of VideoLLMs, which is crucial for their broader adoption. By reducing computation costs, VidCom² makes it more feasible to deploy these models in real-world applications. The plug-and-play nature of the framework is also significant as it allows it to be integrated with existing VideoLLMs without retraining. The performance improvements reported in the paper are substantial, demonstrating a significant reduction in latency while maintaining accuracy. However, the reliance of specific architectural aspects (like attention weights), could pose a barrier to applicability in future model development. The paper also provides useful guidelines of model adaptability, frame uniqueness, and operator compatibility, as key design principles for videoLLM compression.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across diverse benchmarks and VideoLLMs.
    *   Significant performance and efficiency improvements compared to existing methods.
    *   Plug-and-play framework easily integrated with existing models.
    *   The paper clearly identifies and analyzes limitations of existing methods, providing clear design principles.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the combination of techniques is novel, the individual techniques are not new.
    *   The results could be stronger by experimenting on larger model architectures (the authors address this).
    *   The reliance on the frame-based approach does not fully explore the visual dynamics that could exist between frames, e.g., motion information.

*   **Potential Influence:** VidCom² has the potential to significantly influence the field of VideoLLMs by providing a more efficient and practical approach to inference. The emphasis on frame uniqueness could inspire new token compression methods. The reported design principles are likely to be widely cited and adopted. Further improvements in model size experimentation could improve the overall influence.

*   **Rigor:** The paper has rigorous experimentation based on the number of models, experimentation, and thorough baseline comparison. However, a more robust ablation study would aid in increasing the overall rigor of the work.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**  While the individual components of VidCom² are not groundbreaking, the overall framework represents a novel and significant contribution to the field of VideoLLMs. The focus on frame uniqueness, the two-stage compression approach, and the plug-and-play nature of the framework address critical challenges in deploying these models. The experimental results demonstrate substantial improvements in performance and efficiency. While there are some weaknesses (reliance on architectural features and lack of deeper exploration of dynamic visual information), the paper's strengths outweigh them, making it a solid and potentially influential contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### CtrlDiff: Boosting Large Diffusion Language Models with Dynamic Block Prediction and Controllable Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14455v1)
- **Authors**: Chihan Huang, Hao Tang
- **Abstract**: Although autoregressive models have dominated language modeling in recent years, there has been a growing interest in exploring alternative paradigms to the conventional next-token prediction framework. Diffusion-based language models have emerged as a compelling alternative due to their powerful parallel generation capabilities and inherent editability. However, these models are often constrained by fixed-length generation. A promising direction is to combine the strengths of both paradigms, segmenting sequences into blocks, modeling autoregressive dependencies across blocks while leveraging discrete diffusion to estimate the conditional distribution within each block given the preceding context. Nevertheless, their practical application is often hindered by two key limitations: rigid fixed-length outputs and a lack of flexible control mechanisms. In this work, we address the critical limitations of fixed granularity and weak controllability in current large diffusion language models. We propose CtrlDiff, a dynamic and controllable semi-autoregressive framework that adaptively determines the size of each generation block based on local semantics using reinforcement learning. Furthermore, we introduce a classifier-guided control mechanism tailored to discrete diffusion, which significantly reduces computational overhead while facilitating efficient post-hoc conditioning without retraining. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CtrlDiff sets a new standard among hybrid diffusion models, narrows the performance gap to state-of-the-art autoregressive approaches, and enables effective conditional text generation across diverse tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "CtrlDiff: Boosting Large Diffusion Language Models with Dynamic Block Prediction and Controllable Generation" addresses limitations in existing diffusion-based language models, specifically their fixed-length generation constraints and weak controllability. The authors propose a novel semi-autoregressive framework called CtrlDiff. It dynamically determines block sizes using reinforcement learning based on local semantics. It also introduces a classifier-guided control mechanism for discrete diffusion, reducing computational overhead while enabling efficient post-hoc conditioning. The authors demonstrate that CtrlDiff sets a new standard for hybrid diffusion models, bridging the performance gap with autoregressive approaches and facilitating effective conditional text generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of dynamic block size prediction with classifier-guided diffusion for controllable text generation is novel. Prior works have explored diffusion models for language, but CtrlDiff introduces a unique semi-autoregressive approach with reinforcement learning for adaptive block sizing and a computationally efficient classifier guidance. The novelty lies in the specific way these components are integrated to address the limitations of existing diffusion language models.

*   **Significance:** Addressing the fixed-length constraint and controllability issues of diffusion language models is significant. It makes these models more practical and competitive with autoregressive models, especially in scenarios where parallel generation and editability are desirable. The ability to dynamically adjust block sizes based on semantic content offers more fine-grained control and potential efficiency gains. The efficient classifier guidance mechanism tackles a key challenge in applying diffusion models to discrete data.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Technical Contribution:** The design of the dynamic block prediction mechanism using reinforcement learning is a strong technical contribution. The classifier-guided control mechanism appears to be an efficient and practical solution for conditioning discrete diffusion models.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The extensive experiments across various language modeling benchmarks and conditional generation tasks provide strong empirical support for the effectiveness of CtrlDiff.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the technical details of CtrlDiff, the experimental setup, and the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** While the paper demonstrates the effectiveness of CtrlDiff, the complexity of the framework (combining autoregressive, diffusion, and reinforcement learning components) may make it challenging to implement and optimize. Further ablation studies to measure the relative importance of each component would strengthen the analysis.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the classifier guidance is designed to be efficient, the overall computational cost of CtrlDiff (especially with reinforcement learning for block size prediction) may still be significant, potentially limiting its applicability to resource-constrained environments. Quantitative analysis of the computational cost compared to other models would improve the paper.
    *   **Hallucination:** While the paper is well researched on the improvements, a key issue in any generative model is the consistency of the generation. The paper mentions it is a weakness, and more in-depth analysis would be helpful.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the claims of CtrlDiff proving competitive with SOTA autoregressive models holds, it could encourage further research into hybrid approaches that combine the benefits of both autoregressive and diffusion models. The techniques for dynamic block size prediction and efficient classifier guidance may be adopted or adapted in other areas of text generation. However, the broader acceptance of CtrlDiff hinges on its practicality and scalability.

**Overall:**

CtrlDiff presents a valuable contribution to the field of diffusion language models by addressing their limitations in terms of fixed granularity and weak controllability. The paper is technically sound and well-supported by experimental results. While the complexity and potential computational cost are factors to consider, the novelty and significance of the proposed framework warrant a positive evaluation.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** CtrlDiff introduces a novel and significant framework for boosting diffusion language models. While the paper could benefit from further ablation studies, quantitative analysis of computational costs, and analysis of hallucination, the overall contribution is solid. It has the potential to influence future research on hybrid generative models.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### VisualQuality-R1: Reasoning-Induced Image Quality Assessment via Reinforcement Learning to Rank
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14460v1)
- **Authors**: Tianhe Wu, Jian Zou, Jie Liang, Lei Zhang, Kede Ma
- **Abstract**: DeepSeek-R1 has demonstrated remarkable effectiveness in incentivizing reasoning and generalization capabilities of large language models (LLMs) through reinforcement learning. Nevertheless, the potential of reasoning-induced computational modeling has not been thoroughly explored in the context of image quality assessment (IQA), a task critically dependent on visual reasoning. In this paper, we introduce VisualQuality-R1, a reasoning-induced no-reference IQA (NR-IQA) model, and we train it with reinforcement learning to rank, a learning algorithm tailored to the intrinsically relative nature of visual quality. Specifically, for a pair of images, we employ group relative policy optimization to generate multiple quality scores for each image. These estimates are then used to compute comparative probabilities of one image having higher quality than the other under the Thurstone model. Rewards for each quality estimate are defined using continuous fidelity measures rather than discretized binary labels. Extensive experiments show that the proposed VisualQuality-R1 consistently outperforms discriminative deep learning-based NR-IQA models as well as a recent reasoning-induced quality regression method. Moreover, VisualQuality-R1 is capable of generating contextually rich, human-aligned quality descriptions, and supports multi-dataset training without requiring perceptual scale realignment. These features make VisualQuality-R1 especially well-suited for reliably measuring progress in a wide range of image processing tasks like super-resolution and image generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "VisualQuality-R1: Reasoning-Induced Image Quality Assessment via Reinforcement Learning to Rank":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VisualQuality-R1, a novel no-reference image quality assessment (NR-IQA) model that leverages a vision-language model (VLM) fine-tuned with reinforcement learning to rank (RL2R).  The core idea is to move beyond the traditional regression-based approaches for IQA and to treat visual quality as an inherently relative concept, learned through comparisons. The model employs group relative policy optimization (GRPO) to generate multiple quality scores per image, captures predictive uncertainty via sample variance and defines the reward using a continuous fidelity measure derived from human opinion scores. The authors demonstrate that VisualQuality-R1 outperforms existing NR-IQA methods, generates human-aligned quality descriptions, and enables multi-dataset training without requiring perceptual scale realignment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper has a reasonable degree of novelty. The primary novelties lie in:

*   **RL2R for IQA:** Application of Reinforcement Learning to Rank, is not a totally new concept (as pointed out by the authors in "Q-Insight"), but VisualQuality-R1 presents a more sophisticated and effective implementation tailored for IQA.
*   **GRPO with Thurstone model Integration:** The integration of GRPO for generating quality scores and deriving uncertainty measures within the Thurstone model framework is a significant contribution. It allows the model to capture subtle distinctions in quality ranking and to make more reliable relative quality comparisons. This is a clever way to model the inherent subjectivity in IQA.
*   **Continuous Fidelity Measure:** Using a continuous fidelity measure derived from human opinion scores (MOS) as the reward function for RL2R. This offers a more granular signal for guiding the learning process than a discrete binary reward. This contrasts with prior work using binary rewards.

A weakness is that the individual components are not groundbreaking on their own. VLMs for IQA, RL for language models, and ranking-based approaches are all existing concepts. The novelty comes from the specific combination and adaptation of these techniques.

**Significance and Impact:**

The paper's significance lies in several aspects:

*   **Improved Performance:**  The experimental results clearly demonstrate that VisualQuality-R1 outperforms state-of-the-art NR-IQA methods on several benchmark datasets. This highlights the effectiveness of the proposed approach in capturing human perception of visual quality.
*   **Human-Aligned Descriptions:** The ability to generate human-aligned quality descriptions offers advantages in terms of interpretability and trust. These descriptions can be valuable for debugging image processing pipelines, providing targeted feedback, and facilitating human-computer interaction.
*   **Multi-Dataset Training:**  The ability to train the model on multiple datasets without perceptual scale realignment is a significant advantage, enabling the model to leverage a larger amount of training data and to generalize better to unseen distortion scenarios.
*   **Potential for Broader Applications:** The RL2R-based framework could potentially be adapted to other perceptual assessment tasks, such as image aesthetics assessment and perceptual similarity ranking.

However, there are also potential weaknesses in the paper's impact. While performance is demonstrated on KADID-10K and SPAQ, it's unclear how well the model generalizes to real-world, uncurated data. The reliance on a powerful VLM like Qwen2.5-VL-7B could limit its accessibility due to computational resource requirements. The gains may not be significant enough to outweigh the costs. Furthermore, the improvement on image generation domain is quite limited compared to others.

**Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **7/10**.

**Justification:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper presents a novel and well-designed NR-IQA framework that leverages RL2R to capture the relative nature of visual quality.  It integrates several techniques effectively (GRPO, Thurstone model, continuous fidelity measure). The experimental results are compelling and demonstrate that the proposed model outperforms existing methods and generates human-aligned descriptions. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the effectiveness of the different components of the framework. The potential for multi-dataset training is also a significant advantage.

*   **Weaknesses:**  The individual components of the framework are not entirely novel, and the model relies on a powerful VLM, which could limit its accessibility.  The generalizability to real-world data needs further investigation. Also, the performance gain in the image generation domain is limited.  While the performance improvements are consistent, their magnitude may not be transformative.

Overall, VisualQuality-R1 is a solid contribution to the field of image quality assessment. It provides a promising framework for future research and development in this area and demonstrates the potential of RL2R for perceptual assessment tasks. It also opens the door to integrating better descriptions into models, which is the most attractive feature.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 0/10

### Attributional Safety Failures in Large Language Models under Code-Mixed Perturbations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14469v1)
- **Authors**: Somnath Banerjee, Pratyush Chatterjee, Shanu Kumar, Sayan Layek, Parag Agrawal, Rima Hazra, Animesh Mukherjee
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in LLMs have raised significant safety concerns, particularly when dealing with code-mixed inputs and outputs. Our study systematically investigates the increased susceptibility of LLMs to produce unsafe outputs from code-mixed prompts compared to monolingual English prompts. Utilizing explainability methods, we dissect the internal attribution shifts causing model's harmful behaviors. In addition, we explore cultural dimensions by distinguishing between universally unsafe and culturally-specific unsafe queries. This paper presents novel experimental insights, clarifying the mechanisms driving this phenomenon.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the increased susceptibility of large language models (LLMs) to producing unsafe outputs when processing code-mixed prompts (blending multiple languages) compared to monolingual English prompts.  It uses explainability methods to dissect internal attribution shifts that contribute to harmful model behaviors. The study also differentiates between universally unsafe queries and culturally specific unsafe queries. The research presents novel experimental insights to clarify the mechanisms driving this phenomenon, emphasizing the interplay between culture, linguistic complexity, and model safety.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic exploration of LLM safety vulnerabilities *specifically* under code-mixed perturbations, combined with an explainability-driven approach that delves into the *why* behind these failures.  While prior work has addressed LLM safety and cross-lingual robustness, this research makes a significant contribution by focusing on code-mixing, analyzing attribution shifts, and incorporating cultural sensitivity. Differentiating universally unsafe and culturally specific content is another noteworthy element.

**Significance:**  The findings have significant implications for the safe and ethical deployment of LLMs in multilingual and multicultural contexts.  Code-mixing is a prevalent phenomenon, particularly in globalized settings, so understanding and mitigating these vulnerabilities is crucial. The paper offers actionable insights for developing more robust, culturally aware AI systems. The SDA framework to diagnose the attributional causes of behavioral failures in LLMs is a significant contribution. The study's emphasis on interpretability is also valuable, as it facilitates the development of targeted mitigation strategies.

**Strengths:**

*   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper presents a rigorous experimental setup with controlled comparisons between monolingual and code-mixed prompts across multiple languages and LLMs.
*   **Explainability-Driven Approach:**  Using attribution methods to analyze and visualize internal model behaviors provides valuable insights into the mechanisms underlying safety failures.
*   **Cultural Sensitivity:** The focus on cultural nuances and the distinction between universally and culturally specific unsafe content is a crucial and often overlooked aspect of LLM safety.
*   **Actionable Insights:** The study offers concrete recommendations for improving LLM safety training regimes by incorporating code-mixed data and sociolinguistic variation.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written, with clear explanations of the methodology, results, and implications.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Languages:** While the study covers a decent number of languages, expanding the set further would enhance the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Reliance on gpt4-o for Harm Assessment:** While gpt4-o is used for consistency, relying solely on one model to judge harmfulness may introduce bias or limitations. Manual human evaluation would be useful in conjunction with the model for some portion of the results.
*   **Complexity of Code-Mixing Generation:**  The code-mixing method, while based on linguistic theory, may not fully capture the nuances and variability of real-world code-mixed language use.
*   **Limited Exploration of Mitigation Strategies:** The paper primarily focuses on diagnosing the problem; further research is needed to develop and evaluate effective mitigation strategies in depth.

**Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the direction of LLM safety research by highlighting the importance of code-mixing, cultural sensitivity, and attribution-aware alignment strategies. It provides a solid foundation for future work on developing more robust and ethically sound LLMs for diverse global contexts.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a substantial contribution to the field by systematically investigating a critical, yet underexplored, aspect of LLM safety. The rigorous methodology, explainability-driven approach, and emphasis on cultural sensitivity make it a valuable contribution. The weaknesses, primarily related to the limited scope and reliance on a single evaluation model, somewhat temper its impact. However, the actionable insights and potential influence on future research justify a high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Towards Reliable Proof Generation with LLMs: A Neuro-Symbolic Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14479v1)
- **Authors**: Oren Sultan, Eitan Stern, Dafna Shahaf
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) struggle with formal domains that require rigorous logical deduction and symbolic reasoning, such as mathematical proof generation. We propose a neuro-symbolic approach that combines LLMs' generative strengths with structured components to overcome this challenge. As a proof-of-concept, we focus on geometry problems. Our approach is two-fold: (1) we retrieve analogous problems and use their proofs to guide the LLM, and (2) a formal verifier evaluates the generated proofs and provides feedback, helping the model fix incorrect proofs. We demonstrate that our method significantly improves proof accuracy for OpenAI's o1 model (58%-70% improvement); both analogous problems and the verifier's feedback contribute to these gains. More broadly, shifting to LLMs that generate provably correct conclusions could dramatically improve their reliability, accuracy and consistency, unlocking complex tasks and critical real-world applications that require trustworthiness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a neuro-symbolic approach to enhance the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate reliable mathematical proofs, specifically in Euclidean geometry. It addresses the challenge of LLMs struggling with formal domains requiring rigorous logical deduction. The approach combines the generative strengths of LLMs with two structured components: (1) analogical guidance, where similar problems and their proofs are retrieved to guide the LLM, and (2) symbolic verification, where a formal verifier evaluates the generated proofs and provides feedback for iterative refinement. The paper demonstrates significant improvements in proof accuracy using OpenAI's o1 model on the FormalGeo-7k dataset, showing that both analogical guidance and verification contribute to the gains. Additionally, it demonstrates a reduction in computational cost by using a focused theorem dictionary derived from the analogous problems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a sound and well-executed approach to a relevant problem in the field of LLMs and formal reasoning.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The neuro-symbolic approach effectively addresses the weaknesses of LLMs in formal domains.
    *   The use of analogical guidance is well-motivated by cognitive science and improves performance in a structured way. It’s not merely prompting, but incorporates specific analogical proofs retrieved based on identified similarity.
    *   The symbolic verifier provides a crucial feedback loop, enabling iterative refinement of proofs and ensuring their correctness. It’s more than just error identification, and incorporates SMT solving to verify the derived conclusions.
    *   The cost reduction through focused theorem dictionaries is a practical and valuable contribution.
    *   The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating significant improvements in proof accuracy and ablation studies quantifying the contribution of each component.
    *   Error tier analysis to determine LLM failure modes is valuable.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The focus on Euclidean geometry, while serving as a proof of concept, limits the generalizability of the approach. It's unclear how well the same techniques would apply to other formal domains with different types of rules and theorems (e.g., more complex algebraic domains, or logic systems).
    *   The abstraction schema used for analogy retrieval is relatively simple. More sophisticated methods for identifying structural similarities could potentially lead to even better performance.  Also the method for training a proof similarity predictor is relatively simple, and could be improved upon with more recent techniques.
    *   Reliance on OpenAI's o1 model (and the access limitations around it) may make it difficult to reproduce or extend the work using different LLMs. While the results show the *potential* of neuro-symbolic techniques, they are dependent on an LLM that's a "black box."
    *   Some of the limitations listed (e.g., no access to diagrams, dependence on a closed LLM, sensitivity to prompt phrasing) reflect broader challenges with LLMs in general, but they do limit the impact and robustness of the work.
    *   The gains are *relative* to a base model and task.  Although the *percentage* improvement is impressive (58-70%), the overall 80% accuracy on a task like FormalGeo-7k, while good, still leaves room for improvement for real-world application.
    *   Comparisons are not made to other solvers. While the point of the paper is not to outperform solvers, it helps to understand how much gap exists, and whether the neuro-symbolic approach is closer to closing this gap.

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The work is a solid example of a neuro-symbolic approach applied to a challenging task. While neuro-symbolic methods are not entirely new, the specific combination of analogical retrieval *with* verification, the structured error feedback, and the focus on formal mathematical proof generation add significant value.  The emphasis on verifiable proofs is particularly important, as it moves beyond the "plausibility" of LLM-generated text towards something that can be trusted. The cost reduction strategy is also very relevant. The paper’s novelty lies in its specific integration of existing components, and the experimental demonstration of the significant improvement.

**Overall:**

The paper is a well-executed proof of concept for a neuro-symbolic approach to mathematical proof generation. It has clear strengths in terms of methodology, experimental results, and practical cost reduction strategies. The limitations mainly stem from the inherent challenges of working with LLMs and the specific scope of the chosen problem domain. While not revolutionary, it's a significant step towards more reliable and trustworthy AI systems for formal reasoning tasks.
