### KERL: Knowledge-Enhanced Personalized Recipe Recommendation using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14629v1)
- **Authors**: Fnu Mohbat, Mohammed J Zaki
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) and the abundance of food data have resulted in studies to improve food understanding using LLMs. Despite several recommendation systems utilizing LLMs and Knowledge Graphs (KGs), there has been limited research on integrating food related KGs with LLMs. We introduce KERL, a unified system that leverages food KGs and LLMs to provide personalized food recommendations and generates recipes with associated micro-nutritional information. Given a natural language question, KERL extracts entities, retrieves subgraphs from the KG, which are then fed into the LLM as context to select the recipes that satisfy the constraints. Next, our system generates the cooking steps and nutritional information for each recipe. To evaluate our approach, we also develop a benchmark dataset by curating recipe related questions, combined with constraints and personal preferences. Through extensive experiments, we show that our proposed KG-augmented LLM significantly outperforms existing approaches, offering a complete and coherent solution for food recommendation, recipe generation, and nutritional analysis. Our code and benchmark datasets are publicly available at https://github.com/mohbattharani/KERL.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces KERL (Knowledge-Enhanced Personalized Recipe Recommendation using Large Language Models), a unified system designed to provide personalized food recommendations, generate recipes, and provide associated micro-nutritional information.  KERL leverages food knowledge graphs (specifically FoodKG) and large language models (LLMs) to achieve this. Given a natural language question with constraints and preferences, KERL extracts entities, retrieves subgraphs from the KG, feeds these into an LLM to select suitable recipes, and then generates cooking steps and nutritional information for each recipe. To evaluate KERL, the authors create a new benchmark dataset with recipe-related questions, constraints, and personal preferences. Experimental results demonstrate that KERL significantly outperforms existing approaches. The code and datasets are made publicly available. KERL leverages a LoRA approach for fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The integration of food KGs with LLMs for recipe recommendation isn't entirely novel, as the paper itself acknowledges prior work exploring KGQA and LLMs in food science. However, KERL's claimed novelty lies in:
        *   A unified system generating not only recommendations but also cooking steps *and* comprehensive nutritional information.
        *   A personalized approach combining health constraints and user preferences within a single framework.
        *   Multi-LoRA approach with a dedicated adapter for each task within the same base model.
        *   Benchmark generation for evaluating recipe recommendation system.

    *   The incremental addition of cooking steps and nutritional information generation on top of KG-enhanced LLM is valuable, but each component on their own is not individually groundbreaking.
    * LoRA approach reduces the computational power and cost of training significantly, but it is not a new concept.
*   **Significance:**

    *   Food recommendation has a direct impact on health.  Systems like KERL, which attempt to consider dietary restrictions and preferences, are practically important.
    *   The paper addresses a gap in existing research: the lack of a *unified* system that covers the entire pipeline from query to recipe and nutritional analysis.
    *   The creation and release of the benchmark dataset is a significant contribution, as it enables more rigorous evaluation and comparison of food recommendation systems. Prior works often didn't have publicly released datasets for this specific problem.
    * Providing code further enhances reproducibility and facilitates follow-up research and adoption of the system.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Comprehensive System: The integration of recommendation, recipe generation, and nutritional analysis is a strong point.
    *   Benchmark Dataset: The new dataset is a valuable contribution to the research community.
    *   Strong Experimental Results:  The experimental results demonstrate that KERL outperforms existing approaches by a large margin.
    *   Reproducibility:  The authors provide code and data, making the research more reproducible.
    *   Modular Approach: The separation into three modules with respective LoRA adapters makes the system more flexible and customizable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Incremental Novelty: The individual components (KG integration, LLM fine-tuning, recipe generation) are not entirely novel.
    * Reliance on FoodKG: The performance heavily relies on the completeness and accuracy of the FoodKG, as acknowledged in the limitations. This limits the generalizability.
    * Qualitative Failure Cases: While quantitative results are strong, the qualitative analysis reveals instances where KERL still makes errors. Further research into handling more complex queries and user preferences is needed.
*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The work is likely to influence future research in food recommendation systems, particularly those focused on personalized and health-aware approaches.
    *   The dataset is likely to become a standard benchmark for the field.
    *   The multi-LoRA architecture might inspire other researchers to apply this technique in similar domains.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper's core components aren't entirely novel on their own, the *combination* into a unified, personalized, and comprehensive food recommendation system with its associated benchmark is a significant step forward.  The improvements shown in the experiments are substantial and the publicly released dataset addresses a need within the field. The LoRA-based approach is also valuable in terms of efficiency. It falls short of being truly groundbreaking because its constituent elements are derivative of existing work. However, the paper's strong results and contributions to dataset availability justify a score indicating a substantial contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Think Only When You Need with Large Hybrid-Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14631v1)
- **Authors**: Lingjie Jiang, Xun Wu, Shaohan Huang, Qingxiu Dong, Zewen Chi, Li Dong, Xingxing Zhang, Tengchao Lv, Lei Cui, Furu Wei
- **Abstract**: Recent Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have shown substantially improved reasoning capabilities over traditional Large Language Models (LLMs) by incorporating extended thinking processes prior to producing final responses. However, excessively lengthy thinking introduces substantial overhead in terms of token consumption and latency, which is particularly unnecessary for simple queries. In this work, we introduce Large Hybrid-Reasoning Models (LHRMs), the first kind of model capable of adaptively determining whether to perform thinking based on the contextual information of user queries. To achieve this, we propose a two-stage training pipeline comprising Hybrid Fine-Tuning (HFT) as a cold start, followed by online reinforcement learning with the proposed Hybrid Group Policy Optimization (HGPO) to implicitly learn to select the appropriate thinking mode. Furthermore, we introduce a metric called Hybrid Accuracy to quantitatively assess the model's capability for hybrid thinking. Extensive experimental results show that LHRMs can adaptively perform hybrid thinking on queries of varying difficulty and type. It outperforms existing LRMs and LLMs in reasoning and general capabilities while significantly improving efficiency. Together, our work advocates for a reconsideration of the appropriate use of extended thinking processes and provides a solid starting point for building hybrid thinking systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Think Only When You Need with Large Hybrid-Reasoning Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Large Hybrid-Reasoning Models (LHRMs), a novel approach to improve the efficiency of large reasoning models (LRMs).  LRMs, while powerful, tend to overuse their reasoning capabilities, leading to unnecessary computational overhead for simple tasks. LHRMs address this "overthinking" problem by adaptively determining whether to engage in extended reasoning based on the context of the user's query. This is achieved through a two-stage training pipeline: Hybrid Fine-Tuning (HFT), which provides a cold start by training the model on both reasoning-intensive and direct-answer data, followed by Hybrid Group Policy Optimization (HGPO), a reinforcement learning method that teaches the model when to engage in extended thinking.  The paper also introduces a new metric, Hybrid Accuracy (HAcc), to evaluate the model's ability to select the appropriate reasoning mode.  Experimental results demonstrate that LHRMs outperform existing LRMs and LLMs in both reasoning and general capabilities, while significantly improving efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of adaptively engaging reasoning is moderately novel. While prior works acknowledge the "overthinking" issue in LRMs, this paper directly tackles it by developing a mechanism for dynamic mode selection within the model itself. The two-stage training approach (HFT + HGPO) also contributes to the novelty, providing a concrete method for implementing the adaptive reasoning behavior. Introducing the HAcc metric is another positive contribution. This allows for a more precise evaluation of how well the model adapts to different tasks.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in addressing a practical limitation of LRMs. By reducing computational overhead and improving response time for simple queries, LHRMs could make LRMs more suitable for real-world deployment. The improved efficiency also translates to cost savings, which is relevant to many AI development and deployment scenarios. The concept of hybrid accuracy contributes to evaluating the capability of hybrid thinking models. It serves as a quantifiable measurement to assess whether a model can effectively adapt to different tasks or contexts.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly identifies the "overthinking" problem in LRMs.
    *   Well-defined approach: The two-stage training pipeline (HFT + HGPO) is clearly explained and provides a tangible solution.
    *   Empirical validation: Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of LHRMs across various model scales and domains.
    *   New Evaluation metric: The introduction of Hybrid accuracy adds new information to evaluating the models that are used.
    *   The results show that LHRMs models demonstrate an improvement in the thinking ratio across domains.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Complexity of the training pipeline: Implementing HFT and HGPO might be complex and resource-intensive, potentially limiting its adoption by researchers with limited resources. The effectiveness of HGPO might be sensitive to hyperparameter tuning.
    *   Limited exploration of alternative approaches: The paper primarily focuses on the HFT + HGPO pipeline. Exploring alternative reinforcement learning methods or policy optimization techniques could further enhance the performance of LHRMs. The reliance on Llama-3.1-Tulu-3-8B-RM as a reward model might introduce biases or limitations. The paper acknowledges that alternative reward models may be used, which opens the possibility that the hybrid results could vary if a different reward model is applied.
    *   The paper could benefit from additional qualitative analysis. Although the performance metrics are useful, additional examples demonstrating how the model can appropriately leverage the thinking and no-thinking modes could enhance the findings of the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:**  LHRMs could influence the design of future reasoning models, encouraging researchers to prioritize efficiency and adaptive behavior. The concept of hybrid accuracy could become a standard metric for evaluating hybrid reasoning systems. The LHRM architecture may also inform future research on architectures for specific contexts.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and practical solution to a real-world challenge in LRM deployment. The two-stage training approach and the introduction of the HAcc metric are valuable contributions. The empirical results provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of LHRMs. However, the complexity of the training pipeline and limited exploration of alternative approaches hold back the overall significance. The significance will rely on further research building on these architectures.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### General-Reasoner: Advancing LLM Reasoning Across All Domains
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14652v1)
- **Authors**: Xueguang Ma, Qian Liu, Dongfu Jiang, Ge Zhang, Zejun Ma, Wenhu Chen
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has recently demonstrated strong potential in enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Particularly, the "Zero" reinforcement learning introduced by Deepseek-R1-Zero, enables direct RL training of base LLMs without relying on an intermediate supervised fine-tuning stage. Despite these advancements, current works for LLM reasoning mainly focus on mathematical and coding domains, largely due to data abundance and the ease of answer verification. This limits the applicability and generalization of such models to broader domains, where questions often have diverse answer representations, and data is more scarce. In this paper, we propose General-Reasoner, a novel training paradigm designed to enhance LLM reasoning capabilities across diverse domains. Our key contributions include: (1) constructing a large-scale, high-quality dataset of questions with verifiable answers curated by web crawling, covering a wide range of disciplines; and (2) developing a generative model-based answer verifier, which replaces traditional rule-based verification with the capability of chain-of-thought and context-awareness. We train a series of models and evaluate them on a wide range of datasets covering wide domains like physics, chemistry, finance, electronics etc. Our comprehensive evaluation across these 12 benchmarks (e.g. MMLU-Pro, GPQA, SuperGPQA, TheoremQA, BBEH and MATH AMC) demonstrates that General-Reasoner outperforms existing baseline methods, achieving robust and generalizable reasoning performance while maintaining superior effectiveness in mathematical reasoning tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "General-Reasoner: Advancing LLM Reasoning Across All Domains":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GENERAL-REASONER, a novel training paradigm designed to enhance the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) across diverse domains beyond the typical focus on mathematics and coding. The core contributions are: (1) a large-scale, high-quality dataset of questions with verifiable answers curated from web crawling across a wide range of disciplines (WebInstruct-verified dataset); and (2) a generative model-based answer verifier that replaces traditional rule-based verification, allowing for chain-of-thought and context-awareness. The models trained using this paradigm demonstrate superior and generalizable reasoning performance while maintaining strong results in mathematical reasoning tasks compared to baseline methods.  The work is conducted using the "Zero RL" approach where the base LLM is directly fine-tuned using reinforcement learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a Significant Limitation:** The paper directly tackles a critical limitation in the current LLM reasoning research: the over-reliance on mathematical and coding domains due to data abundance and easy answer verification. By extending reasoning to diverse fields like science, finance, and the humanities, the work significantly broadens the applicability and generalizability of LLM reasoning.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:** Creating a large-scale, high-quality, diverse dataset (WebInstruct-verified) is a substantial contribution in itself. The dataset-building pipeline, utilizing LLMs for question selection and annotation, addresses the challenge of obtaining reliably verifiable data outside of math and coding. This dataset has the potential to be a valuable resource for the community.
    *   **Generative Verifier:** The development of a generative model-based answer verifier is another key innovation. This addresses the limitations of rule-based verifiers when dealing with the varied and often ambiguous answer formats found in diverse domains. The model-based verifier, while compact, allows for chain-of-thought reasoning in the verification process.
    *   **Zero RL:** By leveraging Zero RL, the paper avoids the potentially limiting step of supervised fine-tuning. This simplifies the training process and allows for more direct optimization of the base LLM for reasoning.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a comprehensive evaluation across a wide range of challenging benchmarks, demonstrating consistent improvements over baseline methods. The evaluation includes both general reasoning (MMLU-Pro, GPQA, BBEH, TheoremQA) and mathematical reasoning (MATH-500, GSM8K, Olympiad) datasets.
    *   **Strong Results:** The results showcase significant improvements in general reasoning, often with gains of approximately 10% on MMLU-Pro and SuperGPQA. The models also demonstrate competitive performance in mathematical reasoning, even outperforming some math-focused RL frameworks.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The impact of dataset abundance and different verifiers were analyzed systematically through ablation studies.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The dataset creation pipeline and the generative verifier rely heavily on the capabilities of existing LLMs (Gemini). While this is a pragmatic approach, it introduces a potential bias towards the reasoning style and knowledge of the LLMs used in the process. The generated data can only be as good as the LLMs creating it.
    *   **Verifier Quality:** While the generative verifier is a significant improvement, its accuracy and robustness are crucial to the success of the training paradigm.  Although the paper claims high agreement with Gemini-2.0-Flash, a more detailed analysis of failure cases and limitations of the verifier would be beneficial.
    *   **Computational Resources:**  The training process still requires significant computational resources (multiple H100 GPUs).
    *   **Incomplete Data/Code Release:** As indicated, the paper is a Technical Report and Work in Progress. Releasing the WebInstruct-verified dataset and code base would significantly enhance its impact.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The most important novel aspect is the creation and exploitation of the diverse WebInstruct-verified dataset, and the corresponding generative answer verification methodology to make use of the dataset. Prior works have emphasized either the data aspect, or the RL training, but the combination is what is key.
    *   The method represents a clear advance over prior methods, which were limited to domains such as math.
    *   The experiments demonstrate that a general reasoning capability is attained.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The WebInstruct-verified dataset could become a standard benchmark for evaluating general reasoning capabilities of LLMs.
    *   The generative verifier approach could inspire further research into more robust and context-aware verification methods for diverse domains.
    *   The Zero RL training paradigm, combined with these contributions, provides a viable path for improving LLM reasoning beyond specialized domains.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **8** to this paper. The work makes significant contributions to the field of LLM reasoning by addressing a key limitation in domain coverage. The creation of a diverse dataset and a generative verifier are valuable innovations. The strong experimental results and comprehensive evaluation support the effectiveness of the proposed approach. While there are some weaknesses, such as the reliance on existing LLMs and the limited public release (to date), the paper's novelty and potential influence on the field justify this high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### SAFEPATH: Preventing Harmful Reasoning in Chain-of-Thought via Early Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14667v1)
- **Authors**: Wonje Jeung, Sangyeon Yoon, Minsuk Kahng, Albert No
- **Abstract**: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have become powerful tools for complex problem solving, but their structured reasoning pathways can lead to unsafe outputs when exposed to harmful prompts. Existing safety alignment methods reduce harmful outputs but can degrade reasoning depth, leading to significant trade-offs in complex, multi-step tasks, and remain vulnerable to sophisticated jailbreak attacks. To address this, we introduce SAFEPATH, a lightweight alignment method that fine-tunes LRMs to emit a short, 8-token Safety Primer at the start of their reasoning, in response to harmful prompts, while leaving the rest of the reasoning process unsupervised. Empirical results across multiple benchmarks indicate that SAFEPATH effectively reduces harmful outputs while maintaining reasoning performance. Specifically, SAFEPATH reduces harmful responses by up to 90.0% and blocks 83.3% of jailbreak attempts in the DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B model, while requiring 295.9x less compute than Direct Refusal and 314.1x less than SafeChain. We further introduce a zero-shot variant that requires no fine-tuning. In addition, we provide a comprehensive analysis of how existing methods in LLMs generalize, or fail, when applied to reasoning-centric models, revealing critical gaps and new directions for safer AI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SAFEPATH, a lightweight alignment method for Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) designed to prevent harmful reasoning. SAFEPATH fine-tunes LRMs to emit a short, 8-token "Safety Primer" at the beginning of their reasoning process in response to harmful prompts, leaving the remaining reasoning process unsupervised. The paper claims that SAFEPATH effectively reduces harmful outputs while maintaining reasoning performance, requiring significantly less computation compared to existing methods like Direct Refusal and SafeChain. The paper also presents a zero-shot variant of SAFEPATH. The authors analyze existing LLM safety methods within the context of LRMs and discuss directions for safer AI.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using a lightweight "Safety Primer" prefix to guide the reasoning process is somewhat novel. While prefix-tuning and prompting strategies for safety are not new, the specific application within LRMs, the focus on a minimal 8-token signal, and the contrast with full supervision of the reasoning process distinguishes it from existing methods. The zero-shot variant also adds a degree of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge: aligning LRMs for safety without sacrificing their reasoning capabilities. The fact that LRMs, with their structured reasoning, can inadvertently amplify unsafe behaviors makes this a critical problem to solve. If the claims of reduced computation, effective harmful output mitigation, and sustained reasoning performance hold, the method could be valuable. However, the significance largely depends on the robustness of the method to more sophisticated adversarial attacks and its generalizability across different LRMs and tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Lightweight Approach:** The focus on minimal intervention is a major strength, potentially enabling scalability and ease of implementation.
    *   **Reasoning Preservation:** The unsupervised reasoning process allows LRM to continue thinking and solves tasks instead of abruptly stopping and refusing to answer. The claims of preserving reasoning capabilities are a key advantage over methods that strictly refuse or terminate reasoning.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The reported empirical results demonstrate a reduction in harmful outputs and attack success rates. The computational cost comparisons are also compelling.
    *   **Emergent Behavior:** The observation of the Safety Primer being re-activated during intermediate reasoning steps suggests an interesting dynamic.
    *   **Zero-shot Adaptation:** The development of the zero-shot variant improves the potential and accessibility of this work.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Generalization Analysis:** While the paper tests on different models, the analysis of generalizability across diverse LRMs and various harmful scenarios should be a larger focus for practical adoption.
    *   **Adversarial Robustness:** While SAFEPATH is effective against some adversarial attacks, it still shows vulnerability in PAIR. It's unclear how well it would stand up against more sophisticated, adaptive attacks specifically designed to circumvent the Safety Primer.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:** The qualitative examples could be more diverse and include failure cases to provide a more balanced assessment of the method's limitations.
    *   **Over Claiming:** The statement that safety can be achieved without compromising reasoning appears to be overstated. While SAFEPATH seems to reduce this tradeoff, no approach can remove it completely.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the demonstrated benefits hold in real-world scenarios, SAFEPATH has the potential to influence LRM development. It offers a practical and potentially scalable approach to safety alignment. The identified issues in LRM alignment opens up a new direction for research.

*   **Room for Improvement**:
    *   **Comprehensive benchmarking**: The method should be evaluated against diverse datasets and attack vectors.
    *   **Theoretical analysis**: Providing theoretical backing for the efficacy of the proposed approach would be a worthwhile goal.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses of the paper, a score of 7 is justified. The "Safety Primer" approach is novel, and the paper addresses a crucial challenge in LRM safety. The experiments demonstrate promising results, particularly the computational efficiency. However, the limitations related to adversarial robustness and generalizability somewhat temper the score. While the paper presents a valuable contribution, further research is needed to address the identified weaknesses and solidify its position in the field.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### ContextAgent: Context-Aware Proactive LLM Agents with Open-World Sensory Perceptions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14668v1)
- **Authors**: Bufang Yang, Lilin Xu, Liekang Zeng, Kaiwei Liu, Siyang Jiang, Wenrui Lu, Hongkai Chen, Xiaofan Jiang, Guoliang Xing, Zhenyu Yan
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have propelled intelligent agents from reactive responses to proactive support. While promising, existing proactive agents either rely exclusively on observations from enclosed environments (e.g., desktop UIs) with direct LLM inference or employ rule-based proactive notifications, leading to suboptimal user intent understanding and limited functionality for proactive service. In this paper, we introduce ContextAgent, the first context-aware proactive agent that incorporates extensive sensory contexts to enhance the proactive capabilities of LLM agents. ContextAgent first extracts multi-dimensional contexts from massive sensory perceptions on wearables (e.g., video and audio) to understand user intentions. ContextAgent then leverages the sensory contexts and the persona contexts from historical data to predict the necessity for proactive services. When proactive assistance is needed, ContextAgent further automatically calls the necessary tools to assist users unobtrusively. To evaluate this new task, we curate ContextAgentBench, the first benchmark for evaluating context-aware proactive LLM agents, covering 1,000 samples across nine daily scenarios and twenty tools. Experiments on ContextAgentBench show that ContextAgent outperforms baselines by achieving up to 8.5% and 6.0% higher accuracy in proactive predictions and tool calling, respectively. We hope our research can inspire the development of more advanced, human-centric, proactive AI assistants.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ContextAgent, a novel context-aware proactive LLM agent designed to incorporate extensive sensory context from wearable devices (e.g., smart glasses, earphones) to enhance its proactive capabilities.  It addresses the limitations of existing proactive agents that either rely on enclosed environments or rule-based notifications. ContextAgent extracts multi-dimensional contexts (sensory and persona) to understand user intentions, predict the necessity of proactive services, and automatically call necessary tools for assistance. To evaluate ContextAgent, the authors created ContextAgentBench, a new benchmark dataset consisting of 1,000 samples across nine daily scenarios and twenty tools.  The experiments demonstrate that ContextAgent outperforms baselines in both proactive prediction and tool calling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely novel approach. The idea of leveraging sensory context from wearable devices to create a more proactive and useful LLM agent is a significant departure from previous work that focused on desktop environments or rule-based systems. The creation of the ContextAgentBench is also a substantial contribution, providing a much-needed benchmark for this type of agent. The combination of sensory input, persona context, and tool-augmented reasoning is a well-motivated and potentially powerful approach.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of AI assistants. By moving beyond reactive responses and incorporating real-world sensory information, ContextAgent represents a step toward more natural, helpful, and ubiquitous AI assistants. The ContextAgentBench benchmark will likely foster further research in this area. However, The performance of a tool calling agent is dependent on the tool definitions itself. This might over-estimate the significance in the general tool-calling field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing proactive agents and articulates the need for context-aware systems.
    *   **Well-Defined Approach:** The ContextAgent architecture is well-defined, with clear explanations of its context extraction, reasoning, and tool-calling components.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The creation of ContextAgentBench and the comprehensive experiments provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of ContextAgent.
    *   **Practical Applications:** The potential applications of ContextAgent are significant, ranging from personal assistance to healthcare and more.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Sensor Data:** The ContextAgentBench, while valuable, relies on textual descriptions of sensory input. It will be interesting to validate the agent in a real-world deployment with raw video and audio feeds.
    *   **Tool Dependency:** The experiments depend heavily on the curated tool set. The tool-calling abilities may not generalize well to entirely new tool sets.
    *   **Real-World Validity:** While the paper provides a strong start, real-world deployment of this is questionable. The dataset consists of pre-defined scenarios.

*   **Potential Influence:** ContextAgent and ContextAgentBench will likely influence future research in several ways:
    *   **Inspiration for New Architectures:**  The ContextAgent architecture may serve as a template for developing more sophisticated context-aware agents.
    *   **Benchmark for Comparison:** ContextAgentBench will become a standard benchmark for evaluating proactive LLM agents.
    *   **Focus on Sensory Perception:** The work will encourage researchers to explore the use of sensory information to enhance the capabilities of AI assistants.
    *   **Wearable AI:** Promotes further research into AI systems that run on wearable devices, aligning with the direction of ubiquitous computing.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a substantial contribution to the field by introducing a genuinely novel approach to proactive LLM agents. While the dataset is strong, the reliance on synthetic sensory context to test the models' ability is questionable. Furthermore, tool generalization capabilities might over-estimate the performance gains when tested on similar task contexts. The combination of sensory input and tool-augmented LLM reasoning is an intriguing direction for future research, however further validation will be required.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Quartet: Native FP4 Training Can Be Optimal for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14669v1)
- **Authors**: Roberto L. Castro, Andrei Panferov, Soroush Tabesh, Oliver Sieberling, Jiale Chen, Mahdi Nikdan, Saleh Ashkboos, Dan Alistarh
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) has been paralleled by unprecedented increases in computational demands, with training costs for state-of-the-art models doubling every few months. Training models directly in low-precision arithmetic offers a solution, by improving both computational throughput and energy efficiency. Specifically, NVIDIA's recent Blackwell architecture facilitates extremely low-precision operations, specifically FP4 variants, promising substantial efficiency gains. Yet, current algorithms for training LLMs in FP4 precision face significant accuracy degradation and often rely on mixed-precision fallbacks. In this paper, we systematically investigate hardware-supported FP4 training and introduce Quartet, a new approach enabling accurate, end-to-end FP4 training with all the major computations (in e.g. linear layers) being performed in low precision. Through extensive evaluations on Llama-type models, we reveal a new low-precision scaling law that quantifies performance trade-offs across varying bit-widths and allows us to identify a "near-optimal" low-precision training technique in terms of accuracy-vs-computation, called Quartet. We implement Quartet using optimized CUDA kernels tailored for NVIDIA Blackwell GPUs, and show that it can achieve state-of-the-art accuracy for FP4 precision, successfully training billion-scale models. Our method demonstrates that fully FP4-based training is a competitive alternative to standard-precision and FP8 training. Our code is available at https://github.com/IST-DASLab/Quartet.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Quartet: Native FP4 Training Can Be Optimal for Large Language Models" introduces a novel approach to training large language models (LLMs) entirely in the FP4 precision format, leveraging the capabilities of NVIDIA's Blackwell architecture.  It identifies limitations in existing FP4 training techniques and proposes "Quartet," an algorithm with optimized CUDA kernels that enable end-to-end FP4 training. A key contribution is the introduction of a new low-precision scaling law framework that quantifies the trade-offs between accuracy and computation in varying bit-widths. Using this framework, Quartet seeks to maximize both "parameter efficiency" and "data efficiency" in the low-precision regime. The paper demonstrates that Quartet achieves state-of-the-art accuracy for FP4 training on Llama-type models, even surpassing FP8 training in certain scenarios, showing the potential for fully FP4-based training to be a competitive and efficient alternative.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to FP4 training of LLMs. The key contributions are:
    *   **Quartet Algorithm:** The development of a complete end-to-end FP4 training algorithm (as opposed to mixed-precision methods) tailored for NVIDIA's Blackwell architecture and its MXFP4 format. This is a practical and useful contribution.
    *   **Scaling Law Framework:** The proposal of a scaling law that specifically incorporates the impact of low-precision training, particularly focusing on forward- and backward-pass precision and defining parameter and data efficiency. This is a valuable addition to the existing scaling law literature.
    *   **Highly-Optimized Implementation:** A fast CUDA kernel implementation of the algorithm, demonstrating the feasibility of end-to-end FP4 training on modern hardware.
    *   **Bias and MSE Analysis:** Analysis of the bias induced in backpropagation by low-precision quantization, leading to the chosen stochastic rounding scheme.

* **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to significantly reduce the computational cost and energy consumption of LLM training. If FP4 training can consistently match the accuracy of FP8 or even FP16 training, the impact on AI research and development would be substantial. The scaling law framework also provides a valuable tool for understanding the effects of low-precision training, which can inform future algorithmic development and hardware design. The CUDA kernel implementation is essential for realizing the practical benefits of FP4 training. The identification of optimal training regions (FP4 vs. FP8) based on model size and data budget is also practically significant.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes thorough experiments comparing Quartet with several existing FP4/INT4 training methods and an FP8 baseline. The scaling law fitting provides a principled way to compare performance across methods.
    *   **Hardware-Aware Design:** The method is specifically designed to leverage the advantages of the Blackwell architecture, resulting in significant speedups.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the algorithm, the scaling law framework, and the experimental results.
    *   **Practical Implementation:** The paper contains detail on the implementation of Quartet using CUDA kernels, including insights on exploiting new hardware capabilities to maximize arithmetic intensity.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The method is very tightly coupled to the NVIDIA Blackwell architecture and its MXFP4 format. It may not be directly applicable to other hardware platforms or low-precision formats.
    *   **Still Requires Tuning:** While the paper presents a framework for determining the "optimal" precision, the actual selection of parameters and scaling laws still involves some degree of empirical tuning.
    *   **Lack of Deeper Theoretical Justification:** While the paper introduces the "misalignment metric", the paper would benefit from a deeper theoretical justification for why Quartet works so well.
    *   **Experimentation could be broader:** The evaluations are primarily focused on C4. Broader evaluations would solidify the robustness of the method.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence research on low-precision training for LLMs, particularly in the context of hardware acceleration. The scaling law framework could be adopted by other researchers to evaluate different low-precision techniques. The CUDA kernel implementation can serve as a starting point for further optimization of FP4 training on Blackwell GPUs. Given NVIDIA's dominance in the GPU market, this is likely to have a practical impact.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a substantial contribution to the field of low-precision LLM training, particularly in leveraging hardware-supported FP4 formats. The algorithm, its scaling law framework and its CUDA kernel implementation and is presented in a clear and rigorous manner and the thorough evaluation supports the claims of the authors. However, the method's tight coupling to NVIDIA's Blackwell architecture and the MXFP4 format limits its immediate generalizability and applicability, and this fact impacts the assigned score. While the paper explores "parameter efficiency" and "data efficiency", a stronger link to theoretical frameworks for generalization and optimization would elevate the paper's significance. Despite these drawbacks, the paper offers a valuable practical solution and a useful framework for understanding low-precision training and it deserves recognition within the LLM training community. The future impact will be determined by the ability of researchers to generalize the findings to other hardware platforms and low-precision formats and develop a stronger theoretical grounding of the alignment metric and the chosen training scheme.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Training-Free Watermarking for Autoregressive Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14673v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Tong, Zihao Pan, Shuai Yang, Kaiyang Zhou
- **Abstract**: Invisible image watermarking can protect image ownership and prevent malicious misuse of visual generative models. However, existing generative watermarking methods are mainly designed for diffusion models while watermarking for autoregressive image generation models remains largely underexplored. We propose IndexMark, a training-free watermarking framework for autoregressive image generation models. IndexMark is inspired by the redundancy property of the codebook: replacing autoregressively generated indices with similar indices produces negligible visual differences. The core component in IndexMark is a simple yet effective match-then-replace method, which carefully selects watermark tokens from the codebook based on token similarity, and promotes the use of watermark tokens through token replacement, thereby embedding the watermark without affecting the image quality. Watermark verification is achieved by calculating the proportion of watermark tokens in generated images, with precision further improved by an Index Encoder. Furthermore, we introduce an auxiliary validation scheme to enhance robustness against cropping attacks. Experiments demonstrate that IndexMark achieves state-of-the-art performance in terms of image quality and verification accuracy, and exhibits robustness against various perturbations, including cropping, noises, Gaussian blur, random erasing, color jittering, and JPEG compression.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces IndexMark, a novel, training-free watermarking framework designed specifically for autoregressive image generation models.  Unlike existing methods primarily focused on diffusion models, IndexMark leverages the redundancy found in the codebooks of autoregressive models. The core idea involves a "match-then-replace" strategy: pairing similar indices in the codebook, designating them as "red" or "green", and then selectively replacing generated red indices with their corresponding green indices based on index confidence during image generation. This imperceptibly embeds the watermark. The verification process involves calculating the proportion of green indices and utilizing an Index Encoder to improve accuracy. The paper also proposes a cropping-robust validation scheme to address vulnerabilities introduced by VQ-VAE's block-level processing.  Experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of IndexMark in maintaining image quality, achieving high verification accuracy, and demonstrating robustness against various image perturbations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing the under-explored area of watermarking for autoregressive image generation models. The "match-then-replace" strategy based on codebook redundancy is a relatively novel approach. The introduction of an Index Encoder to improve verification accuracy is also a worthwhile addition. The cropping robustness strategy, while not groundbreaking, is essential for practical applications of the watermark.  However, there isn't a deep mathematical or theoretical justification provided for the choice of certain parameters, or analysis of its impact on downstream tasks such as editing. Also, concurrent work has already looked at Safe-VAR to provide watermarking strategies for Autoregressive models. The innovation is not necessarily *groundbreaking*.

* **Significance:**  With the increasing prevalence of autoregressive image generation models, the need for effective watermarking techniques is crucial. IndexMark provides a practical and efficient solution that can contribute to addressing issues such as copyright protection, content moderation, and accountability in AI-generated content. The training-free nature of the method makes it particularly attractive for easy integration with existing models. The approach of considering the problem of model misuse for generative models is relevant and timely. However, the practical applicability of the approach might be limited due to the vulnerability on images with high text embeddings.

* **Strengths:**
    * Training-free:  A significant advantage, reducing development costs and enabling easier integration.
    * Image Quality:  Maintains good image quality compared to other watermarking methods.
    * Robustness: Demonstrates reasonable robustness against common image manipulations and particularly cropping.
    * Practicality: Addresses a relevant gap in existing watermarking techniques.
    * Clear Presentation: The paper is well-written and easy to understand.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * Limited Theoretical Analysis: Lack of deeper mathematical analysis or theoretical justification for parameter choices. There's a reliance on empirical results.
    * Incremental Contribution:  While novel in the specific context of autoregressive models, the underlying watermarking concepts are built upon existing techniques.  Concurrent work like Safe-VAR needs to be carefully distinguished against.
    * Vulnerability to High Text Embeddings: It's possible that the watermarking could significantly impact the downstream usefulness of images with text embeddings.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of watermarking techniques for autoregressive image generation models. It offers a solid foundation for future research and can be adopted by practitioners in the field.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of applying codebook redundancy to training-free autoregressive model watermarking, its practical importance, and its reasonable performance, a score of **7** is appropriate. The work is a valuable contribution, but it lacks groundbreaking theoretical insights and has some limitations, especially concerning its impact on downstream use cases of the images produced. The "match-then-replace" strategy is neat but relatively simple, and the Index Encoder is a practical addition rather than a fundamentally new concept. Further improvements could focus on making it more robust and practical by combining with more advanced watermarking methods and having a deeper analysis of the parameter choices.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reward Reasoning Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14674v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaxin Guo, Zewen Chi, Li Dong, Qingxiu Dong, Xun Wu, Shaohan Huang, Furu Wei
- **Abstract**: Reward models play a critical role in guiding large language models toward outputs that align with human expectations. However, an open challenge remains in effectively utilizing test-time compute to enhance reward model performance. In this work, we introduce Reward Reasoning Models (RRMs), which are specifically designed to execute a deliberate reasoning process before generating final rewards. Through chain-of-thought reasoning, RRMs leverage additional test-time compute for complex queries where appropriate rewards are not immediately apparent. To develop RRMs, we implement a reinforcement learning framework that fosters self-evolved reward reasoning capabilities without requiring explicit reasoning traces as training data. Experimental results demonstrate that RRMs achieve superior performance on reward modeling benchmarks across diverse domains. Notably, we show that RRMs can adaptively exploit test-time compute to further improve reward accuracy. The pretrained reward reasoning models are available at https://huggingface.co/Reward-Reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Reward Reasoning Models (RRMs), a novel approach to reward modeling in large language models (LLMs).  RRMs are designed to perform explicit chain-of-thought reasoning before generating final rewards. This reasoning phase allows the models to adaptively allocate computational resources based on the complexity of the input.  A reinforcement learning framework is used to train RRMs, encouraging them to self-evolve reward reasoning capabilities without requiring explicit reasoning traces in the training data. The paper presents experimental results demonstrating that RRMs outperform existing reward models on a variety of benchmarks, including reasoning, general knowledge, safety, and alignment with human preference. The paper also shows RRMs can be used in practical applications such as reward-guided best-of-N inference and LLM post-training and that they adaptively scale test-time compute for enhanced performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of incorporating explicit reasoning into reward models is a significant contribution.  Most existing reward models treat reward prediction as a direct regression task, lacking the interpretability and adaptability offered by RRMs. Framing reward modeling as a reasoning task and leveraging chain-of-thought offers a distinct approach.  The use of reinforcement learning to train the models *without* relying on pre-existing reasoning traces is also a clever technique to bypass the limitations of supervised learning. However, RRM does rely on an external reward signal from a rule-based environment, which does pose some limitations. Prior work has examined chain-of-thought for reward modeling, as the authors acknowledge, but the focus on adaptive computation and RL-based self-evolution appears novel. The work stands on the shoulders of prior related work, but provides some innovations that are unique and valuable.

**Significance:** Accurate and reliable reward models are crucial for aligning LLMs with human values.  If RRMs can truly improve the quality of reward signals, it could have a broad impact on various post-training tasks, including RLHF and instruction tuning. The demonstrated improvements on diverse benchmarks are promising. The adaptation of RRMs to scalable inference (best-of-N sampling, tournament schemes) further strengthens the practical value of the approach. In particular, the ablation experiment showing that RRMs are better than a similarly trained DirectJudge model which outputs only a final decision is crucial for demonstrating that reasoning actually plays a role. On the downside, this study is limited by the quality of the benchmarks, which are known to have some limitations that may cause concerns in generalizing these findings to diverse applications.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the need for more adaptable and interpretable reward models.
*   **Sound Methodology:** The proposed framework is well-designed, combining chain-of-thought reasoning, reinforcement learning, and adaptive computation.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments cover a range of benchmarks and practical applications. The analyses of scaling behavior and reasoning patterns provide valuable insights.
*   **Detailed Experiments:** The inclusion of detailed results on subsets of benchmarks, ablation studies, and scaling behavior provide strong support for the effectiveness of RRMs.
*   **Open Source Models:** The authors have open-sourced their models, enhancing reproducibility and facilitating further research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependence on Rule-Based Reward:** The rule-based reward environment, while ingenious, could introduce biases or limitations to the reasoning patterns developed by RRMs. The rule-based reward can also be noisy and incomplete.
*   **Benchmark Limitations:** The reliance on existing benchmarks, while standard practice, means that the results are only as good as the quality of the benchmarks themselves.  Some of the benchmarks used have known limitations, which impact the generalizability of the results.
*   **Computational Cost:** The inference-time scaling techniques add computational overhead compared to simpler reward models. The benefits must outweigh the costs in real-world applications.
*   **Incremental Improvement:** RRMs outperform other methods, but this may not necessarily translate into a significant impact on actual applications. The study needs to show more concretely that using RRM improves post-training and downstream task performance when using RRM.
*   **Potential for Hallucination:** Although the rule-based approach is supposed to limit hallucination, it is not clear the model has been fine-tuned to this effect.

**Potential Influence:**

RRMs could influence future research on reward modeling and alignment of LLMs. The framework could be extended to incorporate more sophisticated reasoning techniques, different RL algorithms, or alternative training objectives. The concept of adaptive computation in reward models could inspire other approaches to improve efficiency and effectiveness.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid contribution with several strengths, particularly its novel approach to reward modeling using chain-of-thought reasoning and adaptive computation. However, the limitations associated with the rule-based reward environment and the dependency on possibly flawed benchmarks prevent me from ranking this paper above an 7. It has an important impact, but the generalizability needs to be proven.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Visionary-R1: Mitigating Shortcuts in Visual Reasoning with Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14677v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaer Xia, Yuhang Zang, Peng Gao, Yixuan Li, Kaiyang Zhou
- **Abstract**: Learning general-purpose reasoning capabilities has long been a challenging problem in AI. Recent research in large language models (LLMs), such as DeepSeek-R1, has shown that reinforcement learning techniques like GRPO can enable pre-trained LLMs to develop reasoning capabilities using simple question-answer pairs. In this paper, we aim to train visual language models (VLMs) to perform reasoning on image data through reinforcement learning and visual question-answer pairs, without any explicit chain-of-thought (CoT) supervision. Our findings indicate that simply applying reinforcement learning to a VLM -- by prompting the model to produce a reasoning chain before providing an answer -- can lead the model to develop shortcuts from easy questions, thereby reducing its ability to generalize across unseen data distributions. We argue that the key to mitigating shortcut learning is to encourage the model to interpret images prior to reasoning. Therefore, we train the model to adhere to a caption-reason-answer output format: initially generating a detailed caption for an image, followed by constructing an extensive reasoning chain. When trained on 273K CoT-free visual question-answer pairs and using only reinforcement learning, our model, named Visionary-R1, outperforms strong multimodal models, such as GPT-4o, Claude3.5-Sonnet, and Gemini-1.5-Pro, on multiple visual reasoning benchmarks.
- **Summary**: **Summary**

The paper "Visionary-R1: Mitigating Shortcuts in Visual Reasoning with Reinforcement Learning" addresses the problem of shortcut learning in visual language models (VLMs) trained with reinforcement learning (RL) using question-answer pairs. The authors observe that directly applying the Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) technique to VLMs leads to the model exploiting superficial cues and textual patterns to answer questions correctly without genuine visual understanding. To address this, they propose Visionary-R1, a framework that trains the VLM to first generate a detailed caption of the image *before* reasoning and answering. This forces the model to engage in deeper image context analysis.  The captioning step is incentivized with reinforcement learning from AI feedback. The resulting model outperforms strong multimodal models like GPT-4o and Claude3.5-Sonnet on several visual reasoning benchmarks, despite using only question-answer pairs for training and having a relatively small (3B) parameter size.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of forcing VLMs to generate a caption before reasoning to mitigate shortcut learning is a reasonably novel and practical approach. While the individual components (GRPO, AI feedback for reward shaping) are not new, their combination in this particular architecture and the problem it addresses (shortcut learning in VLMs under RL) is a useful contribution. The exploration and mitigation of shortcut learning in RL-based VLM training using captioning-guided reasoning is well-demonstrated and can lead to further advancements.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in training VLMs for visual reasoning: shortcut learning. This issue significantly hinders the models' ability to generalize and perform well on unseen data. By proposing and demonstrating an effective solution using a relatively simple and cost-effective training pipeline (i.e. RL with only question-answer data), the paper makes a significant contribution to lowering the barrier to entry for training high-performing VLMs. The fact that the 3B parameter model achieves superior results to significantly larger proprietary models on specific reasoning tasks is promising. The detailed analysis of the KL coefficient's impact on training is also valuable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and explains the shortcut learning problem.
    *   **Effective Solution:** The proposed Visionary-R1 framework effectively mitigates the identified problem.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The paper presents compelling experimental results demonstrating Visionary-R1's superior performance compared to baselines and even large proprietary models. The benchmark selection covers a variety of visual formats and questioning.
    *   **Detailed Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component of the framework.
    *   **Analysis of KL coefficient:** The paper provides detailed and helpful analyses on the optimal value of the KL coefficient.
    *   **Public Release:** Promise to release code and models facilitates further research and development.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Model Size Limitation:** The experiments are limited to a 3B parameter model due to computational constraints. It's difficult to say how well the approach scales to larger models.
    *   **Benchmark Selection:** While the benchmark selection is good, more diverse benchmarks could further improve the generalizability of the conclusion.
    *   **Comparative Analysis with other SOTA open models:** The paper compares with closed-source models like GPT-4o, but only very high-level comparison with other open-source SOTA is performed. Adding an empirical comparion is needed.
    *   **Limited Novelty of Individual Components:** The individual components, while well-integrated, lack substantial novelty on their own.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a solid contribution by addressing a significant issue (shortcut learning) in VLM training with RL. The proposed Visionary-R1 framework is effective and the empirical results are compelling. However, the novelty of the individual components is somewhat limited, and the model's scalability to larger sizes needs further investigation. Despite these limitations, the work is well-executed and presents a practical solution with the potential to influence future research in the field. The rigorous experimental evaluation adds considerable value.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### UltraEdit: Training-, Subject-, and Memory-Free Lifelong Editing in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14679v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaojie Gu, Guangxu Chen, Jungang Li, Jia-Chen Gu, Xuming Hu, Kai Zhang
- **Abstract**: Lifelong learning enables large language models (LLMs) to adapt to evolving information by continually updating their internal knowledge. An ideal system should support efficient, wide-ranging updates while preserving existing capabilities and ensuring reliable deployment. Model editing stands out as a promising solution for this goal, offering a focused and efficient way to revise a model's internal knowledge. Although recent paradigms have made notable progress, they often struggle to meet the demands of practical lifelong adaptation at scale. To bridge this gap, we propose ULTRAEDIT-a fundamentally new editing solution that is training-, subject- and memory-free, making it particularly well-suited for ultra-scalable, real-world lifelong model editing. ULTRAEDIT performs editing through a self-contained process that relies solely on lightweight linear algebra operations to compute parameter shifts, enabling fast and consistent parameter modifications with minimal overhead. To improve scalability in lifelong settings, ULTRAEDIT employs a lifelong normalization strategy that continuously updates feature statistics across turns, allowing it to adapt to distributional shifts and maintain consistency over time. ULTRAEDIT achieves editing speeds over 7x faster than the previous state-of-the-art method-which was also the fastest known approach-while consuming less than 1/3 the VRAM, making it the only method currently capable of editing a 7B LLM on a 24GB consumer-grade GPU. Furthermore, we construct ULTRAEDITBENCH-the largest dataset in the field to date, with over 2M editing pairs-and demonstrate that our method supports up to 1M edits while maintaining high accuracy. Comprehensive experiments on four datasets and six models show that ULTRAEDIT consistently achieves superior performance across diverse model editing scenarios. Our code is available at: https://github.com/XiaojieGu/UltraEdit.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ULTRAEDIT paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ULTRAEDIT, a novel training-free, subject-free, and memory-free approach to lifelong model editing for large language models (LLMs).  ULTRAEDIT performs edits via lightweight linear algebra operations, leveraging a "lifelong normalization" strategy that continuously updates feature statistics across editing turns.  This enables fast, consistent updates, mitigates edit collapse, and avoids the need for retraining or external memory.  The authors also present ULTRAEDITBENCH, a large new dataset for model editing. Experiments across several datasets and models demonstrate ULTRAEDIT's superior performance and efficiency, notably its ability to scale to 1 million edits while maintaining high accuracy and stability, even on consumer-grade GPUs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the combination of several key features:
    *   *Training-free editing:*  While some training-free methods exist, the ability to maintain performance over a large number of edits without any training is significant.
    *   *Lifelong normalization:* This is a key contribution. Adapting feature statistics on-the-fly to maintain stable signal distributions during editing is a clever way to avoid catastrophic forgetting and edit collapse.
    *   *Memory-free architecture:*  Avoiding external memory is beneficial for scalability and efficiency.
    *   *Extreme efficiency:*  The focus on lightweight linear algebra operations enables rapid editing speeds and reduced memory footprint. The authors clearly demonstrated they reach 7x speedup and VRAM reduction.

The *combination* of these features distinguishes ULTRAEDIT from existing approaches. Existing methods are mostly based on one to two improvements, which are not able to ensure both model performance and efficiency.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial problem in LLM development: how to continuously adapt models to evolving information in a scalable and reliable manner.
    *   *Practical scalability:*  The ability to edit a 7B model on a 24GB GPU and scale to 1 million edits opens up new possibilities for real-world deployment. The focus on efficiency is a significant step towards making model editing a practical tool.
    *   *Dataset contribution:* ULTRAEDITBENCH is a valuable resource, providing a large and diverse dataset for evaluating model editing methods.
    *   *Strong empirical results:* The experiments are comprehensive, covering multiple datasets and models. The consistent gains over existing methods are compelling.
    *   *Clear ablation studies:* The ablation experiments provide good insight into the contribution of each component of the architecture.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    * The description of the lifelong normalization scheme could be more detailed. While the explanation provided in the paper and algorithm are adequate, it would benefit from a more thorough breakdown of why this method is so important for mitigating Edit Collapse, as well as more discussion regarding its limitations (i.e. under what conditions would this normalization scheme fail to sufficiently mitigate distribution shift?).
    * The experimental results provided in Table 8 for the models Fine-Tuned, MEMIT, MALMEN, RECT, and PRUNE when evaluating their abilities to edit FEVER and WikiBigEdit with models Mistral-7B-v0.3 and LLAMA-3-8B-Instruct are often the same for Efficacy, Generalization, and Specificity, where values equal 0.00 for both the initial and ultra-edited results. This could be due to either the fact that they did not have enough computational resources to finish the ultra-editing analysis, or due to certain technical limitations with the FEVER and WikiBigEdit datasets in of themselves.

*   **Potential Influence:**  ULTRAEDIT has the potential to significantly influence the field by:
    *   Establishing a new benchmark for efficient and scalable model editing.
    *   Inspiring new research on training-free and memory-free editing techniques.
    *   Making model editing a more practical and accessible tool for LLM deployment.

**Justification of Score:**

ULTRAEDIT presents a significant advance in model editing, primarily due to its combination of efficiency, scalability, and simplicity.  The lifelong normalization strategy is a key innovation that enables robust performance over a large number of edits. The creation of ULTRAEDITBENCH is a valuable contribution, and the comprehensive experiments provide strong evidence for the method's effectiveness. Potential weaknesses could be mitigated in future work.
The paper is well-written and clearly articulates its contributions.  The potential for practical impact is high.

Score: 8.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### UniGen: Enhanced Training & Test-Time Strategies for Unified Multimodal Understanding and Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14682v1)
- **Authors**: Rui Tian, Mingfei Gao, Mingze Xu, Jiaming Hu, Jiasen Lu, Zuxuan Wu, Yinfei Yang, Afshin Dehghan
- **Abstract**: We introduce UniGen, a unified multimodal large language model (MLLM) capable of image understanding and generation. We study the full training pipeline of UniGen from a data-centric perspective, including multi-stage pre-training, supervised fine-tuning, and direct preference optimization. More importantly, we propose a new Chain-of-Thought Verification (CoT-V) strategy for test-time scaling, which significantly boosts UniGen's image generation quality using a simple Best-of-N test-time strategy. Specifically, CoT-V enables UniGen to act as both image generator and verifier at test time, assessing the semantic alignment between a text prompt and its generated image in a step-by-step CoT manner. Trained entirely on open-source datasets across all stages, UniGen achieves state-of-the-art performance on a range of image understanding and generation benchmarks, with a final score of 0.78 on GenEval and 85.19 on DPG-Bench. Through extensive ablation studies, our work provides actionable insights and addresses key challenges in the full life cycle of building unified MLLMs, contributing meaningful directions to the future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UniGen, a unified multimodal large language model (MLLM) designed for both image understanding and generation. A key contribution is the Chain-of-Thought Verification (CoT-V) strategy, which enhances the model's generation quality by enabling it to act as its own verifier during test time, assessing semantic alignment between text prompts and generated images. The model is trained entirely on open-source datasets using a data-centric approach, involving multi-stage pre-training, supervised fine-tuning, and direct preference optimization. UniGen achieves state-of-the-art performance on several benchmarks for both understanding and generation compared to similar models. The paper also includes comprehensive ablation studies providing insights into the building of unified MLLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The CoT-V strategy for test-time scaling is a significant novelty. Self-verification, while explored in other contexts, is cleverly adapted here to enhance image generation. The emphasis on a completely open-source training pipeline is also a valuable contribution in a field often reliant on proprietary datasets. The data-centric perspective in training is valuable, and the insights gleaned from the ablation studies contribute to better understanding MLLM training strategies. The paper's focus on decoupling encoders also offers a unique design element.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field by providing a practical and effective method for improving the quality of image generation in unified MLLMs, especially with its focus on open-source training. The performance improvements achieved by UniGen on benchmarks indicate a tangible advance. Furthermore, the detailed ablation studies offer useful guidelines and address key challenges in building unified MLLMs. The demonstration of competitive results with only open-source data is especially important for democratizing MLLM development.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The CoT-V strategy is well-motivated and demonstrably effective.
    *   Comprehensive ablation studies provide valuable insights.
    *   The model is trained on completely open-source data.
    *   The paper is clearly written and well-organized.
    *   Demonstrates strong performance on a variety of benchmarks.
    * The focus on decoupling encoders is another unique approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    * The model used is a relatively small model compared to state-of-the-art image generators (1.5B parameters). It would be important to test how this method scales to larger models.
    *   The performance of the CoT strategy, especially the pseudo-labeling for data generation, relies on the capability of Qwen2.5-7B. Future work should explore stronger labelers or human intervention for high-quality labels.
    *   The paper acknowledges limitations regarding complex prompts and the potential for hallucination, which are important areas for future research.
    *   The limitations are significant that larger models have not been fully explored, it is difficult to assess the scalable potential of this approach.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The CoT-V strategy could be widely adopted by researchers working on unified MLLMs.
    *   The open-source training approach could encourage more research in this area.
    *   The detailed ablation studies could inform the development of future MLLMs.

**Justification for the score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution with the CoT-V strategy and open-source implementation. The comprehensive experiments and analysis significantly strengthen the paper. However, it is important to temper enthusiasm, due to its smaller scale and reliance on strong external verifiers.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mind the Gap: Bridging Thought Leap for Improved Chain-of-Thought Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14684v1)
- **Authors**: Haolei Xu, Yuchen Yan, Yongliang Shen, Wenqi Zhang, Guiyang Hou, Shengpei Jiang, Kaitao Song, Weiming Lu, Jun Xiao, Yueting Zhuang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable progress on mathemati-cal tasks through Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. However, existing mathematical CoT datasets often suffer from Thought Leaps due to experts omitting intermediate steps, which negatively impacts model learning and generalization. We propose the CoT Thought Leap Bridge Task, which aims to automatically detect leaps and generate missing intermediate reasoning steps to restore the completeness and coherence of CoT. To facilitate this, we constructed a specialized training dataset called ScaleQM+, based on the structured ScaleQuestMath dataset, and trained CoT-Bridge to bridge thought leaps. Through comprehensive experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, we demonstrate that models fine-tuned on bridged datasets consistently outperform those trained on original datasets, with improvements of up to +5.87% on NuminaMath. Our approach effectively enhances distilled data (+3.02%) and provides better starting points for reinforcement learning (+3.1%), functioning as a plug-and-play module compatible with existing optimization techniques. Furthermore, CoT-Bridge demonstrate improved generalization to out-of-domain logical reasoning tasks, confirming that enhancing reasoning completeness yields broadly applicable benefits.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Mind the Gap: Bridging Thought Leap for Improved Chain-of-Thought Tuning."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of "Thought Leaps" in Chain-of-Thought (CoT) datasets used for training Large Language Models (LLMs) on mathematical reasoning tasks. Thought Leaps occur when intermediate reasoning steps are omitted by human experts, hindering the learning process and generalization capabilities of LLMs. The authors propose the "CoT Thought Leap Bridge Task," which aims to automatically detect these leaps and generate the missing intermediate steps. To facilitate this, they create a specialized training dataset called ScaleQM+ and train a model named CoT-Bridge.  Through experiments on various mathematical reasoning benchmarks, they demonstrate that models fine-tuned on datasets "bridged" using CoT-Bridge consistently outperform those trained on original datasets. Furthermore, the authors show that their approach can improve distilled data and provide better starting points for reinforcement learning, functioning as a plug-and-play module. They also demonstrate improved generalization to out-of-domain logical reasoning tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of identifying and addressing Thought Leaps in CoT reasoning is novel. While previous works have focused on factual correctness or answer accuracy, this paper specifically targets the completeness and coherence of the reasoning structure. Formalizing this concept and developing a task around it is a significant contribution.  The development of ScaleQM+ and the CoT-Bridge model are also novel contributions. However, one might argue that the techniques used (instruction tuning) are relatively standard, and the primary innovation lies in the problem formulation and data generation.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant because they highlight a previously understudied problem that affects the performance and generalization of LLMs on reasoning tasks. Demonstrating that filling in these "Thought Leaps" consistently improves performance across different models, datasets, and even out-of-domain tasks underscores the importance of reasoning chain completeness. The "plug-and-play" aspect of the CoT-Bridge, which can enhance existing optimization techniques like distillation and reinforcement learning, makes the approach practical and widely applicable. The gains shown across multiple benchmarks, including some considered quite challenging, add to the impact. The claim of improved OOD generalization is particularly impactful.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem formulation and task definition.
    *   Well-designed experiments with comprehensive results on multiple benchmarks.
    *   Demonstrated improvements across different model architectures.
    *   Evidence of plug-and-play compatibility with existing optimization techniques.
    *   Demonstration of OOD generalization.
    *   The ablation studies (e.g., comparing CoT-Bridge with CoT-Bridge-Random) effectively isolate the importance of accurate leap identification.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The training and evaluation are primarily limited to mathematical reasoning. Although there is OOD testing, the approach has limited diversity.
    *   The approach relies on the quality of the CoT-Bridge model, which may introduce errors and noise, even if the impact is analyzed to be low.
    *   The reliance on ScaleQuestMath also limits the evaluation to one dataset structure, rather than varying different CoT formats.
    *   The analysis of different bridging positions is interesting, but perhaps not explored deeply enough (e.g., are there specific patterns related to problem type and bridging position?).
    *   A true test of the generated reasoning steps would be human evaluation, which would confirm the validity of the leaps identified and bridges applied.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on CoT reasoning by shifting the focus towards reasoning chain completeness and coherence. It provides a valuable tool (CoT-Bridge) for enhancing existing datasets and models.
